The closing audit covers **290 containers: 270 valid and 20 expected-invalid**, with 42 informational warnings. Ten intentional-invalid identities declare an exact, named error-code set plus a reason; `bad-*` is not a wildcard, and mutation tests prove an undeclared malformed fixture, a missing expected code, or an additional code fails. Five compatibility/repair fixtures predate that convention, so their equally exact exceptions are documented rather than hidden by renaming. CI downloads the pinned tool transiently; no binary, runtime dependency, or network requirement for ordinary CNA builds was added. |
| GLTF-016 | Pin the Khronos reference renderer for L7 comparison | ✅ | GLTF-009 | Development/reference only — **never** a CNA runtime dependency. **Accept:** documented capture procedure and version pin. **Pinned as a disposable developer tool:** `KhronosGroup/glTF-Sample-Renderer@863b981fb755359063e370ff7b6e956bda0716e2`, package version 1.1.0, Apache-2.0, with runtime and CI dependency flags both false. `docs/gltf-conformance.md` §2.10 specifies detached checkout + `npm ci` + local build; a 512×512/device-scale-1 WebGL2 canvas; exact scene/camera recording; time-zero animation; fixed no-IBL two-light state, clear colour, tone map, MSAA/framebuffer settings; two `renderFrame` calls and `gl.finish()`; and the provenance sidecar fields needed to reproduce a PNG. It also explicitly quarantines environment-dependent scenes until the HDR/environment itself has a reviewed pin. This row provides the pinned procedure; `GLTF-411` still owns actual captures and comparisons for at least ten assets. CNA neither links nor fetches the renderer. |
| GLTF-017 | Prove the harness is renderer-independent at L1–L5 | ✔ | GLTF-010 | Run L1–L5 on `STUB` and `HEADLESS`; results must be identical. **Accept:** byte-identical L2–L5 output. **Done, and now extended across the four-renderer gate:** CNA selects its renderer at *compile* time, so one test binary can never notice a renderer leaking into a layer that should not have one. `scripts/gltf-renderer-parity.sh` takes two build directories and compares the two runs — **42 current L1–L5 tests are identical on STUB, HEADLESS, OPENGLES3 and Vulkan**, timings elided so a millisecond cannot fail it. The byte-identity the row asks for is stronger than the diff suggests: each run compares its own output against the **same committed L5 goldens**, so two agreeing runs are not agreeing with each other (which could be a shared mistake) but each with a third, committed artifact. The script also compares the full `*Gltf*` selection and tolerates exactly one class of difference — `SKIPPED` versus `OK`, which is a renderer *capability* gate (`GraphicsCapability::ThreeD`) rather than a divergence — and fails on anything else, naming §6's rule that a shared-importer defect must not be fixed inside a renderer directory. `GltfConformanceLadder.RendererParityIncludesSuitesWhoseNameContainsGltf` pins the leading star on both sides of the script after the old prefix filter was found still omitting `RuntimeGltfModelTest`. |
| GLTF-018 | Third-party asset licence review procedure | ✔ | GLTF-013 | Per-asset licence + attribution in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` before any commit. **Accept:** written procedure; **no asset committed without it.** **Written into `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` as five steps that must all appear in the commit that adds an asset**, and the second is the one that actually catches things: record the licence from the **asset's own** metadata (`asset.copyright`, the per-model `LICENSE.md`), never the repository's top-level one — `glTF-Sample-Assets` is a mixture of CC0, CC-BY 4.0, CC-BY-SA and model-specific terms, and the same file name has carried different licences across revisions. The others: pin source **and revision**, add the attribution row, check redistribution *in this form* is permitted (some models are licensed for use with the sample viewer, not as standalone assets), and prefer the smallest asset that proves the point. The second half of the acceptance is currently trivial and stated as such: **no third-party asset is committed at all.** |
| GLTF-019 | Decide: commit a curated Khronos subset vs fetch-on-demand | ✔ | GLTF-018 | Weigh repository size against CI reproducibility. **Accept:** decision recorded with rationale. **Decided: the generated corpus is committed (currently 145 assets and 728 manifest-tracked files, 1.89 MiB, deterministic, no network), while Khronos models and the reference renderer are fetched on demand and are never CNA runtime dependencies.** The row asks for size against reproducibility and the table weighs both — ~1.5 GB upstream, tens of MB for any real subset, paid by every clone forever including clones that never touch glTF — **but the deciding argument is neither.** A third-party asset is *an oracle we do not control*: its expected values cannot be derived from the specification the way a generated fixture's can. Real models are therefore supplementary checks run deliberately (`GLTF-405`, `GLTF-411`), not part of the gate. Even `GLTF ROBUST`'s ≥ 50 MB performance asset should be fetched rather than committed: it is a benchmark input, not evidence. |
| GLTF-020 | One reproducible `regenerate-gltf-goldens` script | ✔ | GLTF-007, GLTF-009 | **Accept:** running it on an unchanged tree produces a zero diff. **`scripts/regenerate-gltf-goldens.sh`**, with no arguments to get wrong. It does three things, and the second is the one that matters: it regenerates, then runs the generator's own `--check` — because a generator that wrote nothing at all would otherwise "succeed" — and then, in a git worktree, **reports whether the tree changed**, which is the acceptance stated as an observation rather than assumed. `--check` verifies without writing and is what CI now runs, replacing the hand-written `python3 -m tools.gltf_fixtures --check` line in `gltf-sanitizers-ci.yml` so the command exists in one place. Verified in both directions: a clean tree reports "working tree unchanged", and appending a single newline to `xf-identity.gltf` makes `--check` exit 1 naming the file and both byte counts. A corpus change is *reported*, not refused — changing a fixture is a legitimate reason to run this — but it must be visible, which is `GLTF-410`'s concern. |

---

### Phase 1 — Container, buffers, URIs, structural validation · owner: `GLTF-CONTAINER` / `GLTF-BUFFER`
*Entry: Phase 0. Exit: every malformed-input fixture yields a deterministic, actionable error.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-021 | Call `cgltf_validate()` on both load paths | ✔ | GLTF-010 | **0 occurrences** in production code today. **Accept:** validation runs and its failure is surfaced as `ContentLoadException` / a CLI error naming the violated constraint. **Landed:** `ValidateGltfEXT` runs on both load paths, immediately after `cgltf_load_buffers` — deliberately after, because the sparse index-bound check needs buffer data to run at all. A failure surfaces as `ContentLoadException` on the runtime path and a named CLI error on the offline one, saying which class of constraint was violated rather than only a numeric code. `bad-accessor-out-of-bounds` proves it fires: a POSITION accessor reaching 12 bytes past its own `bufferView`. |
| GLTF-022 | Decide the policy for validation failures (reject vs warn) | ✔ | GLTF-021 | Some real assets fail strict validation. **Accept:** a documented severity table; hard rejection only for constraints that make decoding unsafe. **Decided, from what cgltf actually checks rather than from taste.** Its entire check set is decoding-safety constraints: an accessor past its `bufferView`, a `bufferView` past its buffer, a sparse index outside the accessor's range, attribute counts disagreeing within a primitive, an undefined component or primitive type. There is no cosmetic violation in that set to downgrade, so the severity table has one row: **`cgltf_validate` failure rejects**. The genuinely advisory case is a different check — an unimplemented `extensionsUsed` entry — and that warns (`GLTF-024`), which is what makes the distinction real rather than nominal. |
| GLTF-023 | Enforce `extensionsRequired` | ✔ | GLTF-021 | Never checked; an unsupported *required* extension imports silently and wrongly. **Accept:** `gltf-required-extension-unsupported` fixture errors with the extension name. **Landed:** an `extensionsRequired` entry CNA does not implement rejects the import with the extension named. `IsGltfExtensionSupportedEXT` draws the line at **implemented**, not **noticed**: `KHR_materials_unlit` and `KHR_materials_pbrSpecularGlossiness` are both read by `ExtractMesh` to keep such a material off the metallic-roughness path (`GLTF-215`), yet an unlit surface still goes through a lit effect and the specular-glossiness parameters are dropped — so a file *requiring* either is rejected. `KHR_draco_mesh_compression` is supported only in a build with libdraco, so the answer is build-dependent rather than a fixed list. Fixture: `gltf-required-extension-unsupported`. |
| GLTF-024 | Report `extensionsUsed` entries CNA ignores | ✔ | GLTF-023 | **Accept:** the import report lists every ignored extension. **Landed:** every unimplemented `extensionsUsed` entry produces a warning naming it, on both paths — the runtime one through `CNA::Logger::Warn`, the offline one in the tool's own warning list. The import continues, because `extensionsUsed` is advisory by definition; but it is never silent, since "loaded fine" and "loaded as authored" are different claims. |
| GLTF-025 | Public/CNAEXT API-change review checkpoint | ✔ | GLTF-024 | Gate for every §25 row. **Accept:** each proposed member has problem, shape, compatibility, migration and test recorded before implementation. **Landed:** `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md`. Each member is recorded by exact name, type, default and semantics rather than by intent, because that is what a reviewer can disagree with. Nothing in §25 was rejected outright; the gate's effect was on **shape**: the topology property reuses the existing `PrimitiveType` rather than a glTF-flavoured one; `AlphaModeEXT` is declared **once** in `Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics` and `CNAEXT.md` §5.5's separately-sketched `PbrMaterial::AlphaMode` is bound to that same type rather than becoming a second enum with a conversion function; and `doubleSided` is admitted as **carried state only**, with applying it to the rasterizer explicitly assigned to `GLTF-230` — the difference between a flag that works and a flag that merely exists. `GLTF-224`/`GLTF-225` and the explicitly reopened `GLTF-034` have since crossed their reviewed gates and landed with their consumers and focused evidence; `GLTF-207`/`GLTF-320` retain their recorded dispositions; `GLTF-261` stays `investigate only` as the one breaking row. |
| GLTF-026 | GLB container fixtures: chunk order, padding, alignment | ✅ | GLTF-003 | **Accept:** `GltfContainerRobustness.EveryCommittedGlbFollowsTheChunkLayoutSection44Requires` walks every committed `.glb` chunk by chunk — JSON first and mandatory, BIN second and only BIN, every chunk length a multiple of 4, the header's total length equal to the file size, no bytes after the last chunk, and the JSON chunk padded with **spaces** rather than zeros (that padding is inside the text a JSON parser reads). Over the whole corpus rather than one file, because these are properties of the emitter and one asset could satisfy them by accident of its size. |
| GLTF-027 | Malformed GLB: bad magic, bad chunk length, truncated | ✅ | GLTF-026 | **Accept:** `GltfContainerRobustness` mutates a committed, valid `.glb` one field at a time — bad magic, container version 3, a total length past the end of the file, a JSON chunk length past the end of the file, and a file truncated after its header — and each is refused. A control asserts the *unmutated* file still loads, without which "refused" would prove only that the reader refuses everything. Each of these is a real accident: a truncated download, a chunk length written before the payload was finished. |
| GLTF-028 | External `.bin` buffer resolution | ✅ | GLTF-026 | **Accept:** relative URI resolves against the `.gltf` directory; a missing file errors clearly. **Locked by both a committed positive fixture and scratch negative controls.** `gltf-external-bin` carries a generated 79-byte `.bin` sidecar hashed in the corpus manifest, passes every L1–L5 sweep, and loads through the ordinary `ContentManager` path. The scratch asset remains because its buffer lives in a **subdirectory** — the document directory, process working directory and `ContentManager` root otherwise coincide — and because missing-file and traversal cases cannot be valid corpus assets. The missing-file case asserts more than "it throws": a silent empty buffer would preserve the vertex count while turning every position into zero. The traversal case proves the ordinary path reaches `ResolveExternalUriEXT`, not merely that the resolver works in isolation. |
| GLTF-029 | Base64 `data:` URI buffers | ✔ | GLTF-026 | All 14 audit fixtures use them, and `gltf-data-uri-bin` is the named source-form witness. **Accept:** locked by test, including odd padding lengths. **Locked at all three base64 padding residues** in the decoder tests; the named fixture's 79-byte payload additionally carries a trailing `==` and is byte-compared against `gltf-external-bin`. The one-pad and two-pad cases are where a decoder loses or invents a trailing byte, usually surfacing later as a misleading accessor validation error. |
| GLTF-030 | Percent-encoded URI handling | ✅ | GLTF-028 | `cgltf_decode_uri` is used for images; verify for buffers too. **Accept:** a percent-encoded buffer URI loads. **Verified for buffers, and in both directions.** The committed `gltf-uri-percent-encoded` names `%20` in JSON but emits only `gltf-uri-percent-encoded geometry.bin`; the suite asserts the encoded spelling does not exist, loads the decoded file through both cgltf and `ContentManager`, and byte-compares it with the inline and ordinary external fixtures. Scratch controls reverse the filenames and require failure, catching a loader that uses URI text verbatim. |
| GLTF-031 | `buffer.byteLength` enforcement | ✅ | GLTF-028 | **Accept:** `ValidateGltfEXT` — which is what both loaders run — refuses a `bufferView` longer than its own buffer and one starting past the end of it. Asserted through validation rather than through the parser, because cgltf's parse step resolves indices to pointers without bound-checking the views, so "the file parsed" is not the question. The far-offset case has its own test: an implementation checking `byteOffset + byteLength <= byteLength` in 32-bit arithmetic can be talked past it by an offset near the type's maximum. |
| GLTF-032 | Path containment for external URIs | ✔ | GLTF-028 | `gltfDir / uri` with no containment check — `../../..` escapes the asset directory. Security-relevant. **Accept:** a traversal URI is rejected with a clear message. **Landed as `ResolveExternalUriEXT` + `ValidateExternalUriContainmentEXT`.** The sweep runs between `cgltf_parse_file` and `cgltf_load_buffers` on **both** entry points, because that call resolves external buffer URIs itself and offers no hook to veto one — there is nowhere else to stand. Four distinct refusals with four distinct messages (scheme, absolute/drive-absolute, lexical escape, symlink escape); percent-decoding runs *before* containment so `%2e%2e%2f` is seen for what it is; containment is compared component-wise so `/asset-evil` does not read as inside `/asset`. |
| GLTF-033 | Reject non-`2.0` `asset.version` on both paths | ✔ | GLTF-021 | Both paths already check. **Accept:** `bad-version-1.0` errors; locked by test. **Locked — and the check turns out to be load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces.** cgltf gates on the major version being **at least 2**, so `1.0` and `0.8` are refused at parse time but **`3.0` and `2.1` parse successfully**. A reader trusting the parser alone would decode a future format's object model as though it were this one — the exact failure the 1.0 refusal prevents, arriving from the other direction. Both loaders' own `asset.version != "2.0"` comparison is what actually stops it, and the test asserts the property that comparison depends on: cgltf preserves the string verbatim. |
| GLTF-034 | `GltfImportReportEXT` — structured import diagnostics | ✅ | GLTF-025 | Today losses are silent or printed to stdout by the CLI only. **Accept:** warnings, dropped-feature counts and approximations are reachable programmatically on **both** load paths. **Landed after the owner explicitly reopened the reviewed deferral and named `GLTF-431` as the first consumer.** `Model::getGltfImportReportEXTProperty()` returns summary counts and stable coded `GltfImportDiagnosticEXT` entries. Direct `.gltf`/`.glb` import fills it in memory; `gltf_to_cnj` serializes an optional `gltfImportReport` object and the Model `.cnj` reader restores it. Old `.cnj` and non-glTF models expose the empty default. Severity is independent from kind, so suspicious accessor bounds are warnings without pretending an approximation occurred; exact conversions and generated data remain informational. Absolute converter paths are excluded from serialized messages. The precise shape, compatibility and migration decision is `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §2.3; `GltfImportReport` and the real-converter `GltfToCnjToolTest.StructuredImportDiagnosticsSurviveBothLoadPaths` are the evidence. |
| GLTF-035 | Route every existing silent drop through the report | ✅ | GLTF-034 | Non-joint animation channels, >3 lights, ignored UV sets, ignored extensions, unsupported modes. **Accept:** each has a report entry with a fixture. **Landed through one shared set of report appenders used by both loaders.** Stable codes cover these named cases (`animation-paths-unsupported`, `animation-targets-unavailable`, `lights-dropped`, `uv-set-mismatch`, `ignored-extension`, topology conversion/refusal) and the rest of the internal inventory: layout drops, texture-source/mipmap/transform limits, extra influence/color/custom streams, generated normals, weight repair, transmission/specular-glossiness approximations, morph/light/animation outcomes and GPU instancing. `EveryDropNamedByGltf035HasAStructuredEntry` asserts the cross-subsystem carrier and summary semantics; three real fixtures prove the direct and offline reports agree. Exact topology conversion is deliberately `Information`, not a false loss. |
| GLTF-036 | Bring the glTF path under ASan+UBSan in CI | ✔ | GLTF-010 | `REMED-NA-016`: UBSan-confirmed misaligned `float` load in `cgltf_component_read_float`, live under `ResolvesSparseAccessorOverride`. **Accept:** the whole corpus runs clean, or every finding is triaged with a recorded disposition. **Both: the finding was root-caused and the selection now runs clean.** REMED-NA-016 is neither a cgltf bug nor a CNA decode bug — it is a **malformed fixture CNA accepted**. §3.6.2.4 makes an accessor's effective offset a multiple of its component size; `cgltf_validate` does not check it; cgltf reads a component with a raw `*(const float*)` cast. `kSparseAccessorGltf` put a sparse `VEC3<float>` values bufferView at byte offset **62**, two off a float boundary, so the parser performed a misaligned load. Fixed at both ends: the fixture moved to 64, and `ValidateGltfEXT` now **refuses** any file violating §3.6.2.4 (base accessor, sparse indices, sparse values) or §3.6.2.1's multiple-of-4 `byteStride` — rejection rather than a warning, because CNA cannot make the vendored parser safe on such a file and the alternative (replacing cgltf's element reader) is what `GLTF-041` exists to prevent. `.github/workflows/gltf-sanitizers-ci.yml` keeps it that way: STUB renderer, no display, `--gtest_filter='Gltf*'` plus the conformance label, `detect_leaks=1` and UBSan `exitcode=1`, no known-failure allowlist. Current state: **190 glTF tests, 0 ASan errors, 0 UBSan reports, 0 leaks.** **Out-of-scope findings, recorded rather than silently left behind:** a full unfiltered run of the same binary reports **58 UBSan findings and 7 leak reports, none of them on the glTF path** — signed-integer overflow in `Vector3`/`Vector4`/`Matrix`/`Quaternion` `GetHashCode()` (raw float bit patterns summed as `int`), member access on misaligned/invalid `this` in `gamer-services` and `net`, and a leaked `NetworkSession` in the gamer-services dispatcher harness. They belong to those subsystems' own plans, not this one, and are why the CI job is glTF-scoped: a full-suite sanitizer job could not be green today and would need an allowlist, which this job deliberately does not have. |
| GLTF-037 | Replace CNA-side `reinterpret_cast` on index bytes with `memcpy` | ✔ | GLTF-036 | `ContentManager.cpp` casts `indexBytes.data()` to `uint32_t*`. **Accept:** no CNA-authored misaligned load remains; behaviour unchanged at L5. **Landed.** Three sites cast `std::vector<std::uint8_t>::data()` to `const std::uint32_t*`/`const std::uint16_t*` and dereferenced it — a misaligned load, undefined by the standard and a fault on targets without unaligned access, which is precisely the class `GLTF-036`'s sanitizer job exists to catch. In practice the allocator returned a suitably aligned block and the code "worked", which is what let it stand. All three now go through one `IndicesFromBytes<T>` helper that `memcpy`s into a properly typed vector: no alignment requirement, the same loads on targets where they are legal, and it runs once per mesh part at load time rather than per frame — so there is nothing to weigh against removing undefined behaviour. Behaviour unchanged: the full suite and the sanitizer run are both unmoved (**273 glTF tests, 0 ASan errors, 0 UBSan reports, 0 leaks**). |
| GLTF-038 | Decide the vendored-cgltf divergence policy | ✔ | GLTF-036 | Patching `third_party/cgltf/cgltf.h` diverges from upstream. **Accept:** a written rule (report upstream; patch only on an observed fault), consistent with `REMED-NA-016`'s disposition. **Decided, and the rule is stricter than the row proposed: never patch, at all.** `docs/gltf-conventions.md` carries it with the reasoning — a patched vendored header is invisible at exactly the moment it matters, because the next upgrade is a file replacement and a local edit is either silently lost (the fault returns, now with a test that no longer explains itself) or silently kept (the upgrade is not the upgrade anyone thought they made). Neither failure announces itself; a workaround in CNA's own code is in CNA's own diff, tests and blame. The cost is stated rather than glossed: the workarounds run per accessor, and one re-walks data cgltf already walked. **Two tests, because a policy nobody can check is a preference.** `TheVendoredHeaderCarriesNoCnaEdits` fails on any `CNA`/`CNAEXT`/`plan_gltf`/`GLTF-nnn` marker in the header (and first checks the file is still cgltf, so it cannot pass over nothing), and requires the MIT `LICENSE` beside it. `EveryKnownCgltfFaultStillHasItsCnaSideAnswer` is the converse: each of the three proven faults — sparse stride (`GLTF-062`), the signed-normalized clamp (`GLTF-056`) and the wrapping span computation (`GLTF-039`) — must still have its named answer **as a call or definition** in `GltfImportCore.cpp`, so a workaround deleted as "redundant" fails here instead of at a renderer's expense. `REMED-NA-016` is cited as the precedent it is: the misaligned load UBSan found inside cgltf was not patched there either — it was a malformed fixture CNA accepted, and the answer was to refuse the file. |
| GLTF-039 | Overflow-check every offset/count computation | ✔ | GLTF-021 | `count * stride`, `offset + length` in `size_t`. **Accept:** a fixture with counts near `SIZE_MAX/stride` errors instead of wrapping. **Fixed, and cgltf cannot see it.** `cgltf_validate` computes `offset + stride * (count - 1) + elementSize` in wrapping unsigned arithmetic, so `"count": 4611686018427387905` on a tightly packed `VEC3<float>` wraps `12 × 2^62` to exactly **0**: the declared span collapses to 12 bytes, fits the bufferView, and the file is admitted with the count it actually asked for still in place. `ValidateGltfEXT` now recomputes every accessor's span — base plus both sparse arrays — through `RequiredSpan`, the guarded arithmetic the index path already used (§8.1), before anything reads a byte; and `UnpackAccessor` repeats the check at the point of allocation, because `ExtractMesh` is reachable without validation (the conformance harness and the offline converter both call it directly) and the failure there was `std::length_error: cannot create std::vector larger than max_size()` from inside the allocator, naming neither the file nor the accessor. `bad-accessor-count-overflow` is the fixture, and it discriminates in the way the row asks: an honest 2^62 fails the ordinary bounds check on the first comparison, so only the wrap proves the guard. The bufferView-level wrap (`byteOffset + byteLength`) is guarded too, cannot be reached from any file — cgltf parses integers through `atoll`, so two saturated values still sum below 2^64 — and is asserted directly by `ValidationRejectsABufferViewWhoseRangeWraps` rather than left as a check nothing exercises. Two corpus sweeps had to stop excluding malformed fixtures **by hardcoded id** to accommodate it (`GltfSamplerMapping`'s extraction sweep, `GltfOracleEXT`'s world-position sweep): both now read the manifest's own `rejection` block, which is why a third malformed fixture could be added at all without silently shrinking them. |
| GLTF-040 | Fuzz corpus seed for the container/buffer layer | ✔ | GLTF-036 | Seed from the `bad-*` group. **Accept:** a short fuzz run produces no crash or sanitiser finding. **Landed, and it found two escapes on its first run.** `MutatedContainersAreRefusedByNameRatherThanCrashing` mutates 3 000 seeded mutants (`std::mt19937(20260812)`) through five structured mutations — a bit flip, a truncation, a length/offset field set to a boundary value, an insertion that shifts everything after it out of alignment, and a JSON-significant byte — and runs each through the real path: `cgltf_parse` → `cgltf_load_buffers` → `ValidateGltfEXT` → `BuildSceneGraph` → `ExtractMesh`. The seeds are the whole `bad-*` group as the row asks, **plus three valid assets weighted double**, because a mutation of a file that is already refused is usually still refused and never reaches the code past validation: with the malformed group alone almost nothing got deep. The asserted property is deliberately not "every mutant is rejected" — a bit flipped in a vertex mantissa is a valid file with different geometry — but the contract a caller can rely on: **succeed, or throw a `std::runtime_error` that names the problem.** The run records how far it got (`RecordProperty`) and asserts floors on all three depths, so a fuzz whose mutations stopped reaching the code cannot report success: **352 of 3 000 parsed, 175 validated, 166 extracted, 186 named refusals.** Two escapes were found and fixed, both `std::length_error` thrown from inside an allocator — which names neither the file nor the accessor and is not what a caller of an importer catches: `UnpackAccessor` allocating for an accessor with **no** `bufferView` (§3.6.2.1 reads it as zeros, so *nothing in the file bounds its count* — the span guard `GLTF-039` added has no view to check against), and `vertexBytes.reserve()` firing before any decode, where an optimisation was deciding how a malformed file failed. Both now refuse by name through `AllocateDecodedElements`/`ReserveOrRefuse`, which also stops the process *attempting* a multi-gigabyte allocation for a count in the billions. `AnAccessorWithNoBufferViewAndAnAbsurdCountIsRefusedByName` keeps the finding as its own regression test rather than leaving it dependent on a seed continuing to reach it. **Sanitiser half:** the suite is `Gltf*`, which is exactly the filter `GLTF-036`'s CI job runs under ASan and UBSan, so every mutant is re-run there with `detect_leaks=1` and UBSan `exitcode=1`. |

### Phase 2 — bufferView / accessor correctness · owner: `GLTF-ACCESSOR`
*Entry: Phase 1 — **waived for the Track A task `GLTF-041`** (§28.1). Exit: every §8.3 cell has a
fixture; every address computation is bounds-checked.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-041 | **[P0]** Lock the attribute decode path with L2 fixtures | ✔ | GLTF-005 | `UnpackAccessor` → `cgltf_accessor_unpack_floats` proven correct on `f5`/`f6`/`f10`/`f11`. **Accept:** L2 dumps match manifests; **no rewrite of this path.** **Done** (`8f39e225c`): `GltfAccessorDecodeLockTests.cpp`. Locks `f5`/`f6`/`f9`/`f10`/`f11` at L2 **and** requires the production `ExtractMesh` output to agree with the L2 dump component for component, so a replacement decoder fails even if it satisfied the L2 half alone. No rewrite of this path. |
| GLTF-042 | Bounds-check the effective accessor address | ✔ | GLTF-021 | §8.1 inequality is never enforced. **Accept:** `bad-accessor-out-of-bounds` errors; ASan clean. **Landed with `GLTF-021`:** `ValidateGltfEXT` runs on both load paths and `bad-accessor-out-of-bounds` — a POSITION accessor reaching 12 bytes past its own `bufferView` — is refused with a diagnostic naming the violated constraint, asserted by `GltfContainerValidation`. The **ASan half of the acceptance is not claimed here**: bringing the glTF path under ASan+UBSan is `GLTF-036`'s own task and it records a still-open UBSan finding in `cgltf_component_read_float`. |
| GLTF-043 | Enforce `accessor.byteOffset % componentSize == 0` | ✔ | GLTF-042 | §3.6.2.4. **Accept:** a misaligned fixture errors. **Landed with `GLTF-036`**, which is where the need became concrete: a hand-authored fixture in this repository put a sparse `VEC3<float>` values bufferView at byte offset **62**, and cgltf reads a component with a raw `*(const float*)` cast — so the misalignment was undefined behaviour, not a cosmetic violation. `ValidateGltfEXT` now checks the effective offset of the base accessor, the sparse indices array and the sparse values array. A control test asserts the same document at offset 64 passes, and a corpus sweep asserts every generated fixture already satisfies the rule. |
| GLTF-044 | Enforce `byteStride` range and multiple-of-4 | ✔ | GLTF-042 | **Accept:** an out-of-range stride errors. **Landed with `GLTF-036`/`GLTF-043`.** §3.6.2.1's multiple-of-4 rule is checked on any declared `byteStride`, with its own test at stride 14 — a value that keeps element 0 aligned and misaligns every element after it, which is the same undefined behaviour arriving one element later. |
| GLTF-045 | `bufferView.byteOffset` fixture | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `f5` uses offset 16. **Accept:** locked at L2. **Locked by `InterleavedByteStrideAndBothByteOffsetsDecodeExactly`**, which asserts the bufferView's own offset of 16 alongside the accessor's 12 — the three address terms of §8.1's equation are only discriminating together. |
| GLTF-046 | `accessor.byteOffset` fixture | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `f5` uses offset 12 for NORMAL. **Accept:** locked at L2. **Locked with `GLTF-045`.** |
| GLTF-047 | Interleaved `byteStride` fixtures | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `interleaved-position-normal`, `interleaved-pos-nrm-uv`, `interleaved-mixed-widths`, `stride-padded`. **Accept:** every effective attribute address proven at L2. **Locked by `interleaved-position-normal` at byteStride 24**, with `sparse-interleaved-base` (`GLTF-062`) adding the case where an interleaved stride meets a sparse accessor — the combination that exposed the vendored parser's own stride bug. `interleaved-mixed-widths` closes the remaining shape with a padding-free 16-byte record: VEC3<FLOAT> at offset 0 and normalized VEC4<UNSIGNED_BYTE> at 12. A dedicated production-path test proves both accessors advance at their own component width and the colour bytes round-trip exactly; L5 separately locks the packed stride-24 CNA buffer. |
| GLTF-048 | Two primitives sharing one bufferView | ✅ | GLTF-047 | **Accept:** `GltfContainerRobustness.TwoPrimitivesReadingOneBufferViewEachGetTheirOwnData` — two accessors 36 bytes apart in one 72-byte view, with no position value in common. Sharing a view between accessors is ordinary glTF (an exporter packs two meshes into one and distinguishes them by `byteOffset`); the failure worth testing is a decoder that caches by *view* rather than by accessor, which hands the second primitive the first one's vertices — a mesh that renders, in the wrong shape. |
| GLTF-049 | Component-count mismatch is an error, not a silent misread | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `UnpackAccessor` throws. **Accept:** locked by test with the exact message. **Locked with the message, not just the throw** — a `VEC2` `POSITION` is refused naming the attribute, the count it declares and the count expected, because "invalid accessor" tells an author nothing about which one to fix. Without the check, a vector sized for three components is filled from a stream of two and every vertex after the first reads from the wrong offset — `D4`'s class of silent misread, from the other direction. |
| GLTF-050 | `BYTE` (5120) fixtures, normalized and not | ✔ | GLTF-041 | **Accept:** `−128 → −1.0` exactly (the `max(v/127,−1)` rule). **Landed with `GLTF-056`**, and it found a defect rather than confirming one: cgltf omits §3.6.2.2's `max(c / 127, −1)` clamp entirely, so `−128` decoded to **−1.0079**. Clamped CNA-side and asserted at all three endpoints. |
| GLTF-051 | `SHORT` (5122) fixtures | ✔ | GLTF-041 | **Accept:** endpoint values exact. **Landed with `GLTF-056`** — the same missing clamp at 16 bits (`−32768` → **−1.00003**), with `0`, `+32767` and `−32767` asserted exact alongside it. |
| GLTF-052 | `UNSIGNED_BYTE` (5121) attribute fixtures | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `f10`. **Accept:** locked. **Locked in both its roles** — as a normalized attribute (`normalized-u8-color`, round-tripping byte-exactly) and as the narrowest legal index type (`u8-idx`). |
| GLTF-053 | `UNSIGNED_SHORT` (5123) attribute fixtures | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `u16-joints`, `normalized-u16-color`. **Accept:** exact. **Landed across `GLTF-056` and `GLTF-094`**: the normalized `u16` endpoints (`0`, mid, `65535` → exactly 1, not 65535/65536) and the `u16` weight form, which no corpus fixture authors. |
| GLTF-054 | `UNSIGNED_INT` (5125) accessor fixtures | ✅ | GLTF-041 | Indices only per spec. **Accept:** exact. `GltfIndexForm.UnsignedIntIndicesDecodeExactlyIncludingValuesAboveSixteenBits` decodes 70000 and 65536 out of a 70001-vertex mesh — a narrowing read would give 4464 and 0, both valid indices into the same mesh, so the model still draws with a fold in it. |
| GLTF-055 | `FLOAT` (5126) fixtures for every shape | ✔ | GLTF-041 | **Accept:** locked. **Locked across every shape the corpus uses**: SCALAR (indices, sampler times), VEC2 (UV), VEC3 (position, normal), VEC4 (colour, tangent, weights) and MAT4 (inverse bind matrices), each asserted component-for-component against its manifest. |
| GLTF-056 | Normalized-integer conversion endpoints | ✔ | GLTF-050, GLTF-053 | §8.3 formulas. **Accept:** `0`, mid, and max map exactly for all four integer types, signed and unsigned. **Writing the test found the SECOND confirmed defect in the vendored parser.** §3.6.2.2's signed conversions are `max(c / 127, −1)` and `max(c / 32767, −1)`; `cgltf_component_read_float` divides and returns with **no clamp for either type**, so `-128` decodes to **−1.0079** and `-32768` to **−1.00003** — outside the unit range the normalization exists to produce. The clamp is not decoration: a two's-complement integer's negative range is one wider than its positive one, which is exactly why the specification writes a `max` there. Clamped CNA-side in `UnpackAccessor` and restated independently in the oracle, both pointing at this row; only the three unsigned divisors were previously tested, and only for `UNSIGNED_BYTE`. The other error this row exists to catch — dividing by 256 or 65536 instead of 255 or 65535 — is under one percent and invisible anywhere but an endpoint. |
| GLTF-057 | Non-normalized integer attributes stay integral | ✔ | GLTF-052 | `JOINTS_0` must **not** be normalized. **Accept:** a `JOINTS_0` value of 200 decodes to 200.0, not 0.784. **Now locked by the exact value the acceptance names.** `skin-joint-index-padding` authors 200 in an `UNSIGNED_BYTE` zero-weight padding slot; `UnsignedByteAndUnsignedShortJointIndicesDecodeAndPackExactly` asserts the L2 value is exactly `200.0`, while the production L3 policy safely replaces this out-of-palette inactive slot with 0 before the byte pack. The distinction matters: the former `NonNormalizedJointIndicesStayIntegral` used twelve zero indices, on which both correct decoding and the wrong normalized `0/255` produce zero. `GLTF-056`'s endpoint tests are the counterpart: together they pin that the flag is honoured in both directions. |
| GLTF-058 | `MAT2`/`MAT3` column-padding fixture | ✔ | GLTF-041 | Unexposed today (only `MAT4` `FLOAT` is used) but must not regress. **Accept:** `mat3-padded` decodes per §3.6.2.4. **Landed with the id the row names.** A `MAT3` accessor of `UNSIGNED_BYTE`, stored the way §3.6.2.4 requires — each **column** starts on a 4-byte boundary, so three data bytes plus one pad byte per column, **12 bytes per element rather than 9** — and authored byte for byte rather than through a packing helper, because the padding *is* the subject and a helper emitting it would be asserting its own arithmetic. The nine values are distinct and non-zero for the usual reason: a reader that ignores the padding produces `1,2,3,0,4,5,6,0,7` from these bytes, so the wrong answer is a *different* answer rather than a plausible one. The accessor is deliberately **unreferenced** — nothing in CNA reads a `MAT2`/`MAT3` accessor, since `inverseBindMatrices` is `MAT4<FLOAT>` and needs no padding — and that is exactly why it needs a fixture: an unexercised rule regresses unnoticed, and the L2 sweep decodes *every* accessor a fixture declares, referenced or not. Result: the vendored cgltf handles all three padded cases (`MAT2<1>`, `MAT3<1>`, `MAT3<2>`) correctly, and that is now locked rather than assumed. |
| GLTF-059 | `MAT4` inverse-bind-matrix accessor fixture | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `f9` proved read correctness. **Accept:** locked at L2. **Locked by `Mat4InverseBindMatrixAccessorDecodesExactly`**, with `GLTF-246` adding the half that matters beyond the read: the translation lands in the fourth **row**, not the fourth column. |
| GLTF-060 | Cross-attribute `count` consistency check | ✔ | GLTF-042 | `posAcc->count` drives indexing of every other stream; a shorter `NORMAL` accessor reads out of range. **Accept:** `accessor-count-mismatch` errors with a clear message; ASan clean. **Landed, and the fixture corrected the premise.** `cgltf_validate` *does* catch the disagreement (code 4), so both loaders already rejected such a file before extraction was reached — the task's assumption that it slipped through was wrong. The check is still not redundant: `ExtractMesh` is called **directly, without validation**, by the L3 oracle itself, and had no check of its own, so `POSITION`'s count drove the loop that indexes every other decoded stream and a short `NORMAL` was read past the end of its vector. The check turns that undefined behaviour into a named error for any caller. It runs over **every** attribute the primitive declares, not only the ones the chosen stride uses: an attribute CNA ignores today is still evidence the file is malformed. `accessor-count-mismatch` therefore records **two** refusals, and the rejection test now asserts both — a defence-in-depth check that quietly stopped working would otherwise be invisible behind the layer in front of it. |
| GLTF-061 | Cross-check decoded bounds against `accessor.min`/`max` | ✔ | GLTF-041 | Never read today. Would have caught D4 instantly. **Accept:** `accessor-minmax` warns on divergence via the import report. **Landed as `CrossCheckAccessorBoundsEXT`, run inside `ValidateGltfEXT`.** A **warning**, not a rejection, and the asymmetry is the design: a file whose bounds are merely stale is common and harmless, while the values may still be exactly what the file contains — refusing would turn a diagnostic into a load failure for assets that render correctly today. Scoped to non-normalized `FLOAT` accessors, because an integer accessor's bounds are exact by construction and a normalized one's are in **raw** units while the decode produces unit-range values, so comparing those would report every normalized accessor in every file. The tolerance is `max(1e-5, |bound| · 1e-4)`: bounds are authored as decimal text and values as binary floats, so an exporter's rounding routinely puts an extreme value a few ULPs outside its stated bound, and a check without tolerance would warn on a large share of real files and be switched off within a week. A corpus sweep asserts every generated fixture agrees with its own bounds — the assertion that would have caught `D4` on the campaign baseline. |
| GLTF-062 | Sparse + interleaved base accessor | ✔ | GLTF-047 | **CONFIRMED cgltf bug** (§9.3): the reader walks the sparse **values** array at `accessor->stride` while cgltf's own validator sizes it tightly, so every override after the first is misplaced when the base view is interleaved. `sparse-interleaved-base` decodes correctly through `ApplySparseOverridesTightly`, applied after every unpack site in the importer; the oracle restates the same rule independently; `VendoredParserStillMisreadsSparseValuesAtTheBaseStride` pins the underlying defect so a cgltf upgrade retires the workaround. |

### Phase 3 — Indices and primitive topology · owner: `GLTF-MESH`
*Entry: Phase 2 — **waived for the Track A tasks `GLTF-063` and `GLTF-071`** (§28.1). Exit: no
primitive mode is ever silently reinterpreted; indices decode exactly.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-063 | **[P0] Sparse-safe, bounds-checked index reader** | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `cgltf_accessor_read_index` returns `0` for sparse or null-`bufferView` with no error channel; `f3` decoded `[0,0,0,0,0,0]` for expected `[0,1,2,0,2,3]`. Replace with a CNA reader mirroring `UnpackAccessor`. **Accept:** `sparse-indices` decodes exactly; **critical path.** **Done** (`ef3bc2cbf`): `UnpackIndexAccessor` in `GltfImportCore.cpp`. `sparse-indices` decodes `[0,1,2,0,2,3]` exactly, at L2, L3 and (since `GLTF-007`) byte-exact at L5. Resolves sparse overrides per §3.6.2.3 including the zero-initialised base array and an independent sparse-index component type; requires SCALAR, non-normalized and one of the three unsigned types; bounds-checks every read against the owning bufferView with overflow-checked span arithmetic; reads components with `memcpy`. **D4 → `fixed`**, its record kept as the regression witness. The attribute path is untouched (`GLTF-041` still green). |
| GLTF-064 | Index component-type fixtures | ✔ | GLTF-063 | `u8-idx` ✅ (`f11`), `u16-idx` ✅, `u32-idx` ✔. **Accept:** all three exact at L3. **Closed:** `u32-idx` is now a corpus fixture, so all three widths are under every corpus-wide sweep — L2's decode, L3's index list and L5's packed bytes — instead of `u32` living only on an accessor hand-authored inside a test. A 32-bit index accessor on a three-vertex triangle is legal and is what an exporter that does not narrow emits: the width is a property of the accessor, not of the vertex count. That is also what makes the fixture say something the other two cannot — CNA chooses its **index buffer** width by vertex count, so this fixture's 32-bit source indices must arrive as a 16-bit buffer, and the L5 golden proves the narrowing rather than echoing the file. |
| GLTF-065 | Null-`bufferView` index accessor is an explicit error | ✔ | GLTF-063 | Today it silently yields zeros. **Accept:** clear error. **Done** (`ef3bc2cbf`): an index accessor with neither a bufferView nor sparse data throws naming both; one with no bufferView *and* sparse data is legal per §3.6.2.3 and decodes from the zero-initialised base array, which is tested separately. |
| GLTF-066 | Sparse index accessor L2 dump | ✔ | GLTF-063 | **Accept:** the L2 dump shows the override applied. **Shown, and shown to be *the override* rather than a plausible number** — which is the whole difficulty with this row: "applied" is only checkable against what the dump would have said without it. All three sparse fixtures now state their **base array** in the manifest (`baseValuesIfSparseIgnored`, the values a decoder that skipped the `sparse` block would produce), and `ASparseOverrideIsVisibleInTheDumpAndNotJustPlausible` asserts the decoded values match the effective expectation **and differ from that base**. Without the second half, a fixture whose base happened to equal its overridden values would pass while proving nothing — which is exactly how **D4** hid: `cgltf_accessor_read_index` returned 0 for every element, and 0 is a legal index. `sparse-indices` is the sharpest of the three: its base ends in 0, so ignoring the override yields the degenerate triangle `(0,2,0)` — a quad missing half of itself, and nothing downstream would call that an error. |
| GLTF-067 | Non-indexed primitives synthesise `0..count-1` | ✔ | GLTF-063 | **Accept:** locked at L3. **Closed:** `non-indexed-triangles` is the corpus fixture the note owed — a `TRIANGLES` primitive with no `indices` accessor at all. The topology is deliberate: `f12` (`mode-points`) stopped being able to prove this when `GLTF-071` began rejecting `POINTS` **before** the implicit range is synthesised, so the fixture has to carry a mode that reaches the synthesis. Three vertices, so the synthesised list is `[0,1,2]` and the **L5 golden carries it as real index bytes** — which is the assertion an L3 value comparison cannot make: a synthesis producing nothing at all leaves an empty index buffer rather than a wrong one. |
| GLTF-068 | Validate `index < vertexCount` before packing | ✔ | GLTF-063 | `static_cast<uint16_t>` truncates silently when `vertexCount ≤ 65535`. **Accept:** `bad-index-out-of-range` errors with the offending value. **Closed:** the fixture the acceptance names by id now exists, and the corpus rejection sweep asserts the diagnostic contains **`index 2 = 7`** and **`only 3 vertices`** — the value, its position in the list and the count, because "an index is out of range" in a 200 k-triangle mesh is not actionable. The authored value is 7 against three vertices deliberately: large enough to be out of range, small enough that no 16-bit truncation is involved, so what fails is the range check rather than the narrowing. One thing the fixture *taught*: a full load refuses it one layer earlier, because cgltf's own validator bounds an index accessor against the first attribute's count. That is defence in depth rather than the same check twice, and it is why the CNA-side check is exercised through `ExtractMesh` **directly** — the L3 oracle and the offline converter both reach it that way. |
| GLTF-069 | 16- vs 32-bit index selection rule | ✅ | GLTF-068 | `use32BitIndices = vertexCount > 65535`. **Accept:** documented and tested at both sides of the boundary. `GltfIndexForm.TheIndexWidthFollowsTheVertexCountAtBothSidesOfTheBoundary` builds a 65535- and a 65536-vertex mesh and asserts both the flag and the emitted byte count. The rule keys on the VERTEX count, not the largest index used: an index buffer must address every vertex the part owns. |
| GLTF-070 | Record the "always materialise an index buffer" decision | ✅ | GLTF-067 | **Accept:** documented in `docs/gltf-conventions.md` and tested. The decision is to always materialise one, and the reason is that it keeps a single draw path: every renderer's draw, `ModelMeshPart`, the `.cnj` format and the L5 goldens would each otherwise need a non-indexed variant, and each of those is a place two paths drift. The cost is 2 bytes per vertex against the 32–68 the vertex itself takes. The thing it must never become is an *empty* buffer — that draws nothing, so a model vanishes rather than errors — which `GltfIndexForm.AnIndexlessPrimitiveIsMaterialisedAsASequentialIndexBuffer` pins. |
| GLTF-071 | **[P0] Read `prim.type`; never silently reinterpret** | ✔ | GLTF-063 | `cgltf_primitive_type` has **0 occurrences**; `f4` (strip) and `f12` (points) both decoded as triangle lists. **Accept:** every mode is classified; **critical path.** **Done** (`07312274e`): `PrimitiveTopology` + `ClassifyPrimitiveTopology`/`PrimitiveTopologyName`/`PrimitiveTopologyMode`/`IsPrimitiveTopologySupported`, carried on `MeshOut::topology`. All seven modes classify by number and by specification name; a mode outside 0…6 is rejected rather than assumed. `TRIANGLES` imports byte-identically to before, including the very common no-`mode`-key case. Every other topology is rejected by `ExtractMesh` with its mode named by number and name, and **no index list reaches the `numIndices / 3` path at all**. **D5 → `partially-remediated`**: silent corruption is gone, but the row's own scope ends at classification — `GLTF-072` still owns conversion, so `f4`/`f12` do not import. |
| GLTF-072 | Implement the §10.1 per-mode policy | ✔ | GLTF-071 | Pass through `TRIANGLES`; convert `TRIANGLE_STRIP`/`TRIANGLE_FAN` to a triangle list at import; carry `LINES`/`LINE_STRIP`; convert `LINE_LOOP`; `POINTS` via CNAEXT or an explicit error. **Accept:** all seven `mode-*` fixtures decode to the manifest's triangle/line/point list. **Landed:** all seven `mode-*` fixtures now exist (the topology group is complete at 7) and each decodes to its manifest list. `ConvertToTriangleList` rewrites a strip or fan at import with winding preserved — a strip's odd triangle emits `(i+1, i, i+2)` — and `IsPrimitiveTopologySupported` widens to exactly the three triangle-producing modes. `MeshOut` gained `sourceTopology` alongside `topology`, so a conversion is visible rather than lossy: `topology` is always `Triangles` on anything `ExtractMesh` returns, while `sourceTopology` still names what the file declared (`GLTF-082`). The vertex buffer is untouched by a conversion — only the index list is rewritten — which is exactly why no renderer changed. **Scope boundary:** the four topologies that describe *no* triangles stay rejected, and deliberately so. They decode correctly; what they lack is a draw path, since every loader still computes `numIndices / 3`. Importing them now would move the original defect from the import layer to the draw layer rather than fix it, so `GLTF-073` (a real `PrimitiveType` on `ModelMeshPart`), `GLTF-078` (a topology-aware count) and `GLTF-077` (the points decision) own them, and the rejection message names them. D5 stays `partially-remediated` for that reason, with `GLTF-072` in `closedTasks`. |
| GLTF-073 | Carry primitive type on `ModelMeshPart` | ✔ | GLTF-072, GLTF-025 | XNA already has `PrimitiveType` at the device level. **Accept:** a line-mode fixture draws as lines on `OPENGLES3`. **Landed** behind the `GLTF-025` gate (`docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.1), which chose the shape: a CNAEXT `getPrimitiveTypeEXTProperty`/`setPrimitiveTypeEXTProperty` pair reusing the **existing** `PrimitiveType` rather than a glTF-flavoured enum, defaulting to `TriangleList`. It is CNAEXT even though the type is XNA's own, because real XNA passes the topology to `DrawIndexedPrimitives` and every XNA part is implicitly a triangle list. `ModelMesh::Draw` now draws the part's own topology, both loaders set it, and the `.cnj` carries a `"primitiveTopology"` field written only when it is not the default — so an ordinary triangle-list asset's `.cnj` is unchanged and a pre-`GLTF-073` file still loads as the triangle list it could only ever have been. |
| GLTF-074 | Strip→list conversion preserves winding | ✔ | GLTF-072 | Odd triangles in a strip have reversed winding. **Accept:** `mode-triangle-strip` L3 index list equals the manifest exactly, including the swap on odd triangles. **Landed with `GLTF-072`.** `GltfConformanceL3` asserts the converted list against the manifest's own `triangles` expansion — derived independently of `importPolicy.indices`, so a generator bug that got the rule wrong in both places still fails — and `GltfPrimitiveTopology.StripConversionSwapsTheOddTrianglesCorners` pins the rule on a five-index run where the alternation has to continue rather than merely apply once. |
| GLTF-075 | Fan→list conversion | ✔ | GLTF-072 | **Accept:** `mode-triangle-fan` matches the manifest. **Landed with `GLTF-072`.** `mode-triangle-fan` is generated and imports to `[0,1,2]`,`[0,2,3]`. The two rules are asserted to *differ* on identical input, so neither fixture can pass under the other's conversion. |
| GLTF-076 | `LINE_LOOP` → `LINE_STRIP` + closing segment | ✔ | GLTF-072 | **Accept:** manifest match. **Landed:** `CloseLineLoop` appends the first index again, so `mode-line-loop`'s `[0,1,2]` imports as `[0,1,2,0]` with `MeshOut::topology` = `LineStrip` and a primitive count of 3 against `mode-line-strip`'s 2. The closing segment is implicit in the mode, so dropping the mode lost a whole segment before it lost the topology — the two fixtures author the identical index list and differ only by it. |
| GLTF-077 | Decide `POINTS` support per renderer | ✔ | GLTF-072 | **Accept:** either a CNAEXT point-list path or a documented, per-renderer explicit rejection — never a triangle. **Decided: a point list imports as `PrimitiveType::PointListEXT` and support is answered per renderer at draw time, not by refusing the data.** Import and draw are different questions, and conflating them is what made the original defect: a renderer that cannot draw points is not a reason to discard a file's geometry. `mode-points` imports with 4 points and 0 triangles; whether a given renderer honours `PointListEXT` is its own capability answer. |
| GLTF-078 | Primitive-count helper for every topology | ✔ | GLTF-072 | All three loaders hardcode `numIndices / 3`. **Accept:** one shared helper; §12.3 table asserted at L5. **Landed:** `PrimitiveCountForTopology` implements §12.3's table once, and the loaders' hardcoded `numIndices / 3` is gone from the glTF paths. Stated independently in C++ and in the generator on purpose — a count that agreed with itself but not with the specification would pass either alone. A run too short for one primitive yields `0`, never a negative count, which is where `n-1` and `n-2` could have underflowed. The legacy `SkinnedModel` `.cnj` reader keeps its own `/3`: that format has no topology field and only ever held triangles. |
| GLTF-079 | Degenerate/empty primitive handling | ✅ | GLTF-072 | Zero indices, or an index count not divisible by the topology's stride. **Accept:** deterministic behaviour, reported. **Decided here:** the incomplete tail is dropped and counted in `MeshOut::droppedIncompleteIndicesEXT`, which `ContentManager` logs — reading the remainder as a further primitive walks off the end of the run, and dropping it silently leaves a model missing a face with nothing to point at. Per mode: a multiple of 3 for `TRIANGLES`, of 2 for `LINES`, and a strip/fan/loop shorter than one primitive becomes empty. `cgltf_validate` checks none of this. Four `GltfIndexForm` cases: the trim, the zero-drop control, the odd `LINES` count, and the too-short strip. |
| GLTF-080 | Topology + Draco interaction | ✔ | GLTF-072 | Draco always decodes to triangles. **Accept:** a Draco primitive declaring a non-triangle mode is rejected or documented. **Established, refused and corrected against the extension's narrower normative rule.** `KHR_draco_mesh_compression` permits exactly `TRIANGLES` and `TRIANGLE_STRIP`, not every core topology that happens to produce triangles: `TRIANGLE_FAN` is invalid alongside the four point/line modes. `IsDracoTopologyAllowedEXT` owns that separate 2/5 partition instead of reusing `ProducesTriangles`'s correct core-glTF 3/4 partition. Five real parsed primitives prove each invalid mode is rejected with the two allowed modes named **before decoder availability matters**. For the permitted strip case, a decoded `draco::Mesh` already exposes explicit face triples; `NormalizeTriangleIndicesEXT` preserves those as the output triangle list instead of passing them through ordinary strip conversion again and inventing overlapping triangles. The 19-case `GltfPrimitiveTopology` suite locks the seven-mode partition and a discriminating two-face normalization witness without requiring `libdraco`; an actual compressed parity asset remains `GLTF-353`, not hidden here. |
| GLTF-081 | Topology + morph interaction | ✅ | GLTF-072 | Deltas are per-vertex, so topology conversion must not reorder vertices. **Accept:** asserted for `mode-triangle-strip` + morph. **`mode-triangle-strip-morph`.** The conversion rewrites the **index** list (`[0,1,2,3]` → `[0,1,2, 2,1,3]`) while morph deltas are addressed **per vertex**, by position in the target accessor — so the two only coexist if the vertex order is left completely alone. The failure guarded against is not a crash but the obvious optimisation: vertices 1 and 2 appear in both of a four-index strip's triangles, so de-duplicating or compacting them would still produce a mesh, still produce a morph, and put every delta on the wrong vertex — a plausible deformation of the wrong shape. Asserted on the vertex count, the delta count, and each delta's landing vertex; the four deltas move different distances along +Z so a permutation is a different staircase rather than a subtly different surface, which identical deltas would have made indistinguishable from correctness. |
| GLTF-082 | Report every topology conversion in the import report | ✅ | GLTF-035, GLTF-072 | **Accept:** conversions are visible, not silent. **`MeshOut` already carried both topologies and neither loader said anything about them.** Now reported per primitive, at **debug** rather than warning severity — deliberately, because unlike every other report in that block the conversion is *exact* and loses nothing. What it does do is renumber: the triangle a consumer draws is not at the index the file put it at, so anything mapping a picked triangle or a debug index back to the source primitive is wrong without knowing. `sourceTopology != topology` is the signal, and a test asserts it is true exactly when a conversion happened — for a strip and a fan, and not for an already-listed primitive or a line primitive that is not converted at all. |

### Phase 4 — Vertex semantics · owner: `GLTF-MESH`
*Entry: Phase 3. Exit: every supported semantic decodes exactly, with documented defaults and losses.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-083 | `POSITION` is required and always at offset 0 | ✔ | GLTF-041 | **Accept:** locked at L3/L5; a primitive without `POSITION` errors (already does). **Both halves locked.** The refusal matters beyond conformance: `POSITION`'s accessor count is what drives the packing loop, so a primitive without one has no vertex count at all and would otherwise produce a silently empty mesh. The offset half asserts the bytes *at* offset 0 are the authored positions, which is the other side of `GLTF-150`'s table claim — together they stop the table and the packer drifting apart. |
| GLTF-084 | `NORMAL` decode and default | ✅ | GLTF-083 | Absent normals currently emit `(0,0,1)` — a **fabricated** default, not glTF's "compute flat normals". **Accept:** decision recorded (§8 of Phase 8) and tested. **The default half is `GLTF-173`'s**, decided and landed there: CNA computes the area-weighted vertex normal, `normal-absent` is tilted out of the XY plane so it can tell that from the old fabricated `(0,0,1)`, and the renormalisation policy table in `docs/gltf-conventions.md` records where each generated normal is made unit. The **decode** half was the untested one. Core §3.7.2.1 permits only FLOAT `NORMAL`; normalized BYTE and SHORT are the additional formats of **required** `KHR_mesh_quantization`. That distinction is now authored rather than implied: both quantized fixtures list the extension in `extensionsRequired`, `normal-quantized` stores each SHORT VEC3 at the mandated 8-byte stride, and `normalized-i8-normal` stores each BYTE VEC3 at stride 4. CNA's extension registry claims the already-generic decode path and the corpus gate proves the official Validator accepts both source shapes. Reading the SHORT witness as raw integers gives a normal of **length 32767**, which no later layer rejects and which lights as pure white. It authors only 32767 and 0, mapping exactly to 1.0 and 0.0, so the assertion is `EXPECT_FLOAT_EQ`; its three normals point along different axes so a component permutation fails differently per vertex. The BYTE witness separately pins the signed −128 clamp. A corpus-wide sweep asserts every packed normal is unit length whatever its storage form, which doubles as a check on the pass-through policy: an authored normal is never renormalised, so a mis-decode has nowhere to hide. |
| GLTF-085 | Warn when `NORMAL` is dropped by stride selection | ✔ | GLTF-084, GLTF-035 | Strides 20 and 24 have no normal slot; the attribute is silently discarded. **Accept:** import report entry. **Landed with `GLTF-241`**, which is where the case stopped being hypothetical: a vertex-coloured PBR primitive lands on stride 24 and so loses its normals *and* its PBR material at once. `MeshOut::droppedNormalForStrideEXT` is set at extraction and `ContentManager` reports it per primitive, naming the stride that has no slot; `GltfKnownDefectTests` asserts the flag on `mat-vertex-color-pbr`. |
| GLTF-086 | `TANGENT` decode for non-PBR meshes | ✔ | GLTF-083 | Read only when `usePbr`. **Accept:** decision recorded; either carried or reported. **Decided: reported, because it cannot be carried** (`docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.10). Only PBR strides 48/60 and 68/76 have a tangent slot — unlike the material factors `GLTF-219` ungated, which `MeshOut` could hold even with no effect to consume them, there is literally nowhere to put a tangent in a stride-32 vertex. `MeshOut::droppedTangentForStrideEXT` names it and both loaders log it, matching `GLTF-241`'s treatment of the dropped normal. Worth reporting rather than shrugging at: a file that authored tangents did so for a reason, usually a normal map the material also declares. |
| GLTF-087 | `TEXCOORD_0` decode | ✔ | GLTF-041 | **Accept:** locked at L3. **Locked as the default the other UV rules are deviations from.** |
| GLTF-088 | `TEXCOORD_n` selection by the base-colour texture's `texCoord` | ✔ | GLTF-087 | `UsesTexcoordSetSelectedByMaterial` already covers it. **Accept:** extended to the corpus. **Locked with two disjoint UV sets**, so which one was selected is readable off any single vertex. This original single-map assertion remains useful, while `GLTF-182`/`183` extend it to two simultaneously packed streams and independent selection for all five PBR maps. |
| GLTF-089 | `COLOR_0` as `VEC3` and `VEC4` | ✔ | GLTF-041 | Missing alpha defaults to 255. **Accept:** both shapes locked at L5 byte level. **Locked against each other**, not merely each against a literal: the two shapes must agree byte-for-byte on the three channels they share, so the `VEC3` path cannot quietly diverge from the `VEC4` one. The implied alpha is asserted as **255, not 0** — 0 being exactly what a zero-initialised buffer supplies, and the value that would make every vertex of such a mesh fully transparent. |
| GLTF-090 | Document `COLOR_0` precision loss to 8-bit | ✅ | GLTF-089 | **Accept:** documented in `docs/gltf-conventions.md`'s table of attributes CNA has nowhere to put, and asserted at the endpoints — an authored red arrives as `255,0,0,255`. Deliberately **not** warned about: quantisation is lossless at the endpoints and within half a step everywhere else, and is what every XNA `Color` does, so a warning would be noise on every coloured mesh. The rounding rule itself (`round`, not truncate) is `GLTF-154`'s, tested at mid values where the two differ. |
| GLTF-091 | `COLOR_1+` ignored — document | ✅ | GLTF-089 | **Accept:** `MeshOut::extraColorSetsEXT` counts them and the loaders **warn** with the count. XNA's vertex layouts carry one colour channel, so a second set is not a feature CNA declines gracefully — it is data that does not arrive, and a mesh whose real tint lives in `COLOR_1` imports looking like a mistake nobody can trace. Tested alongside `COLOR_0` surviving, so the warning cannot come from the first set being mishandled. |
| GLTF-092 | Custom `_*` attributes ignored — document | ✅ | GLTF-083 | **Accept:** documented, no error, and **named**. §3.7.2.1 reserves the `_` prefix for application-specific semantics precisely so a reader may ignore them, so `MeshOut::ignoredCustomAttributesEXT` records each by name and the loaders log them at *debug* level rather than warning — a file whose own tooling reads `_BATCHID` still imports as ordinary geometry, and that is the expected outcome rather than a problem. Tested with a `_BATCHID` attribute alongside a second colour set, which get different severities for exactly that reason. |
| GLTF-093 | `JOINTS_0` component types | ✅ | GLTF-057 | `UNSIGNED_BYTE` and `UNSIGNED_SHORT` decode correctly; the **`uint8` `BlendIndices` pack** truncates above 255. **Accept:** decode locked; truncation handled by `GLTF-254`. **Closed after reconciling the dependent fixes and replacing a test that could not discriminate.** `GLTF-261` refuses a skin above the 72-entry GPU palette before packing, and `GLTF-254` still guards every remapped value against the byte range, so the former >255 residue is unreachable and explicitly defended rather than silently truncated. `GltfAccessorDecodeLock.UnsignedByteAndUnsignedShortJointIndicesDecodeAndPackExactly` uses `skin-four-weighted` (`UNSIGNED_BYTE`, values through 3) and `skin-ushort-joint-indices` (`UNSIGNED_SHORT`, values through 2), checks component type and the non-normalized L2 values, then compares every component against production L3 `BlendIndices`; the shared L5 oracle pins the final bytes. This replaces `NonNormalizedJointIndicesStayIntegral`, which used `skin-armature-ancestor` — twelve zero indices, a fixture on which correct integer decoding and the wrong normalized `0/255` are indistinguishable. |
| GLTF-094 | `WEIGHTS_0` component types | ✔ | GLTF-041 | `f9` proves `FLOAT`; normalized `u8`/`u16` weights need a fixture. **Accept:** all three exact at L5. **Locked, and the two integer forms were genuinely unexercised** — every fixture in the corpus authors `FLOAT`. The `u8` case uses 128 + 127 = 255 so the pair sums to exactly 1: an off-by-one divisor (`c/256`) would sum to 0.996, which `GLTF-256` would then renormalise *back* to 1 and hide behind its own correction, so the decoded values are asserted rather than only their sum. At 16 bits the same error is 15 parts per million — inside the renormalisation tolerance, so a sum could never catch it at all. |
| GLTF-095 | `JOINTS_1`/`WEIGHTS_1` detection and reporting | ✔ | GLTF-035 | Silently ignored — a >4-influence mesh loses its tail. **Accept:** import report entry; `GLTF-257` decides support. **Landed as a measurement, not a count.** `MeshOut::extraInfluenceSetsEXT` says how many sets were authored and `worstDroppedInfluenceEXT` says the largest share of a single vertex's influence that truncation discarded — measured **before** `GLTF-256`'s renormalisation, because that is the only number distinguishing a fifth influence weighted 0.002 (exporter noise) from one weighted 0.4 (a visibly different pose). Both loaders report it. New fixture `skin-eight-influences`, whose three vertices lose 40%, 1% and nothing, so a report that merely counted sets could not pass. |
| GLTF-096 | Attribute-set index parsing (`_0`, `_1`, …) | ✔ | GLTF-088 | cgltf's `attribute.index`. **Accept:** locked. **Locked with `GLTF-088`** — the set index is what `texCoord` selects, and the two sets are disjoint so a mis-parse cannot land on the right values. |
| GLTF-097 | Vertex-count consistency across all streams | ✔ | GLTF-060 | See `GLTF-060`. **Accept:** shared assertion. **Landed as exactly that — one assertion, not a pair of special cases.** `GLTF-060`'s check is driven by the primitive's own attribute list, so it covers every declared stream including ones CNA does not yet read; there was never a second place to add it. Tested in both directions: the malformed fixture is refused with a diagnostic naming the attribute and the constraint, **and** every primitive of every other corpus fixture extracts cleanly — which is what stops the check being satisfied by a version that refuses anything with more than one attribute. |
| GLTF-098 | Deterministic vertex ordering | ✔ | GLTF-083 | `ExtractMesh` emits vertices in accessor order. **Accept:** locked at L5 — required for morph delta indexing. **Locked, and it has the most dangerous failure mode in the file.** A morph target's delta array is indexed *by a vertex's position in the buffer*; nothing in the morph path carries an identity for a vertex. So any reordering during extraction — a cache-friendly sort, a dedupe pass, a Draco round-trip that renumbers — applies every delta to the wrong vertex, and **no test in the morph path could see it**, because the deltas would still be the right values on the wrong vertices. The fixture's positions descend in X, so a sort is visible immediately. |
| GLTF-099 | Stride selection decision table as data, not nested ternaries | ✅ | GLTF-072 | The current one-line ternary chain is unreadable and duplicated implicitly in renderers. **Accept:** a table-driven selector with a unit test per row of §2.3. **`VertexLayoutTableEXT()`**, indexed by a named `VertexLayoutRequestEXT` rather than by position in a ternary. The chain had three problems a table does not, and only the first is readability: it could not be **enumerated**, so no test could walk its rows and every assertion had to pick a combination by hand; it could not be asked what a row **loses**, which is what `GLTF-100` needs; and every renderer's own `ApplyLayout` is an implicit restatement of it with no way to check the two agree. The tests close all three — totality over all **sixteen** combinations of the four flags rather than the nine the table spells (`useDualTexture` is only ever set alongside three falses today, and a future change setting it elsewhere would fall through a ternary silently), every row cross-checked against `InferredLayoutForStride` for the slots it claims, and a corpus sweep asserting the importer and the table agree on real assets. That sweep initially reached only four strides because it passed `nullptr` for the skeleton, leaving all four skinned rows unexercised while still reporting success — the exact blind spot the file exists to close, so it now builds each file's real skeleton. |
| GLTF-100 | Reject unrepresentable attribute combinations loudly | ✅ | GLTF-099 | e.g. `usePbr && colored` is currently impossible and silently downgrades. **Accept:** the combination is either supported or reported. **Reported, per table row.** Each row of `GLTF-099`'s table now states what its stride cannot carry and **why**, and `MeshOut::unrepresentableForStrideEXT` carries that text to the loaders. The point is that a downgrade stops being a property of whichever ternary branch was taken and becomes a fact about the layout, enumerable and testable: three tests hold the text honest in both directions — a row claiming to lose a Normal must select a stride with no normal slot, a row claiming to lose *nothing* must select one carrying every slot its combination needs (a PBR row without a tangent would normal-map with a rest-pose basis and look like a material bug), and every lossy row must give a reason rather than only a casualty. Reported at **debug**, deliberately: each individual consequence — the dropped normal (`GLTF-085`), the dropped tangent (`GLTF-086`), the dropped PBR material (`GLTF-241`) — already warns on its own, and this is the summary of the combination an author is actually looking at, not a second alarm. |
| GLTF-101 | L3 semantic-mesh manifest for every corpus asset | ✅ | GLTF-005 | **Accept:** `MeshOut` field-by-field comparison for every asset. **The comparison already existed; what did not was any guarantee it was total.** `GltfConformanceL3` iterates the *manifest's* primitives, so a fixture whose `l3.primitives` is empty passed it without comparing anything at all — a sweep reporting success over a set it had silently shrunk to nothing. And the converse was never checked either: a primitive the importer produced that no manifest entry named was simply never compared, so a multi-primitive mesh whose second primitive stopped being described would import however it liked with every layer above reporting success. Both directions are now asserted, over all 63 importable fixtures and 64 primitives, with a floor so the corpus cannot shrink silently. (The row's "135 assets" was the count projected when the plan was written; the corpus settled at 69, each asset earning its place by what it discriminates rather than by hitting a target.) |
| GLTF-102 | Attribute fuzz: random valid permutations | ✅ | GLTF-040 | **Accept:** no crash, no sanitiser finding, no silent drop without a report entry. **200 seeded permutations**, each a valid single-primitive document built in the test: a random subset of `NORMAL`/`TANGENT`/`TEXCOORD_0`/`TEXCOORD_1`/`COLOR_0`/`COLOR_1`/`_BATCHID`, shuffled into a random **declaration order** (glTF's `attributes` is a JSON object, so nothing constrains it, and an importer keying off position rather than name would pass every corpus fixture and fail here), optionally skinned, optionally with a material, optionally unlit. Seeded rather than clock-driven, because a fuzz whose failures cannot be reproduced is a flake generator. The asserted property is not "it does not crash" — that is the floor — but the campaign's thesis: **every authored attribute either arrives or is named in a report**, checked in both directions so a report firing for an attribute nobody authored fails too. `COLOR_1` must be counted, `_BATCHID` named, and the dropped-normal/dropped-tangent flags must agree with the stride actually selected and with `GLTF-100`'s combination summary. The first version reached only two strides because the document carried `JOINTS_0`/`WEIGHTS_0` and no `skins` entry — so nothing was ever skinned, while the fuzz reported success — which is the same shrunk-set failure `GLTF-101` is about; it now emits a real skin and an unlit variant and reaches six of the eight ABI strides. |
