### Phase 10 — Textures, images, samplers, colour space · owner: `GLTF-TEXTURE`
*Entry: Phase 9. Exit: samplers are honoured and every map is sampled in the right colour space.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-193 | Embedded `bufferView` images | ✔ | GLTF-026 | **Accept:** locked at L6 (texture dimensions + a sampled pixel). **Locked on the image's own PNG signature and its trailing `IEND`, not on a byte count** — a length check alone passes for any wrongly-offset slice of the same buffer. |
| GLTF-194 | External URI images | ✔ | GLTF-028 | **Accept:** locked; a missing file errors clearly. **Locked by `gltf-external-image` plus the negative scratch case.** The fixture's generated PNG sidecar is hashed in the corpus manifest, referenced by a textured material, and byte-compared with the inline fixture and both GLB twins; its signature and terminal `IEND` are checked so an equally long wrong slice cannot pass. The missing-file control remains scratch-only and requires the diagnostic to name the path: the commonest cause is an asset shipped without its textures, and the fix is entirely about which file is absent. |
| GLTF-195 | `data:` URI images | ✔ | GLTF-029 | **Accept:** locked. **`gltf-data-uri-image` is byte-compared with `gltf-external-image` and both GLB twins**, because the point is that they are the same generated PNG through different source paths. The bufferView form remains a focused scratch document, and a `data:` URI with no comma is additionally refused — decoding the media type as base64 would yield plausible bytes that are not an image and fail much later with no useful cause. |
| GLTF-196 | PNG and JPEG decode | ✔ | GLTF-193 | Via `stb_image` in `Texture2D::FromStream`. **Accept:** both locked with a known-pixel fixture. **Locked on the decision that selects the decoder:** §3.9.2 makes `mimeType` authoritative over the file name, so a `.png`-named file declared `image/jpeg` *is* a JPEG. Trusting the name hands the wrong decoder bytes it will refuse, and the texture disappears with the blame on the image rather than the naming. The sourceless case is asserted to yield **no image** rather than empty bytes — empty bytes would turn a missing texture into a decode error, which points at the wrong thing. |
| GLTF-197 | Image cache keyed by `cgltf_image*` | ✅ | GLTF-193 | Correct within one load. **Accept:** locked; a shared image decodes once. |
| GLTF-198 | Path containment for image URIs | ✔ | GLTF-032 | Same traversal exposure as buffers. **Accept:** rejected with a clear message. **Landed with `GLTF-032`** — one resolver, two gates: the up-front sweep covers `data->images` alongside `data->buffers`, and `ExtractImage` resolves through the same function rather than joining the URI itself, so a caller reaching it without the sweep is still contained. `ExtractImage`'s hand-rolled `cgltf_decode_uri` + `gltfDir / uri` is gone. |
| GLTF-199 | MIME-type vs content sniffing | ✅ | GLTF-196 | **Accept:** `SniffImageExtension` now decides from the bytes — PNG's 8-byte signature, JPEG's SOI marker — with `mimeType`, a `data:` URI's media type and a filename extension all demoted to hints used only when the signature says nothing. Applied to all three source branches, which previously guessed independently. It matters because the extension **travels with the bytes**: the offline path writes a file named with it and a `.cnj` then references that file, so `image/png` above JPEG bytes (what a silently failed "convert to PNG" step produces) handed a consumer a file whose contents did not match its name. Tested in both directions plus the unrecognised-signature fallback. |
| GLTF-200 | Unsupported image formats must not vanish silently | ✔ | GLTF-035 | The real shape is one level up from the row's own description: the texture's `image` is null (the pixels hang off `KHR_texture_basisu`/`EXT_texture_webp` instead), so `ExtractImage` was never reached at all and every downstream check simply read "no texture on this slot". **Landed:** one `ImageForTexture` helper behind all five map finders, feeding `MeshOut::unsupportedTextureSourcesEXT` — one entry per map, naming the map and the extension — which both loaders log. It also catches the second shape, a plain `source` whose `mimeType` is `image/ktx2`/`image/webp`/`image/basis`, at import where the map's name is still known rather than at a decode failure that cannot say which map it was. |
| GLTF-201 | Zero-byte / corrupt image handling | ✅ | GLTF-196 | **Accept:** deterministic every time and never a crash, over the four shapes bytes-from-a-file can actually take: an empty `data:` payload (no bytes fabricated, an extension still owed so a caller can report what it thought it had), a payload that is valid base64 and not an image (the bytes arrive exactly as authored — extraction does not decode), a `data:` URI with no comma (a named `runtime_error`), and a missing external file (named, **with the filename in the message** — a texture that vanishes silently gets blamed on the material, the sampler or the light, never on the file). Runs in the ASan/UBSan CI job with the rest of the glTF suite. |
| GLTF-202 | **Import `cgltf_sampler`** | ✔ | GLTF-025 | `cgltf_sampler`, `mag_filter`, `wrap_s` all have **0 occurrences**. **Accept:** sampler data reaches the model. **Landed:** `SamplerOut` per texture slot on `MeshOut`, read from the texture each material view names, and carried to a new CNAEXT `ModelMeshPart::SamplerStatesEXT`. **This overrules `GLTF-207`'s own sketch**, which proposed hanging the array off `ModelMeshPart::Tag`: `Tag` already *is* `MorphTargetDataEXT` for a morphing part, so a morphing part with clamped textures could not have expressed both — the same one-object collision §1.5 records for rigid clips. A plain property, as `GLTF-073` already established for `PrimitiveTypeEXT`. Per **slot** rather than per material, because glTF attaches a sampler to a texture and a material may legitimately clamp its base colour while repeating its normal map. Recorded as `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.8. |
| GLTF-203 | Map glTF filters/wraps to `SamplerState` | ✔ | GLTF-202 | The §14.2 table. **Accept:** every row tested; `uv-out-of-range-*` correct at L7. **Landed, and it corrected §14.2.** The section claimed XNA "cannot express independent min/mag filters for the four mixed combinations" — it can: the nine `TextureFilter` values, `MinLinearMagPointMipLinear` and its three siblings among them, cover all eight min×mag×mip combinations **exactly**, so those four need no approximation at all. The one real approximation is elsewhere: glTF's `NEAREST`/`LINEAR` minFilters mean *no mipmapping*, which XNA expresses through the texture's level count rather than through `TextureFilter`, so the mip mode is arbitrary there; `SamplerOut::minFilterHasNoMipStage` makes that visible instead of implied. Every row is tested directly — `MapGltfSamplerEXT` takes raw glTF enum values, so the whole table is exercised without a file, which no realistic number of fixtures could match. The end-to-end declaration reaches L6 (`GLTF-208`) and the discriminating `uv-out-of-range-*` framebuffer half is now locked by `GLTF-189`; only the corpus-wide image matrix remains under `GLTF-009`. |
| GLTF-204 | Document the min/mag approximation | ✅ | GLTF-203 | **The row's premise was wrong, and the correction is the deliverable.** §14.2 claimed XNA cannot express independent min/mag filters; it can — the nine `TextureFilter` values cover all eight min×mag×mip combinations exactly, so there is no approximation there to document. `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.8 records that finding, and `GltfSamplerMappingTests` asserts every combination against the XNA value that means exactly it. The **one real** approximation is elsewhere and is recorded where it happens: glTF's `NEAREST`/`LINEAR` minFilters mean *no mipmapping*, and XNA has no filter value for "base level only" — that is a property of the texture's level count. `SamplerOut::minFilterHasNoMipStage` carries it so the arbitrary choice (point, the least-blending mip mode) is visible rather than implied. |
| GLTF-205 | Default sampler when absent | ✅ | GLTF-203 | glTF §5.29: `sampler` is optional, and absent means repeat wrapping with the client free to choose filtering. **Accept:** `LinearWrap`, tested — and the `declared` flag stays false, so an absent sampler is reported as absent rather than as an authored one. Before `GLTF-202`/`GLTF-203` every imported texture drew with the device's `LinearWrap` **by coincidence**; a default that is chosen rather than inherited is what makes that no longer a coincidence, and what makes a future change to it visible. |
| GLTF-206 | Mipmap generation policy | ✔ | GLTF-203 | `Texture2D::FromStream` produces one level; the mipmapped min filters assume more. **Accept:** decision recorded and implemented or explicitly deferred with a report entry. **Explicitly deferred, with the report entry landed.** A generic RGBA downsample is not correct for all five roles: base colour/emissive need sRGB-aware filtering, normals need renormalisation, and metallic-roughness/occlusion need linear per-channel filtering. CNA therefore keeps one level and every LOD samples level zero — deterministic authored data with lower minification quality, rather than generated material corruption. `SamplerOut::minFilterRequiresMipChain` marks exactly the four explicit `*_MIPMAP_*` filters (undefined is not an authored request); `MeshOut::mipmappedSamplerMapsWithoutMipChainEXT` names only present maps the selected effect samples; both direct and offline loaders warn. `sampler-trilinear` is the positive real-file witness and base-level-only `tex-reference-checkerboard` the silence control, with the raw table covering all four mip enums. Policy: `docs/gltf-conventions.md`; API/report review: `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.8. |
| GLTF-207 | Where sampler state lives on the model | ✅ | GLTF-202, GLTF-025 | **Accept: reviewed, and the review overruled this row's own sketch.** `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.8: a `SamplerStateArrayEXT` on `ModelMeshPart::Tag` cannot work, because `Tag` already *is* `MorphTargetDataEXT` for a morphing part — a morphing part with clamped textures could express only one of the two. That is the same one-object-per-`Tag` collision §1.5 records for rigid clips. What landed instead is a plain CNAEXT property per slot (`getSamplerStatesEXTProperty`/`setSamplerStateEXTProperty`), the shape `GLTF-073` already established for `PrimitiveTypeEXT`, with slots named by a `TextureSlotEXT` enum rather than bare indices — because an array indexed by an untyped `int` is exactly what acquires an off-by-one when a slot is added. Per slot rather than per material, because glTF attaches a sampler to a *texture*: a material may legitimately clamp its base colour and repeat its normal map. |
| GLTF-208 | Sampler state actually applied at draw time | ✅ | GLTF-207 | **Accept:** the L6 capture now records the sampler state **per slot**, and `GltfConformanceL6.ADeclaredSamplerSurvivesImportOntoThePartThatDrawsWithIt` asserts `tex-reference-checkerboard`'s `NEAREST`/`CLAMP_TO_EDGE` arrives as `Point`/`Clamp` on the drawn part — three separate departures from the `LinearWrap` every imported texture used to get by inheriting whatever the device had. `GltfSamplerMappingTests` had the mapping covered exhaustively over raw enum values; what it could not show is that a **real file's** sampler reaches a part, because until `GLTF-190` no corpus fixture had a texture. A control asserts an untextured fixture still reports `LinearWrap`, which is what catches a per-part array that is really shared state. **Carried, application-applied rather than implicitly bound:** `SamplerState` is per-draw device state an XNA application owns, so `Model::Draw` does not set it — the same boundary `GLTF-231` drew for `doubleSided`. `GLTF-189` now proves the complete intended path: select the part's public per-slot state on the device immediately before `Model::Draw`, and all three out-of-range address modes produce their distinct framebuffer colours. Automatic binding would silently take sampler control away from every non-glTF XNA model as well, because those parts carry the compatible `LinearWrap` default and no origin flag. |
| GLTF-209 | **Colour-space decision** | ✔ | GLTF-025 | §13.3 options A/B/C. Today every map is RGBA8 UNORM sampled raw, with no output encode. **Accept:** decision recorded with rationale; provisional recommendation is B (shader-side). **Decided: B**, recorded in full as `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.7. A was rejected on blast radius — a new `SurfaceFormat` across 41 renderers to fix a glTF defect is the wrong order of work — and C on precision plus the fact that it would make an imported texture disagree with the file it came from. B is renderer-local, adoptable one renderer at a time, and observable at L6 without a renderer at all. The decision records three things the shape had to get right: **three flags rather than one**, because "what this texture contains" (×2) and "where this fragment is going" are different kinds of statement and an sRGB render target needs the third switched off without the first two changing; **no flag at all for the three linear maps**, because §3.9.2 leaves no choice there and an invented choice is a thing someone eventually sets wrong; and **factors are never transferred**, only texture samples, since the factors are already linear and `KHR_materials_emissive_strength` may push emissive above 1. |
| GLTF-210 | sRGB decode for base colour and emissive | ✔ | GLTF-209 | **Accept:** a known-sRGB fixture produces the manifest's linear values at L6/L7. **Landed:** `GpuDrawParams::pbrBaseColorTextureIsSrgb`/`pbrEmissiveTextureIsSrgb` carry the declaration; both PBR effects expose it as CNAEXT properties defaulting to glTF's own rule; `EasyGLRenderer`'s two PBR shaders decode the **samples** and multiply the linear factors afterwards. The transfer itself is stated once in `CNA/Internal/Graphics/SrgbTransfer.hpp` — as a macro of string literals, because a renderer builds shader source by literal concatenation and a `const char*` cannot join that — and the C++ functions beside it are the same arithmetic, so the GLSL and the CPU cannot drift into two slightly different curves. `SrgbTransferTests` pins endpoints, mid-grey (the 2.3× error the fix removes, stated as a number), both knees **branch-against-branch** rather than by finite difference, a 256-level round trip, and the no-clamp property `KHR_materials_emissive_strength` depends on. L6 asserts the declarations reach every PBR draw. **The pixel half is now discriminating too:** registered `EasyGL_Pbr_SrgbTransfer` binds a `(128,128,128)` texture separately as base colour and emissive; both rigid and identity-skinned programs return byte 128 through decode+encode and byte 188 when the relevant decode flag is deliberately disabled. A linear factor of 0.5 produces 92, proving the factor is multiplied after sample decode rather than transferred itself. This is focused EasyGL evidence; the corpus matrix and Vulkan remain `GLTF-009`/`GLTF-213`. |
| GLTF-211 | Linear handling for normal / occlusion / metallic-roughness | ✔ | GLTF-209 | **Accept:** asserted unchanged by the decode path. **Landed as an absence, deliberately:** these three maps get **no flag at all**. §3.9.2 declares them linear unconditionally, so there is nothing to configure, and adding a flag would invent a choice someone would eventually set wrong. The decode in the shader touches the base-colour and emissive samples by name and nothing else, so the three are unchanged by construction rather than by a guard that could be inverted. Recorded in `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.7. |
| GLTF-212 | Linear → sRGB encode on output | ✔ | GLTF-209 | **Accept:** a flat-lit fixture's framebuffer values match the manifest. **Landed:** `pbrEncodeOutputToSrgb`, on by default and settable per effect — unlike the two decode flags this is a real policy choice, since an application drawing into an sRGB target or running its own tone map must switch it off. Encoding happens **last** and touches **RGB only** (§3.9.4 makes alpha coverage, never colour), and the fog mix now happens in linear space with `uFogColor` decoded first, since mixing an encoded colour into a linear result would tint the fade as it thickens. **A boundary is named rather than hidden:** because the encode lives in the PBR shader, a scene mixing `PbrEffect` with the stock XNA effects writes differently encoded colour into one render target. That is the price of option B over A; it is not a regression, since the stock effects behave exactly as before and XNA content never had colour management to lose. The proper fix is a colour-managed render target, which belongs with the `SurfaceFormat` work. **Framebuffer evidence now exists on OPENGLES3:** the independently derived OETF maps the old linear byte oracle `(64,74,87)` to `(137,146,158)`, both rigid and identity-skinned programs produce it, and all four regenerated 8×8 PNG regions pass their registered CTests. The analytic mid-grey test independently pins encode placement too: `OETF(decode(128/255)*0.5)` is byte 92 and a base+emissive linear sum is 112 on both programs. The broader corpus rung remains `GLTF-009`; Vulkan agreement remains `GLTF-213`. |
| GLTF-213 | Colour space across every PBR-capable renderer | ✅ | GLTF-212 | **Accept:** identical L7 within tolerance on `OPENGLES3` and `VULKAN`. **Complete, and extended beyond the minimum acceptance.** Every one of the 15 PBR implementations now consumes independent base-decode, emissive-decode and output-encode flags; normal/MR/occlusion samples and material factors stay linear, fog is decoded before the linear mix, and RGB is encoded exactly once at the end. `EveryPbrShaderHonorsColorSpaceDeclarations` locks all three CPU uses and all three shader equations, requiring two copies for separately stored rigid/skinned fragments. The shared byte-128 oracle runs six discriminating cases on each program: decode+encode round-trips to 128, bypass+encode is 188, a linear half factor after decode is 92, and the base+emissive linear sum is 112. OPENGLES3 and Vulkan both pass the exact 12-case matrix, satisfying the named L7 acceptance; native Bgfx, Diligent, LLGL, OpenGL, SDL GPU, WebGPU and Wicked paths add focused execution. Microsoft compilers accept the regenerated SM3/SM5 shaders and all D3D frontends build; D3D9/11 additionally pass 12/12 as explicitly non-DXVK WineD3D diagnostics, while D3D12 remains build-only under the pre-existing Wine swap-chain limitation. Metal is source/ABI-locked on this Linux host. Full evidence and carriers are in `docs/gltf-renderer-pbr-fallbacks.md`. |
| GLTF-214 | Vertex-colour colour space | ✔ | GLTF-089 | glTF vertex colours are **linear**; `BasicEffect` treats them as-is. **Accept:** documented and tested. **Established: they are linear, they stay linear, and that is now stated.** §3.7.2.1 declares `COLOR_0` a **linear** value — unlike `baseColorTexture` and `emissiveTexture`, which §3.9.2 declares sRGB-encoded — so `GLTF-210`'s decode must not touch it. Applying the transfer to a vertex colour would darken it by the same 2.3× mid-grey factor the decode exists to *correct* on a texture. The finding is that this is already right, and right **by construction** rather than by luck: the decode is applied in the shader to two named texture samples, and a vertex colour is neither. What was missing was anything saying so — which is exactly what a future "apply the transfer everywhere" simplification would have needed to run into. The test additionally asserts the fixture has a mid-range component, since at 0 and 1 the sRGB transfer is the identity and a fixture of only those could not tell a decoded vertex colour from an untouched one. |

### Phase 11 — Materials and PBR · owner: `GLTF-MATERIAL`
*Entry: Phase 10. Exit: every §13.1 row is either implemented and tested, or explicitly rejected.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-215 | **Replace the map-presence PBR-selection rule** | ✔ | GLTF-011 | `usePbr = !colored && (normalImage \|\| metallicRoughnessImage)`; `f8` proved a gold factor-only material becomes a white `BasicEffect`. glTF's default material *is* metallic-roughness. **Accept:** a primitive whose material has `pbrMetallicRoughness`, or has no material, imports as PBR. **Landed:** the rule is now the material **model** the file declares, not which maps it carries. Metallic-roughness is glTF's default in two distinct ways, and both are honoured: a primitive with no material at all gets the default material, and a material that merely omits the optional `pbrMetallicRoughness` object still uses that model with default factors. Only `KHR_materials_pbrSpecularGlossiness` and `KHR_materials_unlit` — neither of which CNA's PBR shaders implement — are excluded, alongside the existing `!colored` limit (no PBR shader reads a vertex Color stream). **Consequences, all deliberate:** stride 32 no longer occurs anywhere in the corpus — an unskinned uncoloured primitive is 48 and a skinned one 68 — and the `DualTextureEffect` glTF path is **superseded**: a base-colour + occlusion material now reaches `PbrEffect`'s real `OcclusionMap` instead of CNB-72/73's halved occlusion-as-lightmap approximation, so the CNB-88 brightness compensation is no longer applied to it. `DualTextureEffect` itself is untouched and still reachable through every other content path; a test pins the replacement so it cannot be undone silently. |
| GLTF-216 | **Carry `baseColorFactor` end-to-end** | ✔ | GLTF-215 | Not read anywhere; `MeshOut` has no field. `PbrEffect::DiffuseColor` + `Alpha` already exist and reach the shader. **Accept:** `mat-factor-only-gold` shows `(1,0.72,0.315)` and alpha `0.5` at L6. **Landed:** the factor is read into the material carrier and applied to `PbrEffect`/`SkinnedPbrEffect`'s `DiffuseColor` (RGB) and `Alpha` (A). `mat-factor-only-gold` carries `(1, 0.72, 0.315)` with alpha `0.5` at L3, asserted against the fixture's own spec-derived expectation. **Correction found by `GLTF-237`:** the offline writer did emit `diffuseColor`/`alpha`, but the PBR `.cnj` reader did **not** consume them; the earlier claim that both loaders were already correct was false. `GLTF-237` fixed the reader and locks direct/offline equality at L6. |
| GLTF-217 | Primitive with no material at all | ✔ | GLTF-215 | Falls to white `BasicEffect`. **Accept:** `mat-default` imports as the glTF default metallic-roughness material. **Landed with `GLTF-215`:** `prim.material == nullptr` selects PBR, which is what glTF's default material means. Every materialless corpus fixture now imports at stride 48 (or 68 skinned) with the default factors. |
| GLTF-218 | `baseColorFactor` × `baseColorTexture` | ✔ | GLTF-216 | The two multiply. **Accept:** `mat-basecolor-factor-times-texture` matches at L7. **Landed end to end on the EasyGL reference renderer.** The generated fixture independently authors a one-pixel sRGB texture at byte 128 and a linear factor of 0.5; its manifest states `decode(128/255)=0.2158605`, product `0.10793025` and analytic output byte **92**. L1–L5 lock the file, material semantics, tangent/UV carrier and exact stride-48 buffers; the corpus L6 sweep proves both operands reach the draw. Registered `EasyGL_Gltf_BaseColorFactorTexture` then loads the committed `.gltf` through `ContentManager`, verifies `PbrEffect`, the non-null sRGB texture and `(0.5,0.5,0.5,1)` factor, and renders through `Model::Draw` twice on both OPENGLES2 and OPENGLES3. Ignoring the factor gives 128, multiplying after output encode gives 64, and decoding the already-linear factor gives 61, so none can pass the tolerance of two. Cross-renderer colour-space parity remains `GLTF-213`; corpus-wide PNG orchestration remains `GLTF-009`, neither of which weakens this material equation on the reference renderer. |
| GLTF-219 | `metallicFactor` / `roughnessFactor` ungated | ✔ | GLTF-215 | Read only when `usePbr` today. **Accept:** always read for a metallic-roughness material. **Landed:** both are read for any material carrying `pbrMetallicRoughness`, outside the old `if (usePbr)` guard that was the second half of D7. |
| GLTF-220 | Factors reach the shader | ✔ | GLTF-219 | **Accept:** L6 capture matches the file. **Landed with `GLTF-008`:** `MaterialFactorsReachTheBoundEffect` compares every PBR draw's `diffuseColor`, `pbrMetallicFactor`, `pbrRoughnessFactor` and `emissiveColor` against the manifest's own spec-derived L3 material block, across the corpus. This is the assertion D7 would have failed for the whole of the audit. |
| GLTF-221 | `emissiveFactor` ungated | ✔ | GLTF-215 | **Accept:** `mat-emissive-factor` correct at L6. **Landed:** read for any material, with `KHR_materials_emissive_strength` still applied in the spec's own order. |
| GLTF-222 | `KHR_materials_emissive_strength` ungated | ✔ | GLTF-221 | Applied only when `usePbr`. **Accept:** `mat-emissive-strength` correct, HDR values > 1 preserved. **Landed:** `GLTF-219`/`GLTF-221` moved the whole factor block out from behind the `usePbr` guard, so the multiplier is read for any material; `mat-emissive-strength` now proves it end to end. The fixture states all three numbers separately — the authored `[0.4,0.2,0.1]`, the strength `5`, and the product `[2.0,1.0,0.5]` — because a fixture that gave only the product could not tell "strength applied" from "a factor authored at that value in the first place". The L6 assertion names the two failure shapes apart: an emissive equal to the authored factor means the strength was dropped, and one at or below 1 means it was clamped, which would make carrying the extension pointless. |
| GLTF-223 | Emissive applied after the texture, per spec order | ✅ | GLTF-222 | **Accept:** locked. **Now locked in the real EasyGL shaders, not only by source inspection.** `EasyGL_Pbr_SrgbTransfer` isolates the emissive texture first (128 through decode+encode, 188 with emissive decode deliberately bypassed), then combines a quarter-strength textured base with a half-strength textured emissive term. Both rigid and identity-skinned programs produce byte 112, exactly `OETF(decode(128/255)*0.25 + decode(128/255)*0.5)`. Multiplying the branches, dropping either one or adding emissive after output encoding all produce different values. |
| GLTF-224 | `normalTexture.scale` | ✔ | GLTF-025 | Never read; no effect parameter exists. **Accept:** new `PbrEffect::NormalScale`; `mat-normal-scale` correct at L7. **Landed** as CNAEXT `NormalScaleEXT` on both PBR effects, `GpuDrawParams::pbrNormalScale`, and PBR shaders that scale the sampled tangent-space normal's **X and Y only** — scaling Z as well would merely rescale the vector, which normalization then undoes, so the perturbation would not change at all. **A trap the fixture caught:** cgltf sets a texture view's `scale` to 1 only when it *parses* that view, so a material with no `normalTexture` object leaves it zeroed by `calloc`. Reading it straight through gave 0 — "flatten the map completely" — for every material that simply does not mention one, the exact opposite of glTF's default. An undeclared view now falls back to 1 explicitly, and a test pins that case separately from the authored one. L6 proves the imported scalar. Registered `EasyGL_Pbr_MaterialMaps` supplies the first focused L7 evidence for both rigid and identity-skinned programs: a `(255,128,191)` normal sample produces bytes 104/138/151 at scales 1/.35/0. `GLTF-379` later found that every other PBR backend ignored the transported scalar; all 15 implementations now have a CPU-to-shader inventory lock, and Vulkan independently renders byte 79 at scale 0 versus 35 for the tilted sample on both programs. The named corpus L7 case remains part of `GLTF-009`. |
| GLTF-225 | `occlusionTexture.strength` | ✔ | GLTF-025 | Never read. **Accept:** new `PbrEffect::OcclusionStrength`; `mat-occlusion-strength` correct at L7. **Landed** alongside `GLTF-224`, applied with §3.9.3's own formula `1 + strength * (sampled - 1)`. That form matters: at `strength = 0` the result is **1** — no occlusion at all, whatever the map holds — where the plausible wrong formula (multiplying by the strength) would darken everything to black. The same undeclared-view trap applies and is handled the same way. The focused EasyGL pixel test pins output bytes 137/207/255 for strengths 1/.5/0 on both PBR programs. `GLTF-379` later corrected the same scalar omission in the other 14 PBR backends; the 15-renderer audit locks the exact formula and Vulkan independently renders byte 160 for a red-channel byte 64 at strength .5 on both programs. The named corpus L7 case remains part of `GLTF-009`. |
| GLTF-226 | Occlusion channel is `.r` | ✅ | GLTF-225 | Shader reads `.r`. **Accept:** locked with a three-distinct-channel fixture. **Locked in registered `EasyGL_Pbr_MaterialMaps`:** a `(64,128,192)` linear occlusion texel renders byte 137 on both rigid and identity-skinned paths. Reading green or blue would instead yield approximately 188 or 225, so a channel swap cannot satisfy this oracle. |
| GLTF-227 | Normal-map decode `rgb*2−1` in TBN | ✅ | GLTF-175 | **Accept:** locked at L7. **Locked in registered `EasyGL_Pbr_MaterialMaps`:** `(255,128,191)` decodes to approximately `(1,.0039,.498)` and, under the analytic +Z lighting rig, produces distinct 104/138/151 framebuffer bytes at scales 1/.35/0. The rigid and identity-skinned programs must both pass; omitting `rgb*2−1`, ignoring scale or scaling Z cannot match all three cases. |
| GLTF-228 | **`alphaMode`** | ✔ | GLTF-025 | Never read; `PbrEffect` has `uAlphaTest` but nothing configures it. **Accept:** CNAEXT `AlphaModeEXT` (`Opaque`/`Mask`/`Blend`); all three fixtures correct at L7. **Landed** behind the gate (`docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.3), which chose the shape: `AlphaModeEXT` (`Opaque`/`Mask`/`Blend`) declared **once**, in `Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics` rather than on an effect, so `CNAEXT.md` §5.5's separately-sketched `PbrMaterial::AlphaMode` binds to the same type instead of becoming a second enum with a conversion function. Carried on `MeshOut::material`, through the `.cnj` (written only when not `OPAQUE`, so an ordinary material's `.cnj` is unchanged) and onto both PBR effects. The L7 half of the acceptance criterion waits on `GLTF-009`. |
| GLTF-229 | `alphaCutoff` | ✔ | GLTF-228 | Default 0.5. **Accept:** `alpha-mask` cuts exactly at the manifest threshold. **Landed:** default `0.5`, carried end-to-end alongside `alphaMode`. Kept even when the mode is not `Mask`, because a material that switches modes must not lose the threshold it authored. |
| GLTF-230 | `BLEND` needs blend state and draw ordering | ✅ | GLTF-228 | **Accept:** `alpha-blend` composites correctly; ordering policy documented (CNA does not sort by default). **Verified at the deliberate XNA device-state boundary.** `mat-factor-only-gold` is loaded through `ContentManager` and retains straight `(1,.72,.315)` RGB, alpha `0.5` and `AlphaModeEXT::Blend`. Registered `EasyGL_Gltf_AlphaBlend` parks lighting and output encoding so the hardware equation is observable directly, clears blue, and draws the real `Model` three times under caller-selected states: `Opaque` gives `(255,184,80)`, the required `NonPremultiplied` straight-alpha state gives `(128,92,168)`, and XNA's premultiplied `AlphaBlend` gives the intentionally wrong distinguishing control `(255,184,208)`. All three pass on OPENGLES2 and OPENGLES3. `Model::Draw` preserves application state and source mesh/part order; it does not know the destination surfaces and does not sort transparent primitives. Applications therefore draw opaque/masked work first, then select `BlendState::NonPremultiplied` and issue BLEND parts back-to-front. This is application-applied, not an implicit global-state mutation by a model. |
| GLTF-231 | `doubleSided` | ✔ | GLTF-025 | Never read. **Accept:** `double-sided` renders both faces; a single-sided fixture culls back faces. **Landed as carried state and now pixel-proven through the deliberate application boundary** (gate §1.4). The flag survives import, the `.cnj` and both PBR effects. Applying it is a `RasterizerState::CullMode` change, which in XNA is per-draw device state the application sets — having `Model::Draw` mutate it as a side effect would surprise every XNA caller. `EasyGL_Gltf_AlphaBlend` now turns the real imported flag into `CullNone` for a Y-mirrored, back-facing triangle; the ordinary glTF `CullClockwise` control removes exactly that triangle first. Both results pass on OPENGLES2 and OPENGLES3, alongside `GLTF-230`'s application-owned blend proof. Thus carried does not mean unused, and application-applied does not mean an implicit global mutation. **Closing-evidence extension:** `1a27463fe`. |
| GLTF-232 | `doubleSided` interacts with negative-scale winding | ✅ | GLTF-231, GLTF-116 | **Accept, at the layer both facts live on:** `GltfMirroring.MirroringAndDoubleSidednessAreCarriedIndependentlyOfEachOther` — the same mesh under a `[-1,1,1]` node with a single-sided and a double-sided material, asserting the placement is mirrored either way and the material's own sidedness is untouched either way. The two interact at *draw* time and must not be conflated at import: a double-sided mirrored primitive is unaffected by the missing winding reversal, a single-sided one shows its back face, and a renderer needs both flags to tell them apart. Marking a mirrored primitive double-sided to hide the problem would silently change what the file asked for — single-sidedness is a modelling decision, not a workaround slot. Like `GLTF-230`'s now-verified blend path, resolving the two carried facts into device state and draw order remains the application's responsibility. |
| GLTF-233 | Metallic-roughness channel semantics | ✅ | GLTF-220 | Shader reads `.g` roughness, `.b` metallic — correct per spec. **Accept:** locked with a three-distinct-value fixture. **Locked at the shader boundary:** the shared texture-slot test uses a pure-green MR texel (`G=1,B=0`) under an analytic head-on BRDF rig, producing the fully rough dielectric bytes. A G/B swap instead selects a near-smooth metal and cannot meet the oracle. Both rigid and identity-skinned programs pass; `GLTF-234` extends the proof across the renderer inventory. |
| GLTF-234 | Channel semantics on every renderer | ✅ | GLTF-233 | **Accept:** the same fixture passes on `OPENGLES3` and `VULKAN`; a swapped G/B would fail. **Complete.** `EveryPbrShaderUsesTheGltfPackedTextureChannels` requires normal RGB with the `*2−1` remap, MR green→roughness, MR blue→metallic and occlusion red in every one of the 15 PBR implementations. Separately stored rigid/skinned fragments are counted independently; LLGL additionally locks its GL source, Vulkan source and generated embedded GL copy. The shared ten-case texture-slot executable passes on both requested backends: OPENGLES3 in 0.32 s and Vulkan in 15.28 s on the local GPU. Its pure-green MR sentinel makes a G/B swap a sharply different near-smooth metallic result; asymmetric occlusion and tilted normal sentinels independently lock the other packed channels. Exact evidence is in `docs/gltf-renderer-pbr-fallbacks.md`. |
| GLTF-235 | BRDF analytic spot-checks | ✔ | GLTF-233 | GGX D, Smith-Schlick-GGX G (`k=(r+1)²/8`), Schlick F, `F0 = mix(0.04, albedo, metallic)` — matches Appendix B. **Accept:** normal-incidence and grazing values within tolerance, not an image diff. **Landed:** `GltfPbrBrdf` evaluates the direct-light equation in dot-product space and pins every named term at normal incidence, plus a symmetric 80-degree grazing case where `H=N` exactly. The grazing geometry value distinguishes direct-light `k=(r+1)²/8` from the tempting IBL variant, and a three-channel half-metal control pins the F0 interpolation rather than only its endpoints. The suite belongs to the renderer-independent L6 rung; existing renderer pixel tests consume the same hand-derived normal-incidence values separately. |
| GLTF-236 | Material data model on the model | ✔ | GLTF-025 | `MeshOut` carried loose fields; `CNAEXT.md` separately sketches an optional engine-layer `PbrMaterial`. **Accept:** a coherent carrier for every §13.1 field on both load paths. **Landed without a second public material object:** internal `MaterialOut` owns the five image slots, sampler states, UV selectors and texture transforms, base colour/metallic/roughness/emissive factors, normal scale, occlusion strength, alpha mode/cutoff, sidedness and source material identity. `MeshOut` owns exactly one; the offline writer copies the same record instead of maintaining a second list. Authored UVs remain L5 vertex bytes while selectors/transforms round-trip as effect state. `ModelMeshPart` carries the applied `PbrEffect`/`SkinnedPbrEffect`, avoiding a second mutable material truth. |
| GLTF-237 | `.cnj` material serialisation | ✔ | GLTF-236, GLTF-129 | **Accept:** every field round-trips; offline and runtime paths agree at L6. **Landed, finding three real losses:** the writer omitted `normalScale` and `occlusionStrength`, and although it wrote `diffuseColor`/`alpha`, the PBR reader never assigned them. All four now round-trip for both PBR effect classes; non-default sampler filter/U/V state is serialised per each of the five slots too. `GLTF-184` additionally added the optional flat 25-number `textureTransforms` field, identity defaults for older documents, exact-length/finite validation, and direct/offline equality of all ten shader rows on rigid and skinned PBR. `SerializesAndReloadsPbrMaterialThroughTheOfflineCnjPath` uses all five maps, every scalar/factor, MASK cutoff, sidedness and a point/clamp/mirror sampler, then compares direct and offline L6 material state. `OfflineAndRuntimePathsHaveIdenticalL6MaterialStateForTheCorpus` sweeps all 14 `mat-*` fixtures with a coverage floor and compares every material-bearing draw at L6. |
| GLTF-238 | Material sharing / de-duplication | ✅ | GLTF-236 | **Accept:** effect count matches the distinct material count — `GltfModelShape.TwoPrimitivesOfOneMaterialShareASingleEffect` and its control, `TwoMaterialsStillGetTwoEffects`. A file states a material once and may use it on any number of primitives; one `Effect` each copied every factor and texture binding per primitive and cost a redundant state change per draw for a material the GPU was already set up for. The cache key is the **material pointer** plus the four flags that decide which effect class and which parameters it gets — two primitives of one material still need separate effects when one is skinned and the other is not, because they are not even the same type. Identity, not contents: two materials with identical factors are still two materials, and a file that *repeats* one is the case worth sharing. The control matters as much as the sharing: a key too coarse would give the second primitive the first material's colour, which is a rendering bug with no error anywhere. |
| GLTF-239 | `DualTextureEffect` occlusion-as-lightmap path | ✔ | GLTF-215 | `RemapOcclusionImageForDualTextureEXT` halves RGB for the `0.5 = neutral` convention. **Accept:** kept and tested, but no longer chosen for a genuine PBR material. **Locked on what is verifiable without a renderer**: the remap produces a decodable PNG, is **not the identity**, and **refuses** undecodable bytes rather than fabricating an occlusion map. The identity check is the load-bearing one — a remap that returned its input satisfies every structural assertion while leaving each unoccluded texel twice as bright as `DualTextureEffect`'s 0.5-is-neutral convention expects, which reads as a lighting setting rather than a conversion that never ran. |
| GLTF-240 | `BasicEffect`/`SkinnedEffect` remain reachable | ✅ | GLTF-215 | **Accept:** the whole selection policy is a table in `docs/gltf-conventions.md` — material model in, effect and stride out, including the two downgrades (`DualTextureEffect` and vertex-coloured PBR) — and reachability is **tested** rather than intended: `mat-unlit` and `skin-unlit` are corpus fixtures on strides 32 and 52 with byte-exact vertex goldens, so a change that made `BasicEffect` unreachable would fail an L5 comparison, not go unnoticed. |
| GLTF-241 | Vertex-coloured PBR | ✔ | GLTF-215 | `usePbr && colored` is currently impossible. **Accept:** either supported (new stride + shader) or reported — not silently downgraded. **Landed as the second outcome: reported.** Supporting it needs a vertex layout holding a Colour *alongside* a Tangent, which no CNA stride has — and the obvious packing (12+12+16+8+4) collides with the existing stride 52, exactly the reinterpretation hazard `VertexDeclarationFidelity` exists to stop — plus a shader variant on every renderer. That is the same blast-radius argument that ruled out colour-space option A. **The fixture found the loss runs deeper than the material:** stride 24 has no Normal slot either, so a coloured primitive's authored NORMAL is discarded too and it cannot be lit *at all*, not merely lit without PBR. `MeshOut` now names both losses (`unsupportedMaterialModelEXT`, `droppedNormalForStrideEXT`) and both loaders log them. `mat-vertex-color-pbr` records the divergence in the corpus ledger as `partially-remediated` with `GLTF-238` remaining — which immediately made the ledger demand an executable test for it, the bookkeeping working as designed on the first non-audit defect it was given. The material's factors do still reach `MeshOut::material` (`GLTF-219`/`GLTF-221`), so the importer understood the material perfectly and the vertex layout is what could not carry it. |
| GLTF-242 | Lighting-default policy for CNA | ✅ | GLTF-215 | **Accept:** the defaults are deliberately unchanged — zero ambient, every light disabled, which is correct XNA behaviour and means a file declaring no light imports perfectly and renders **black**. What would be a bug is being unable to tell that apart from a broken import, so the loader now reports the contributed light count **always**, not only when something was dropped, and says explicitly when the answer is zero. Documented in `docs/gltf-conventions.md` beside the `KHR_lights_punctual` approximation it shares a report with. |
| GLTF-243 | Record the IBL / tone-mapping boundary | ✅ | GLTF-242 | **Accept:** stated once, in `docs/gltf-conventions.md`. CNA has no image-based lighting and no tone mapping, so a CNA render of a glTF sample differs from Khronos's own in overall brightness and in every specular highlight — and that is **not** a conformance failure. Conformance here means the material's parameters reaching the shader correctly (asserted at L6) and the BRDF's terms matching Appendix B (`GLTF-235`); it does not mean matching a renderer that solves a different lighting integral. |
| GLTF-244 | Full material L6/L7 regression over the corpus | ✅ | GLTF-237 | **Complete on two independent full-PBR rasterisers, with a third reduced CPU cross-check.** All 14 generated `mat-*` fixtures compare direct glTF against offline `.cnj` at the L6 effect boundary, and the complete EasyGL and Vulkan 145-asset renderer-owned L7 campaigns cover them in two byte-identical viewer processes with zero delta. SOFTWARE repeats all 14 through its deterministic base-colour CPU fallback but is not counted as full material-map fidelity. The focused `scripts/gltf-l7-vulkan-materials.py` runner remains a fast 14/14 Vulkan gate (23,729 foreground pixels each); the generalized `scripts/gltf-l7-corpus.py` repeats those rows inside both full native campaigns. The focused Vulkan, full Vulkan and full SOFTWARE integrity tests hash-lock their scopes without requiring graphics in an ordinary test. |

---

### Phase 12 — Skinning · owner: `GLTF-SKIN` · **CRITICAL PATH**
*Entry: Phase 5 (transforms) — **waived for the Track A tasks `GLTF-245`, `GLTF-247`, `GLTF-248`,
`GLTF-260`** (§28.1); `GLTF-260` still genuinely needs `GLTF-114`. Exit: every `skin-*` fixture
passes numerically at L4 **and** `GLTF-260` proves no double application.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-245 | **[P0] `BuildSkeleton` must walk the full scene ancestry** | ✔ | GLTF-114 | Parent links are resolved **only within the joint set**; `f9` proved an armature `translation [0,100,0]` is dropped from `bindPoseLocal` while the file's IBM keeps it ⇒ skin transform `translate(0,−100,0)`. `globalTransform(joint)` must include **every** scene ancestor, joint or not, **and every ancestor above `skin.skeleton`** (§15.1.1) — no early stop. The palette this produces is the `paletteIndex` space of §15.1.2 and must not reorder the scene hierarchy. **Accept:** `skin-armature-ancestor` places the vertex at `(1,0,0)`; a test asserts an ancestor transform above `skin.skeleton` still contributes. **Critical path.** **Landed:** `BuildSkeleton` gained a four-argument overload taking the scene graph and the skinned mesh node's world transform. A root joint's full scene ancestry — every ancestor, joint or not, and regardless of `skin.skeleton` — is resolved from `SceneGraphOut`, falling back to `cgltf_node_transform_world` for a joint parented outside the default scene rather than dropping the chain. |
| GLTF-246 | Lock IBM reading | ✔ | GLTF-059 | `ConvertGltfMatrix` + `ScaleTranslation` read IBMs correctly (`f9`). **Accept:** locked at L2. **Locked on the one thing a translation-only reading cannot fake:** glTF writes a matrix column-major, so its translation is the fourth **column** (elements 12–14) while XNA keeps one in its fourth **row**. Reading the array row-major instead lands it in `M14`–`M34`, a *projective* term — the mesh does not merely move, it shears and diverges as the perspective divide picks it up. Asserted at −100 on Y, `D8`'s own fixture scale. |
| GLTF-247 | **[P0] Add the `inverse(meshNodeWorld)` term** | ✔ | GLTF-245 | glTF §3.8: the mesh node's own transform must be cancelled. Absent entirely today. **Accept:** `skin-mesh-node-transform` (mesh node `T=[0,0,50]`) places the vertex at `(1,0,0)`. **Critical path.** **Landed:** The `inverse(globalTransform(meshNode))` term is composed onto the same prefix. Both terms ride on `BoneOut::parentWorldPrefix` / `SkinningData::SkeletonRootPrefix` rather than being folded into the bind pose, so an animated root joint substitutes only its own local transform and cannot undo them. `AnimationPlayer` composes `world(root) = local * prefix`; an empty prefix array reads as all-identity, so a pre-existing skeleton is unaffected. |
| GLTF-248 | **[P0] The whole `skin-*` ladder passes at L4** | ✔ | GLTF-247 | The 13 fixtures in §15.4. **Accept:** every expected world position matches to 1e-6. **Skinning is not complete here** — `GLTF-260` closes the track. **Critical path.** **Landed:** `skin-armature-ancestor` now yields a joint matrix of exactly identity, was `translate(0,−100,0)`. `GltfSkinSpaces` asserts the joint matrix and the resulting skinned vertex through the real loader; D8 is `fixed` in the ledger with the audit's measurement under `priorActual` and its inverted known-defect test deleted. |
| GLTF-249 | Honour `skin.skeleton` **as a root hint, never as a traversal stop** | ✔ | GLTF-245 | Parsed by cgltf, never read. §15.1.1: it names the declared skeleton root — useful for locating and naming the rig — but **must not truncate the ancestry** used for `globalTransform(joint)`. An implementation that walks up only until `skin.skeleton` recreates D8 in a new disguise. **Accept:** `skin.skeleton` is honoured as the declared/semantic root **while** joint global transforms are still derived from the complete scene-node ancestry the glTF skinning equation requires; a fixture in which a transform-bearing ancestor sits **above** `skin.skeleton` still produces the correct world position, and a fixture where `skin.skeleton` differs from the natural common ancestor is covered. No required ancestor transform may be dropped because an ancestor lies above `skin.skeleton` or is not itself a joint. **Landed:** the traversal-stop half was already right — `GLTF-245` made the ancestry walk scene-driven and the code says in as many words that `skin.skeleton` does not truncate it — but nothing proved it, and the **hint half did not exist at all**: `skin->skeleton` was read by nobody, so an application had no way to find the rig root the file declares. It is now recorded on `SkeletonResult` and carried to `SkinningData::SkeletonRootNodeIndexEXT`/`SkeletonRootNameEXT` as a `sceneNodeIndex` (§15.1.2), so it indexes `Model::Bones` like any other node. It is **data, never control flow**, and both header comments say so. `skin-skeleton-hint` makes each half fail separately: `Outer` carries `T(0,100,0)` and is the *parent* of the declared root, so a truncated walk drops it and reproduces D8's exact 100-unit displacement; and the declared root is deliberately **not** the joints' common ancestor (`Mid` is), so substituting one for the other is visible. The manifest states the **wrong** answer by name under `truncatedAtDeclaredRoot`, so the test asserts against both poles rather than merely away from one. |
| GLTF-250 | Missing `inverseBindMatrices` ⇒ identity | ✔ | GLTF-246 | Spec-correct today, untested. **Accept:** `skin-no-ibm` locked. **Locked.** A rig whose joints sit at the origin in bind pose legitimately declares no matrices at all, and the failure mode is not an error but a **zero** matrix — which multiplies every bound vertex to the origin and collapses the whole mesh to a point, `D8`'s symptom exactly. |
| GLTF-251 | Joint matrix formula documented in one place | ✅ | GLTF-247 | **Accept:** §3.7.3.2's equation in both conventions in `docs/gltf-conventions.md`, with the note that XNA's row-vector form reverses the order term for term because the *left* operand applies first. Three load-bearing properties are recorded with it, each of which was a defect when it was missing: `globalTransform(joint)` is the joint's full scene ancestry (`D8`/`GLTF-245`), `inverse(globalTransform(meshNode))` exists to cancel the skinned mesh node's own placement rather than to ignore it (`GLTF-247`), and `skin.skeleton` is a hint rather than a traversal stop (`GLTF-249`). |
| GLTF-252 | Joint palette ordering — **keep it separate from scene-node identity** | ✅ | GLTF-245, GLTF-113 | Breadth-first with an `oldToNew` remap; `LoadsSkinnedAnimatedModelDirectlyFromGltfWithReversedJointOrder` covered it as a palette operation, but there was no actual `sceneNodeIndex ↔ paletteIndex` map and the corpus never made `oldToNew` non-identity. **Accept:** the existing reorder is locked; the scene hierarchy introduced by `GLTF-113` is **not** reordered to match palette order; `JOINTS_0` remapping targets `paletteIndex`; the per-skin mapping is explicit and tested in both directions. **Landed without a public API.** `SkeletonResult` now carries `sceneNodeIndexToPaletteIndex` (one slot per imported scene node, `-1` for non-joints) and `paletteIndexToSceneNodeIndex` (one slot per palette bone, `-1` for a joint outside the default scene), while the pointer map remains available for animation and out-of-scene joints. `skin-parented-joints` now authors `[JointB child, JointA parent]`; the palette becomes `[JointA, JointB]` without reordering `SceneGraphOut`, and all four `JOINTS_0` components — including valid zero-weight padding — are remapped byte-exactly. This exposed an oracle weakness too: L3 and the skin ladder had called the same `joints` field both authored and post-remap, a contradiction hidden because every corpus remap was identity. The manifest now records authored indices under `importPolicy.authoredJoints`, L3/L5 state palette indices, and the ladder independently applies `oldToNew`. `GltfSkinSpaces.SceneNodeAndPaletteMappingsAreExplicitAndBidirectional`, the L3 oracle, the L5 golden and the full skin ladder cover the four required properties. |
| GLTF-253 | `AnimationPlayer` composition order | ✔ | GLTF-252 | `world[i] = local[i] * world[parent]`, `skin[i] = IBM[i] * world[i]` — correct XNA row-vector order. **Accept:** locked. **Locked with a parent that both scales and translates**, so the two orders give 102 and 201 rather than the same number. Any rig whose parents sit at the origin — which is most test rigs and none of the real ones — cannot tell them apart at all. |
| GLTF-254 | `BlendIndices` `uint8` truncation | ✔ | GLTF-093 | Silent above 255 joints. **Accept:** `skin-256-joints` errors or uses a wider index; never wraps. **The 256-joint case is unreachable** — `GLTF-261` refuses any skin past 72 joints in `BuildSkeleton`, so no remapped index can approach 255 and `skin-256-joints` would only re-test `GLTF-261`'s own boundary. A runtime guard now couples the two limits explicitly rather than leaving the `uint8` cast trusting a constant declared far away. **The real residue was something else**, found while checking this: an out-of-range `JOINTS_0` value fell back to joint **0** unconditionally, binding the vertex to the root and dragging it there — the same collapse as `H6`/`H12`, arriving from a malformed file. Now refused, **but only when the influence carries weight**: a zero-weighted slot with a garbage index is the universal exporter padding pattern and contributes nothing to the skin equation, so refusing it would reject real assets to prevent nothing. Two fixtures pin exactly that boundary — `skin-joint-index-out-of-range` (weight 0.4, refused) and `skin-joint-index-padding` (same indices, zero weight, imports). |
| GLTF-255 | `WEIGHTS_0` decode | ✔ | GLTF-094 | **Accept:** locked at L5 for `FLOAT` and normalized integer forms. **Locked with `GLTF-094`**, plus the case where quantisation and `GLTF-256` actually meet: three equal `u8` weights *cannot* sum to 1 (84 each gives 252/255), which is a real exporter outcome. Asserted to be corrected **and counted** — silently correcting it would hide a genuinely broken file behind the same code path, and the recorded deviation is what lets a caller tell 0.988 from 0.6. |
| GLTF-256 | **Weight renormalisation policy** | ✔ | GLTF-255 | Never renormalised. Weights summing to < 1 shrink vertices toward the origin — an independent collapse mechanism (H12). **Accept:** `skin-unnormalized` behaves per a written policy; the report flags non-conforming input. **Decided: renormalise, and report.** Refusing would be useless — a slightly-off sum is what quantised exporters routinely emit — and leaving it is H12: the skin equation is a weighted sum of joint matrices, so weights summing to 0.75 apply 0.75 of the vertex's transform, dragging it three-quarters of the way toward a joint at the origin. **The two exceptions matter as much as the rule**, and `skin-unnormalized` authors all three cases in three vertices so they can fail separately: a sum within `1e-4` of 1 is float error and is left untouched *and uncounted*; a sum of **zero** means the vertex is unweighted, and `0/0` is not a normalisation, so it is counted and left alone rather than assigned to an arbitrary joint. `MeshOut` carries the renormalised count, the zero-weight count and the **worst deviation** — the last because a count alone cannot tell exporter quantisation from a broken file. **L2 holds the authored weights and L3 the renormalised ones**, which is exactly the distinction between the two layers: L2 is what the accessor decodes to, L3 is what the importer understood after its own documented policy ran. |
| GLTF-257 | `JOINTS_1`/`WEIGHTS_1` — support or report | ✔ | GLTF-095 | Silently ignored. **Accept:** >4 influences are either supported or reported; never silently truncated. **Decided: reported, because it cannot be supported** — the same blast-radius argument as the second UV channel (`GLTF-181`) and vertex-coloured PBR (`GLTF-241`). XNA's `BlendIndices`/`BlendWeight` are four-component by definition; carrying eight would need a new stride, a new `VertexDeclaration`, and a skinning shader change in **every** renderer, to serve a case one file in a thousand authors. **The finding that makes truncation survivable is `GLTF-256`'s renormalisation**: without it a vertex whose set-0 weights sum to 0.6 applies three-fifths of its transform and is dragged toward the origin — `H12` arriving by a second road. With it, the retained four are rescaled to 1, so the result is a *coarser* skin, not a collapsed one. `skin-eight-influences` pins both halves: vertex 0 sums to 0.6 as authored and to exactly 1 as imported. |
| GLTF-258 | Influence count reaches the shader | ✔ | GLTF-255 | `WeightsPerVertex` / `uWeightsPerVertex`. **Accept:** L6 capture. **Landed with `GLTF-008`:** asserted as four on every skinned corpus draw by `SkinnedDrawBindsThePaletteAndFourInfluencesPerVertex`. |
| GLTF-259 | Skinning is applied only to skinned primitives | ✔ | GLTF-245 | Gated on `JOINTS_0 && WEIGHTS_0`. **Accept:** locked. **Locked in both directions.** The gate is the *primitive's* own `JOINTS_0`/`WEIGHTS_0`, not the node's `skin`: a file mixing a character with its props would otherwise hand the static parts a bone palette they have no indices for, binding every vertex to joint 0 and dragging the props onto the character's root. Also asserted at the layout level — an unskinned primitive's chosen stride must carry no blend slots at all. |
| GLTF-260 | **[P0] A skinned mesh's node transform must not be applied twice** | ✔ | GLTF-247, GLTF-114, GLTF-248 | Remediating D1–D3 changes the assumptions D8's fix rests on: Phase 5 gives the skinned mesh's node a real `ModelBone` for the first time, and that is precisely the transform glTF requires to be *cancelled* for a skinned mesh (§15.1). Without this task the node-hierarchy work can silently re-apply what `GLTF-247` just cancelled, leaving skinning double-transformed rather than fixed. **Accept, both halves required:** (a) the mesh-space cancellation term `inverse(globalTransform(meshNode))` is present and effective — `skin-mesh-node-transform` (mesh node `T=[0,0,50]`) places the vertex at `(1,0,0)`; and (b) the new real node hierarchy does **not** re-apply that same transform — the identical fixture yields `(1,0,0)`, not `(1,0,50)`, with the node bone present and non-identity, asserted at L4 through **both** loaders. **Critical path — skinning is not complete without it.** **Landed:** New fixture `skin-mesh-node-transform` (mesh node `T=[0,0,50]`, identity joint and IBM) makes the three outcomes distinguishable: no cancellation → identity, cancelled once → `T(0,0,−50)`, cancelled twice → `−100`. Both halves asserted — the cancellation exists and is applied exactly once, **and** the node's bone still exists carrying its transform while the mesh stays parented to the identity root, so the hierarchy cannot silently re-apply it. |
| GLTF-261 | `MaxBones = 72` | ✔ | GLTF-025 | Rigs above 72 joints silently exceed the palette. Raising it changes a real XNA constant and every renderer's uniform array. **Accept:** `skin-73-joints` errors clearly, or the limit is raised deliberately with the cost recorded. **Decided: error clearly, do not raise the limit.** The palette is 72 `mat4`s — a real XNA constant every renderer's uniform array is sized by — so raising it is not a local change. `BuildSkeleton` now refuses an over-limit rig, naming the skin, its joint count and the limit, instead of letting it surface later as `SetBoneTransforms`' generic "exceeds MaxBones", which says nothing about which asset caused it. **Refusing rather than truncating is the whole point:** truncation leaves the joints past the limit at the identity, so every vertex bound to them collapses toward the origin — the audit's H6, and exactly the silent wrongness this campaign removes. `skin-73-joints` sits **one** past the boundary, with every vertex bound to the *last* joint (the one a truncating reader drops), and the test asserts 72 is still accepted, because an off-by-one in the check is as much a failure as no check. Uncovered along the way: the L3 oracle let a refused skeleton throw through its whole sweep, so one malformed fixture took every other fixture's L3 with it; it is total now, as its own design principle already claimed. |
| GLTF-262 | Bind pose must be applied when no clip is playing | ✔ | GLTF-253 | `SkinnedEffect` defaults to 72 identity bones, so an unanimated skinned model renders **unskinned**. **Accept:** loading a skinned model yields a usable bind-pose palette without game-code setup, or the requirement is documented and the viewer satisfies it (`GLTF-425`). **Landed:** the first branch, not the second. An identity palette is not "no skinning" — it means every joint matrix is the identity, so the mesh is posed in joint space and glTF's own `inverse(globalTransform(meshNode))` cancellation (`GLTF-247`) never applies; an unposed skinned model rendered **wrong**, not merely still, and `GLTF-008`'s L6 capture is what turned that from a suspicion into a measurement. Both loaders now call the new `ApplyBindPoseBoneTransformsEXT` once after building the model, which computes the palette exactly the way an application would — an `AnimationPlayer` over the model's own `SkinningData` with no clip started — rather than deriving a second notion of "bind pose" that could disagree with playback at `t = 0`. Any later `SetBoneTransforms` simply overwrites it. Approved through the gate as `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.6, which records why real XNA's sample gets away without this (its content pipeline bakes the mesh into skeleton space, so *there* the bind pose really is the identity palette — glTF's is not). |
| GLTF-263 | Bone palette upload | ✔ | GLTF-262 | `SetBoneTransforms` → `uBones[72]`. **Accept:** L6 capture matches `GetSkinTransforms()`. **Landed with `GLTF-008`:** the test performs the application's own step — `AnimationPlayer::GetSkinTransforms()` into `SkinnedPbrEffect::SetBoneTransforms()` — and then asserts the captured palette equals it entry for entry, in `paletteIndex` order (§15.1.2). A companion test measures the *unpopulated* case, which is XNA's own `MaxBones` identity default rather than an empty palette, and proves the fixture's real palette differs from it — so the main assertion cannot be passing on a coincidence. |
| GLTF-264 | Skinned normals and tangents | ✔ | GLTF-248 | The skinned shader transforms Position, Normal and Tangent by the palette. **Accept:** `skin-nonuniform-joint-scale` normals correct at L4/L7 (inverse-transpose where required). **Landed first on EasyGL and now on all 15 skinned PBR paths.** The original defect was measured before the EasyGL fix: direct `mat3(skinMat) * normal` sent `(0,.6,.8)` through joint `S=[1,2,1]` to `(0,.832050,.554700)` and rendered byte **212**, while the specification-preserving inverse transpose yields `(0,.351123,.936329)` and byte **90**. EasyGL's shared GLSL ES 1.00-compatible cofactor helper serves both stock programs and skinned PBR; `EasyGL_SkinnedEffect_WorldNormal` locks uniform/non-uniform joint scale on the two stock programs (12 analytic cases), and `EasyGL_Pbr_MaterialMaps` independently locks PBR at byte **93** rather than **139**, on OPENGLES2 and OPENGLES3. `GLTF-379` then found the other 14 skinned PBR renderers still using the direct palette 3x3. They now use equivalent determinant-sign-preserving cofactor helpers with the existing near-singular fallback; Metal also transports the missing world normal matrix and OpenGL2 no longer uses raw World. A 15-renderer source inventory locks those paths, and Vulkan independently renders the corrected byte **28** rather than the old direct-transform value of about **66**. Tangents remain directions transformed by the palette 3x3 and are re-orthogonalised against the corrected normal for TBN. This closes the PBR cross-renderer half and EasyGL's two stock paths without claiming the other renderers' stock skinned programs; corpus-wide image capture remains `GLTF-009`. |
| GLTF-265 | Multiple skins in one file | ✅ | GLTF-137 | **Closed on both paths.** The offline tool emits one `.cnj` per skin (`ImportsAllSkinsAsSeparateModels`); direct `Load<Model>` builds one independent `SkeletonResult` and `SkinningData` per group and imports every group's mesh instances. `Model::getSkinsEXTProperty()` exposes the source-ordered skin name, data and exact mesh set; the first data pointer remains on `Model::Tag` for compatibility. Skin identity is part of the skinned-effect cache key, so two skins sharing a material never share one mutable bone palette, and `ApplyBindPoseBoneTransformsEXT` uses the mapping to touch only the selected skin's meshes. The existing test still loads both offline outputs, then on HEADLESS directly loads the same two-skin source, sees two meshes/two named skins/two effects, gives the bind poses distinct translations `3` and `7`, and proves applying either palette cannot overwrite the other. |
| GLTF-266 | World/View/Projection order for skinned draws | ✔ | GLTF-104 | **Accept:** L6 capture. **Landed with `GLTF-008`:** `BoundWorldMatrixMatchesTheExpectedNodeWorld` asserts the bound world equals the manifest's **L4** node world matrix times the application's own, over every single-instance corpus fixture — compared against L4 rather than against a second walk of the bone hierarchy, so it is a conformance test and not a consistency one. `ViewAndProjectionReachEveryDrawUnaltered` pins the other two against a camera that is nothing like the identity in any of its matrices. |
| GLTF-267 | `uNormalMatrix` is `transpose(inverse(world3x3))` | ✔ | GLTF-264 | **Accept:** verified under non-uniform scale at L6. **Landed with `GLTF-008`:** the L6 capture records the normal matrix using the identical cofactor/inverse-determinant expression `EasyGLRenderer::BindDrawParams()` uploads, so the assertion is over the number a renderer actually sends; `NormalMatrixIsTheInverseTransposeOfTheWorldUpper3x3` checks it across the whole corpus against XNA's own `Matrix::Invert` + `Transpose`. The corpus had **no** non-uniform scale at all, so §11.4's planned `xf-scale-nonuniform` was generated to supply the case that can fail: `scale = [2,3,4]` with normals along `(0.6,0.8,0)`, where the world 3x3 and its inverse transpose send the same normal in visibly different directions. A separate test asserts that separation numerically, so the fixture cannot decay into one that merely agrees. |
| GLTF-268 | Skinned + PBR combined path | ✔ | GLTF-264 | Stride 68, `SkinnedPbrEffect`. **Accept:** a skinned PBR fixture correct at L5/L6/L7. **Landed end to end on `skin-nonuniform-joint-scale`.** Its committed L5 oracle proves three exact 68-byte vertices (position, authored normal, generated tangent, UV, weights and indices); the corpus-wide L6 tests prove the selected `SkinnedPbrEffect`, four-influence declaration and automatically-applied non-identity bind palette. Registered `EasyGL_Gltf_SkinnedPbrNonUniformJoint` now supplies the previously missing L7 half through the real runtime chain — `ContentManager::Load<Model>` → stride-68 part → bind-pose `S=[1,2,1]` → `Model::Draw` → framebuffer. It checks the effect type and palette rather than assuming setup succeeded, then obtains analytic byte **93** twice; dropping skinning or reverting GLTF-264's direct normal matrix produces different values. The same test passes on OPENGLES2 and OPENGLES3. This closes the combined path for the reference renderer without claiming `GLTF-009`'s corpus-wide PNG matrix. |
| GLTF-269 | Skinning + morph interaction | ✅ | GLTF-278 | Morph writes into the same buffer the skinning shader reads. **Accept:** `GltfSkinComposition.AMorphedSkinnedPrimitiveKeepsBothItsSkinAndItsBlendedPositions` — a one-joint skinned triangle with a morph target and a mesh-level default weight of 1, so the imported rest pose is genuinely morphed. Three things are asserted: both features survive import (neither drops the other), the default weight really is the mesh's own (`GLTF-281`), and the blend writes at the **skinned** layout's offsets — the blended buffer's `BlendWeight` is intact while its position moved. That last one is the failure a naive blend would produce and hide: Position sits at offset 0 in every stride, so a blend assuming an unskinned layout gets the position right and corrupts the fields after it. |
| GLTF-270 | Skinning + node transforms | ✅ | GLTF-260 | **Accept:** the new `skin-mesh-node-parent-transform` fixture — a skinned mesh node translated `[0,0,50]` under a parent translated `[30,0,0]` — asserted at L4 by the existing skin-space suite. `skin-mesh-node-transform` proved the cancellation uses the mesh node's transform; this proves it uses the node's **composed** one, ancestors included, which is what §3.7.3 means by "the joints place it". An implementation cancelling only the node's *local* transform passes the earlier fixture exactly and misses by the parent's 30 units here — and misses in the direction that looks like a rigging problem, because the mesh lands somewhere plausible and the skeleton does not. CNA was already correct; the fixture is what stops that being accidental. |
| GLTF-271 | Skinning + Draco | ✅ | GLTF-353 | **Accept:** `draco-skinned` matches its uncompressed twin at L3. **Exact, not merely near:** the generated asset places compressed and ordinary primitives beside one another with the same two-joint skin. `GltfDracoParity.SkinJointsWeightsAndPbrStreamsMatchTheirOrdinaryTwinExactly` requires identical 68-byte PBR vertices, decoded positions/normals/authored tangents/UVs, remapped joints, repaired weights and face connectivity. L4 and L5 then independently lock the bind-pose geometry and packed bytes. **Closing commit:** `d4b0fe44d`. |
| GLTF-272 | Skeleton `.cnj` sidecar round-trip | ✅ | GLTF-248 | **Accept:** `GltfToCnjToolTest.TheOfflineAndRuntimePathsProduceIdenticalSkinningDataForEverySkinFixture` — swept over **every** `skin-*` fixture, not one, because the ways the two paths can diverge are per-shape (an armature above the joints, a transformed mesh node, a declared root, 73 joints, unnormalised weights, eight influences). Compared field for field — bone tree, bind pose, inverse bind pose and `SkeletonRootPrefix`, where `GLTF-245`/`GLTF-247`'s own terms live — so a divergence names the bone and the matrix element rather than "the skeletons differ". The sweep also requires the two to agree on **refusal**: a file one loader rejects and the other imports is a file whose validity depends on which loader you used. |
| GLTF-273 | Skinning import report | ✅ | GLTF-034 | **Accept:** `SkinReportEXT`/`BuildSkinReportEXT` — joint count, dropped influence sets *and the worst single influence in them*, renormalised vertex count *and the worst deviation*, and whether a skeleton root was declared. Internal, for the reason `GLTF-145` records. Each field is a place a rig is imported approximately and each is silent alone: a fifth influence weighted 0.002 is exporter noise and one weighted 0.4 is a visibly different pose, and only the number tells them apart. Tested against the corpus fixture that **isolates** each approximation (`skin-eight-influences`, `skin-unnormalized`, `skin-skeleton-hint`) rather than one document trying to be all three, plus a control asserting an unskinned primitive reports zero rather than the previous skin's numbers. |
| GLTF-274 | Full skinning L4 regression | ✅ | GLTF-248 | **Accept:** every `skin-*` fixture is green on both loaders — at L4 through `GltfConformanceL4` (world positions against the spec-derived manifest, over the whole corpus) and across the loader boundary through `GLTF-272`'s sweep, which compares the `SkinningData` both paths produce for each of them. The fixture set grew to 15 with `skin-unlit` and `skin-vertex-color` (`GLTF-149`), and the sweep is directory-driven rather than a fixed list, so a fixture added later is covered without editing the test. |

---

### Phase 13 — Morph targets · owner: `GLTF-MORPH`
*Entry: Phase 12. Exit: deltas apply on every stride that has the slot; both weight sources honoured.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-275 | POSITION delta extraction and blending | ✔ | GLTF-101 | Extracted, unit-scaled, blended. **Accept:** `morph-position-single`, `morph-position-two-targets` exact at L4. **Locked on accumulation, which is the failure that still produces a mesh.** Two targets pulling on one vertex must both contribute; a blend that assigns rather than accumulates gives the last target's delta alone, so on a face rig one expression silently cancels every other and the result still looks like a face. A zero-weighted target is additionally asserted **byte-identical** to the base, not merely close — otherwise a fifty-target rig accumulates float error while nothing is animating. |
| GLTF-276 | Targets without a POSITION delta | ✔ | GLTF-275 | Zero-filled. **Accept:** `morph-target-without-position` locked. **Locked.** §3.7.2.2 lets a target carry any subset of POSITION/NORMAL/TANGENT, and a normals-only target is a legitimate way to author a shading variation. The absent stream must read as zero rather than as whatever lies past the end of a shorter array. |
| GLTF-277 | NORMAL delta extraction | ✔ | GLTF-275 | Extracted, **not** unit-scaled (correct — deltas of a unit vector). **Accept:** locked at L3. **Locked with `GLTF-283`.** |
| GLTF-278 | **NORMAL deltas must apply on strides 48 and 68** | ✔ | GLTF-277 | `BlendMorphTargetsEXT`'s `hasNormalSlot = (stride==32 \|\| 52 \|\| 56)` excludes 48 and 68, yet both carry Normal at offset 12. Every **PBR** morph target keeps stale normals. **Accept:** `morph-normal-delta-pbr` correct at L4/L7. **Landed:** the literal list was written when 32/52/56 were the only strides a mesh with normals could have; `GLTF-215` changed which effect a metallic-roughness material selects and with it the strides an ordinary glTF mesh gets, and the list did not follow — so every PBR morph target's positions moved while its normals stayed put. Restating an ABI is what allowed the drift, so the predicate is now a **query against the canonical stride table** (`CNA::Internal::Graphics::InferredLayoutForStride`) that the renderers themselves dispatch on, and the write offset comes from the same answer instead of a hardcoded 12. Tests sweep every entry of that table: the five strides with a Normal slot must all apply a delta identically, and the two without one (20/24) must leave every byte past the position untouched — a blend that "helpfully" wrote a normal at offset 12 there would land on a TextureCoordinate or a Color. Both tests were confirmed to fail against the old rule before the fix was kept. The dedicated `morph-normal-delta-pbr` fixture is still owed by `GLTF-277`'s group; the stride sweep is what makes the defect impossible to reintroduce in the meantime. |
| GLTF-279 | TANGENT deltas | ✔ | GLTF-278 | Never extracted, though strides 48/68 have a tangent slot at offset 24. **Accept:** `morph-tangent-delta` correct, `.w` preserved. **Landed.** The old scope cut predated `PbrEffect`'s real tangent-space normal mapping, so a morphed PBR surface kept its **rest-pose tangent basis** while its positions and normals moved — normal mapping then lit the deformed surface with the undeformed basis. Deltas are stored as `Vector3`, not `Vector4`, and that is the whole design: glTF morphs the tangent **direction** only, because `w` is the UV winding's handedness and interpolating it would pass through 0, which is not a handedness at all. Storing three components makes preserving `w` structural rather than a rule someone has to remember, and a test asserts it survives in **both** signs and at a half weight. The tangent offset comes from the same canonical stride table `GLTF-278` moved the normal offset to, so a stride with no tangent slot is left entirely untouched — asserted, because writing a tangent at offset 24 of a stride-32 vertex would land on its TextureCoordinate. |
| GLTF-280 | `mesh.weights` as the default pose | ✔ | GLTF-275 | Applied at import when non-zero. **Accept:** `morph-mesh-weights-nonzero` locked. **Locked.** Ignoring `mesh.weights` imports every morphing mesh in its neutral shape, so a model authored permanently in one expression — a common way to ship a variant — arrives as the base mesh instead. |
| GLTF-281 | `node.weights` overrides `mesh.weights` | ✔ | GLTF-280 | Never read. **Accept:** `morph-node-weights-override` correct. **Landed.** §3.7.2.2 gives the instancing node the final say, and **overrides** is the operative word: a node declaring `[1,0]` for a mesh whose own weights are `[0,1]` must start at `[1,0]`, not `[1,1]`, and a node array shorter than the target count leaves the remainder at **zero** rather than at the mesh's value. `node.weights` was read by nobody, so a mesh instanced by several nodes wore every node's expression at once. The fixture (`morph-node-weights-override`) is still owed by `GLTF-280`'s group, which has no morph assets in the corpus yet; the override rule itself is exercised by the runtime loader passing each instance's own node. |
| GLTF-282 | A mesh instanced by two nodes with different weights | ✔ | GLTF-281, GLTF-136 | `ExtractMorphWeightTrack` takes the **first** node found; `MorphTargetDataEXT` is per-part, so two instances cannot differ. **Accept:** supported after Phase 6, or reported. **Landed as supported, and the precondition turned out to already hold.** `MorphTargetDataEXT` is per **part**, so two instances can only differ if the importer builds a part per instance — and it does: the runtime loader extracts each mesh instance separately, which `GLTF-137` made true for every group rather than just the first. With `GLTF-281` passing each instance's own node, the two differ by construction. `morph-node-weights-override` proves it: one mesh with `weights: [0.5]`, instanced by nodes declaring `[1]` and `[0]`, must produce 1 and 0. The test names all three ways it could go wrong — both instances the same (node weights ignored), an instance keeping the mesh's 0.5 (not overridden), or a weight above 1 (merged rather than replaced). |
| GLTF-283 | Blended-normal renormalisation | ✔ | GLTF-278 | Performed. **Accept:** locked. **Locked with the number that says why it matters:** +Z plus +X has length **√2**, and a 1.41-length normal brightens every surface it touches by 41%. Without renormalisation a morphed mesh lights differently from an unmorphed one, which reads as a lighting bug rather than a morph one. `GLTF-279`'s tangent rule is asserted alongside it — the handedness `w` is a sign decided by the UV winding, so only xyz is blended. |
| GLTF-284 | Weight-vector length validation | ✔ | GLTF-275 | `BlendMorphTargetsEXT` throws on a mismatch. **Accept:** locked with the exact message. **Locked with the message and with a control.** A mismatched weight vector is a *caller* bug rather than a file one, which is exactly why tolerating it silently is worse than failing: ignoring extra weights or zero-padding missing ones yields a plausible mesh from an incoherent call. Three wrong lengths are asserted to throw naming both counts, and a correct one to succeed — without that control, a guard that rejected every call would satisfy all three. |
| GLTF-285 | Record the CPU-morphing design decision | ✅ | GLTF-278 | **Accept:** documented in `docs/gltf-conventions.md` with its cost, not only its rationale — one full vertex-buffer upload per weight change per morphed primitive, scaling with the mesh rather than with the target count, which for a face rig driven every frame is the dominant cost of the feature.
