It buys more than portability across 46 renderers: the blended buffer is what *everything* sees, so a CPU-side bounds computation, a picking ray and a `.cnj` re-export all get the morphed mesh, where a GPU blend would leave them reading the rest pose. GPU morphing is explicitly **GLTF ROBUST** — the rendered result is identical, only the path differs. |
| GLTF-286 | Morph + skin | ✅ | GLTF-269 | **Accept:** the same test — the two rows are the same interaction seen from the morph side and the skin side, and one assertion covers both: the blended buffer keeps its skinning fields and carries its morphed positions. |
| GLTF-287 | Morph + node transforms | ✅ | GLTF-114 | **Accept:** asserted — `GltfMorphBlending.DeltasAreMeshLocalAndTheNodeTransformIsNotBakedIntoThem`. §3.7.2.2 makes a delta a displacement in the mesh's own space, and CNA's architecture agrees by construction (`GLTF-103` Option A: positions stay mesh-local, placement travels as a bone transform). The test guards the change that would break it — a future "helpful" pre-transform of either stream — by asserting the delta, the base position it applies to, *and* that the node's +100 translation is in the world transform where it belongs. Baked in, the model would move 100 units per unit of morph weight, which reads as an animation bug rather than a space error. |
| GLTF-288 | Morph + Draco | ✅ | GLTF-353 | **Accept:** `draco-morph` matches its uncompressed twin. **The base and every delta stream match exactly.** The compressed/ordinary pair produces byte-identical stride-48 base vertices and indices, then `MorphBaseAndEveryDeltaStreamMatchTheirOrdinaryTwinExactly` compares POSITION, NORMAL and TANGENT deltas vertex by vertex. L4/L5 cover the default morphed pose and final packed buffers, so a decoder that preserves the base but renumbers morph correspondence cannot pass. **Closing commit:** `d4b0fe44d`. |
| GLTF-289 | Morph sidecar `.cnj` round-trip | ✅ | GLTF-275 | `BuildMorphBytes` + the `.cnj` reader exist and are tested. **Accept:** extended to tangent deltas. **Landed with backward compatibility rather than a silent format fork.** The CNB-82 position/normal prefix stays unchanged, and an optional magic/versioned `MTAN` trailer carries each target's tangent xyz deltas; an older reader consumes the prefix and ignores the tail, while the new reader still accepts an old sidecar with no trailer. `TANGENT.w` is not duplicated — it remains in the base vertex bytes and morphing never changes handedness. The same work found two more offline-only defects. A legal target with no `POSITION` wrote `vertexCount = 0` and then wrote its NORMAL values anyway, so the reader consumed none of them and the trailing floats corrupted the rest of the sidecar; it now writes a zero-filled legacy position stream, preserving its functional meaning for old readers and keeping the following semantic structurally readable. And the converter called `GetMeshDefaultWeights` without the instancing node, so `node.weights` never overrode `mesh.weights`: two instances authored at weights 1 and 0 both round-tripped at the mesh default 0.5. Both default and material-variant writer paths now pass `instance.node`. `GltfToCnjToolTest.TangentAndNormalOnlyMorphTargetsRoundTripThroughTheOfflinePath` pins the 84-byte legacy prefix, the trailer magic, clean rejection of an unknown trailer version, and exact direct/offline default blended bytes over **all 13 `morph-*` fixtures**, not just the witness that motivated the fix. |
| GLTF-290 | Large target counts | ✅ | GLTF-275 | **Accept:** the glTF path now refuses above the same `100000` the `.cnj` reader has applied since CNB-83. Both loaders load the same content, so a bound that applies to one and not the other is not a limit — it is a note about which loader you happened to use, and the unbounded path allocated three delta arrays per target from a number the file chose. |
| GLTF-291 | Morph import report | ✅ | GLTF-034 | **Accept:** `MorphReportEXT`/`BuildMorphReportEXT` — target count, how many targets carry no position, normal or tangent deltas, and whether the applied default weights are non-zero — logged by the loader and copied into the public `GltfImportReportEXT`. A missing delta kind is legal (§3.7.2.2) and simply does not move that stream, which is exactly why it is worth counting: a normal-mapped surface whose targets carry positions and **no tangents** deforms with its rest-pose basis and lights wrongly, so that specific combination also gets a named warning. A non-zero default weight is reported too — a file whose rest pose is already morphed (`GLTF-281`) is legitimate and surprising to debug unaided. |
| GLTF-292 | Full morph L4 regression | ✔ | GLTF-278 | **Accept:** all 12 `morph-*` fixtures green on both loaders. **There were 1. There are now 13,** and building the other twelve found a diagnostic that could never fire. Each fixture differs in exactly one way a plausible implementation gets wrong: one target or eight (with interleaved zero weights, so an implementation iterating targets rather than weights lands elsewhere), weights of **1.75 and −0.5** (§3.7.2.2 does not bound them, and a reader clamping to `[0,1]` silently renders a pose the file did not ask for), a target with **no `POSITION`**, `mesh.weights` with no `node.weights` versus a node saying **explicitly zero** over a mesh saying one (absent is not zero — the pair is what separates the two readings), per-vertex distinct deltas, a base with **no authored `NORMAL`** so the blend runs against a basis `GLTF-173` computed, and all three delta semantics at once for `GLTF-289`'s residue. The base normal is tilted `(0.6,0,0.8)` throughout, because a normal delta added to a normal that is already the answer proves nothing. `EveryMorphFixtureBlendsToTheWeightsAndPoseItsManifestStates` compares against a blended pose the **generator** computed from §3.7.2.2's own equation, so it is two independent derivations rather than one against itself. **The find:** `morphPositionDeltas[ti].resize(vertexCount)` ran *unconditionally*, so a target authoring no `POSITION` arrived zero-filled and indistinguishable from one authoring zeros — which made `MorphReportEXT::targetsWithoutPositions` **structurally unable to report**, and made the blend add a zero vector per vertex per such target. Normals and tangents already used emptiness to mean "contributes nothing"; positions do now too, and `BlendMorphTargetsEXT` guards the read that used to be safe only by accident. One rule instead of two. |

---

### Phase 14 — Animation · owner: `GLTF-ANIMATION`
*Entry: Phase 13. Exit: every path × interpolation combination is exact at keyframes and midpoints.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-293 | **Import animation of non-joint (rigid) nodes** | ✔ | GLTF-114 | `ExtractClips` skips any channel whose target is not in `skel.nodeToNewIndex`, and the offline tool calls it only when `hasSkin`. `f7` produced a `.cnj` with **no** `animations` key and no warning. **Accept:** `anim-rigid-node` produces a playable clip targeting the mesh node's bone. **Landed:** `ExtractSceneNodeClips` resolves channels against `SceneGraphOut` instead of a joint set, so `anim-rigid-node` yields one clip `Spin` with one track on the `SpinningMesh` node's own bone — identity at `t=0`, a quarter turn about +Z at `t=1`, scale filled from the node's bind pose rather than zero. Both original mechanisms are gone: extraction is no longer gated on a skin, and a non-joint target resolves instead of being skipped. `ClipOut` gained `targetSpace` (`ClipTargetSpace::JointPalette` \| `SceneNode`) so §15.1.2's two index spaces can never be silently interchanged; it defaults to `JointPalette`, leaving every existing consumer's meaning unchanged. Channel gathering and keyframe resampling are now one shared code path for both extractors — the two differing only in how a target node resolves — since D6 existed precisely because they were never separated. **Scope boundary:** the clip is **not** serialised to the `.cnj` yet. A scene-node track's `boneIndex` is a `sceneNodeIndex`, and the clip schema has no field distinguishing it from a palette slot; writing one would let a reader apply it as a palette index — a fresh silent corruption in place of the old one. `GLTF-294` adds the field and the unified playback path, so the converter **reports** the clip by name and track count instead of dropping it. **D6 → `partially-remediated`.** Its ledger entry also named the wrong owner (`GLTF-284`, the morph weight-vector validation task); corrected to `GLTF-293`/`GLTF-294`. |
| GLTF-294 | Unify joint and node animation on the bone hierarchy | ✔ | GLTF-293, GLTF-103 | With a real `ModelBone` tree, both are bone tracks. **Accept:** one code path; `.cnj` carries clips for both. **Half done by `GLTF-293`:** channel gathering and resampling are already one shared path, and `ClipOut::targetSpace` names the space each clip is in. What is left is the serialisation and playback half — a `targetSpace` field in the `.cnj` clip schema, a reader that honours it, and a way to drive `ModelBone` transforms from a `SceneNode` clip. **This is what keeps D6 open.** **Landed:** one writer serialises both clip kinds — they differ only by a `"targetSpace"` field in the file, not by a second serialiser, since keeping two would let them drift. `AnimationClipEXT` gained `TargetSpace`, a rigid clip reaches an unskinned model's `Tag` as `ModelAnimationsEXT`, and `ApplyClipToBonesEXT` poses `Model::Bones` from it — **refusing** a joint-palette clip outright, because applying palette indices to bones would pose the wrong ones with no symptom but wrong motion. **D6 is `fixed`.** **Boundary recorded, not resolved:** `Model::Tag` holds one object and a skinned model already uses it for `SkinningData`, so a file with **both** a skin and rigid node animation has nowhere to put the rigid clips; the importer reports that by name and `GLTF-295` owns it. |
| GLTF-295 | Call `ExtractClips` unconditionally | ✔ | GLTF-293 | `gltf_to_cnj` gates it on `hasSkin`. **Accept:** a skinless animated file produces clips. **Landed with `GLTF-293`/`GLTF-294`:** `ExtractSceneNodeClips` runs unconditionally on both paths and `anim-rigid-node` — a file with no skin at all — produces and round-trips its clip, which is exactly the stated acceptance. The tool's own comment had gone stale in the meantime, still saying the clip was extracted but not serialised; `GLTF-294` added the `targetSpace` field that made serialising it safe, and the comment now says so. The **separate** collision this task's own body describes — a file with *both* a skin and rigid node animation, where `Model::Tag` has room for only one object — is unchanged and still reported by name rather than dropped; it is tracked as a named limitation in `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.5, not as part of this acceptance. |
| GLTF-296 | Animation of camera and light nodes | ✔ | GLTF-294 | Same non-joint drop. **Accept:** imported or reported. **Landed as imported, and it needed no camera-specific path at all** — §3.11 animates *nodes*, and a camera node is an ordinary node, so `GLTF-293`'s `ExtractSceneNodeClips` already resolves it against the scene graph. `camera-animated-node` proves that rather than assuming it, and the clip's track is asserted to drive the **camera's own** bone rather than merely some bone. **The consequence worth pinning:** `ModelCameraEXT::WorldTransform` is a snapshot taken at import, so posing the model moves the camera's *bone* and not that matrix. A consumer reading the stored one every frame would render an animated camera as a stationary one. The field now says so, and a test asserts both halves — the live transform follows the bone, and the stored one deliberately does not — with a guard that the two actually differ at `t = 1`, so the test cannot pass by their coinciding. Lights are the same shape and the same code path; they have no fixture yet because the corpus has no `lights-punctual-*` asset (`GLTF-326`'s group). |
| GLTF-297 | Quantify the union-time resampling error | ✅ | GLTF-294 | Bone channels are resampled onto the union of their own three channels' times, baking a piecewise-linear approximation of CUBICSPLINE between keys. **Accept:** the error is measured on a fixture and either accepted with numbers or replaced by lazy evaluation (as the morph path already does). **Measured and accepted as the current compatibility approximation.** `anim-cubicspline` is a zero-tangent 0→10 smoothstep with union keys at 0, .5 and 1; those three baked values replay as the line `10t`. At `t=.25`, Hermite is 1.5625 and runtime is 2.5: 0.9375 units, or **9.375% of the value span**. Its analytic maximum is `10sqrt(3)/18 = 0.962250449`, or **9.622504%**, at `(3-sqrt(3))/6` and its reflection. `GltfRigidAnimation.CubicSplineUnionBakeHasAMeasuredPlaybackError` exercises the real runtime loader and `ApplyClipToBonesEXT`, reads the independently generated manifest numbers, and locks both measurements. The fixture, conventions and limitations state the essential caveat: this is not a universal bound, because unconstrained glTF tangents may overshoot the endpoint span. Exact preservation would require carrying interpolation/tangents through an additive `AnimationClipEXT`/`.cnj` schema and evaluating lazily; meanwhile `resampledTrackCount` makes the accepted loss observable. |
| GLTF-298 | Rotation interpolation | ✔ | GLTF-294 | `Slerp` for LINEAR; component-wise Hermite + renormalisation for CUBICSPLINE. **Accept:** `anim-rotation-slerp` exact at the midpoint, and a negated-quaternion twin yields the same pose (shortest path). **Locked on the property that distinguishes `Slerp` from a component-wise lerp: unit length at every resampled time.** The two samples are 180° apart about Y, where a lerp is worst — its midpoint has length cos 45° = 0.707, a 29% shrink — and a non-unit quaternion applied as a matrix *scales as well as rotates*, so the failure is a limb that shrinks mid-swing and returns, which reads as a rigging problem. |
| GLTF-299 | Clip duration when the first key is > 0 | ✔ | GLTF-294 | `maxTime` only tracks the last input sample. **Accept:** `anim-nonzero-start` has the correct duration and start behaviour. **Landed — and the answer is that both behaviours were already right, which nothing proved.** glTF animations live on an absolute timeline anchored at 0, so each question has exactly one spec-conformant answer: the duration is the **last** input sample, not the span between the first and last keys, and the pose below the first key is that key's, held (Appendix C's clamping) — not the node's rest pose. `anim-nonzero-start` is the first fixture that can tell those apart: its first key is at `t = 1.5` and its last at `3.0`, so "last sample" and "key span" are different numbers, and its node's rest rotation differs from its first key, so "holds the first key" and "falls back to the rest pose" are different poses. Both rules are stated in the fixture's own manifest, with the reason, so the decision lives with the data rather than in a test's expectations. A duration that collapsed to the key span would shift every authored time by the offset — a whole-clip error no single pose comparison would localise, which is why it gets its own assertion. |
| GLTF-300 | LINEAR translation/scale | ✔ | GLTF-294 | **Accept:** `anim-linear-translation` yields `[5,0,0]` at `t=1`. **Locked at the endpoints**, which is what an interpolation cannot get wrong by rounding: at an authored time the sampler must return that sample verbatim, not a blend of its neighbours. |
| GLTF-301 | STEP interpolation | ✔ | GLTF-294 | Existing test covers a foreign resample time. **Accept:** `anim-step-translation` locked at `t=1.999` and `t=2`. **Writing the lock found a real defect, and a badly-placed one.** §3.6's intervals are half-open — `times[i]`'s value holds on `[times[i], times[i+1])` — so at exactly `times[i+1]` the next sample is already in force. `FindBracket` reports that moment as `lo = i, amount = 1`, which is right for LINEAR (`Lerp(a,b,1)` is `b`) and was wrong for STEP, whose branch ignored `amount` entirely. **This is not an edge case**: `ExtractClips` resamples onto the union of a bone's own channel times, so *every interior keyframe of a STEP channel lands exactly on a sample boundary*. A three-sample STEP channel played **0, 0, 20 instead of 0, 10, 20** — the last value correct only because the end clamp takes a different path, which is what made it look plausible. Fixed for translation/scale and rotation via a shared `StepSampleIndex`, and in `EvaluateMorphWeightsEXT`, which had the identical shape. |
| GLTF-302 | CUBICSPLINE tangent packing and `Δt` scaling | ✔ | GLTF-294 | `HermiteEvaluate` uses the spec's interval scaling; `EvaluatesCubicSplineWithRealHermiteBasis` exists. **Accept:** `anim-cubic-translation` matches an independently computed manifest to 1e-6. **Locked on the packing, which is the half that can be misread silently.** §3.6 packs a CUBICSPLINE output as (inTangent, value, outTangent) per sample — three times as many elements as a LINEAR channel with the same times — so reading it as a flat value array takes the *in-tangent* of each triplet: an animation driven by slopes instead of positions, which still moves and still loops. The fixture's in-tangents are huge and its values small, so the two readings cannot be confused. |
| GLTF-303 | Multi-channel single node with disjoint key times | ✅ | GLTF-297 | **Accept:** `GltfAnimationSampling.DisjointChannelTimesResampleOntoTheirUnionWithEveryComponentFilled` — translation authored at t = 0 and 2, rotation at t = 1 and 3, so every keyframe of the union carries at least one component neither channel authored there. t = 1 is the case that separates the three plausible implementations: translation there must be **10**, the interpolated value, which is neither 0 (the nearest earlier sample, or a default) nor 20 (the nearest later one). The rotation at t = 2 is checked the same way, by transforming a vector rather than by comparing quaternion components, and the clip's duration is the union's end rather than either channel's. |
| GLTF-304 | Bind pose as the fallback for an unanimated component | ✔ | GLTF-294 | `EvaluateVec3Channel` falls back to the decomposed bind pose. **Accept:** locked. **Locked with a joint carrying a non-default bind scale**, so bind-pose fallback and default-construction give visibly different answers. glTF drives each component independently, so a clip animating only translation says nothing about rotation and scale — and every keyframe still carries a full TRS, whose other two slots must hold the node's own bind pose, not the zero and identity a default-constructed keyframe holds. A scale falling silently to zero collapses the bone and everything under it to a point. |
| GLTF-305 | Multiple animations per file | ✔ | GLTF-294 | One `ClipOut` each; one `.cnj` per clip. **Accept:** locked. **Locked on names and durations.** A file with "Idle" and "Walk" is the normal case; merging them gives one clip whose second half is another animation, which still plays and looks like a badly authored loop. Names must survive because an application selects a clip by name. |
| GLTF-306 | Clip naming and lookup | ✅ | GLTF-305 | Unnamed animations become `Clip<index>` — the only identifier the file itself provides. **Accept:** deterministic, asserted across two extractions of the same document, with a control proving an authored name passes through verbatim (a generator that renamed everything uniformly would satisfy the first test alone). It matters because `AnimationPlayer` and every `.cnj` consumer look a clip up **by name**: a name that varied between runs would make a saved game referencing `Clip1` find a different animation after a reload. |
| GLTF-307 | Playback clamping and looping | ✔ | GLTF-305 | `AnimationPlayer` clamps or floor-mods on ticks. **Accept:** locked at `t>end` and across a loop boundary. **Locked on both branches and on the two inputs that break the naive versions of each.** Without looping, the position must *stop* at the duration — running past is not a crash, because the bracket search clamps to the last keyframe, so the pose looks right while any code comparing position against duration to detect the end never fires. With looping, a **negative** time must wrap forward: C++'s `%` follows the sign of the dividend, so a plain `pos % duration` returns a negative position that indexes a track backwards, and playing an animation in reverse is an ordinary request. A zero-duration clip — a single-keyframe pose exported as an animation — is asserted to hold at zero rather than divide by it. Exactly-on-a-period is asserted too: it must wrap to 0, or a looping clip freezes for one frame every period. |
| GLTF-308 | Loop-boundary continuity | ✅ | GLTF-307 | **Accept:** `AnimationPlayerClampAndLoop.ALoopingClipShowsTheSamePoseAtItsEndAsAtItsStart` — a track authored to return to its start pose, sampled at `t = 0` and at `t = Duration` **through the wrap** rather than by clamping. The failure it catches is a wrap written as `position > duration` instead of `>=`: one frame of the end pose leaks through per cycle, which at 60 Hz reads as a twitch rather than as an off-by-one. The midpoint is asserted too, so the agreement at the boundary cannot be the agreement of a player returning one pose for every time. |
| GLTF-309 | Time domain and units | ✔ | GLTF-294 | Seconds throughout, `System::TimeSpan` at the boundary. **Accept:** locked. **Asserted with `GLTF-300`**: the clip's duration is the last sample time in *seconds*. A millisecond or tick domain would put it three orders of magnitude out, which reads as an animation that never plays rather than as a unit error. |
| GLTF-310 | Animation targeting a node not in the default scene | ✅ | GLTF-133 | **Accept:** ignored and reported. **Owned by `anim-out-of-scene-target`**, whose animation has two channels: one on a node in the default scene, one on a node only the second scene contains. `animations` is a top-level array scoped to nothing, so a channel may name any node in the file, and CNA imports the default scene alone (`GLTF-133`) — such a channel drives a bone that does not exist. Ignoring it is the only available answer, so the whole task is the report: `AnimationReportEXT::skippedOutOfSceneChannels` counts it and the warning names the clip. The second channel is what makes the outcomes separable — one track survives, so "a channel was skipped" cannot be read as "the animation was dropped" or "the file was rejected", and the surviving track is asserted to land on the in-scene node's own bone rather than merely to exist (an unresolved target degrading to index 0 would also produce one track). |
| GLTF-311 | Empty / single-keyframe channels | ✔ | GLTF-294 | Handled by `FindBracket` and the size-1 paths. **Accept:** locked. **Locked.** One sample is a legal channel meaning "hold this value" — a common way to author a static offset alongside animated siblings — and every bracket-finding routine has an off-by-one waiting in it for a one-element array: reading `keys[i + 1]` walks off the end, and returning nothing drops the pose entirely. |
| GLTF-312 | `unitScale` applies to translation channels and tangents | ✔ | GLTF-121 | Already applied to both. **Accept:** locked. **Locked in both directions in one fixture** — a clip animating translation *and* rotation, imported at `unitScale` 0.01. The translation must scale (or a centimetre-authored asset stays a hundred times too large) and the rotation must not (or the quaternion denormalises, giving the same shrink as `GLTF-298`'s, arriving from a unit conversion). |
| GLTF-313 | Sampler input must be strictly increasing | ✅ | GLTF-294 | `FindBracket` assumes sorted input. **Accept:** unsorted input is rejected or sorted, deterministically. **Split by how the rule is broken, because the two cases are not equally harmful.** A **decreasing** step is refused with a named error (`bad-animation-input-order`): the curve doubles back, so a time inside the reversed span has two authored values and `FindBracket` returns whichever it meets first. Sorting instead would silently re-pair each time with a value the exporter did not write — a broken file turned into a plausible-looking wrong animation, which is worse than a named failure. **Equal** adjacent times are tolerated and counted (`anim-repeated-time`): they are what an exporter emits for a hard cut, the zero-length span already yields the earlier sample so nothing reads out of range, and refusing them would reject assets that play correctly. The cost of tolerance is asserted rather than glossed — a track holds one keyframe per time, so the value in force *at* the cut survives and the post-cut value is not representable. `cgltf_validate` has no rule about input ordering, so this is CNA's own check; the extraction probe in `GltfContainerValidation` had to learn to run clip extraction at all, the third instance of the blind spot `GLTF-261` and `GLTF-254` each closed one layer higher. |
| GLTF-314 | Animation `.cnj` round-trip | ✅ | GLTF-294 | **Accept:** both loaders produce identical `AnimationClipEXT`. **Swept over the whole `anim-*` corpus**, not one fixture, because the ways two entirely separate pieces of code producing one type can diverge are per-shape: disjoint key times, STEP, CUBICSPLINE, two clips, an unnamed clip, a parent and a child, a skipped channel path. Clip count, name, duration, `TargetSpace`, track order, bone index and every key's T/R/S are compared. `TargetSpace` matters most: defaulting to `JointPalette` on the way back in would apply scene-node indices to `Model::Bones` and pose the wrong bones with no symptom but wrong motion. Track **order** is compared too, which is what forced `ExtractClips` to sort its tracks — `byBone` is an `unordered_map`, so the two paths agreed only until a rehash. |
| GLTF-315 | Animation import report | ✅ | GLTF-034 | Skipped channels, unsupported paths, resampling. **Accept:** populated; the existing stdout warning becomes a report entry. **`AnimationReportEXT`, filled by both extractors**, remains the computation-oriented form and now feeds public `GltfImportReportEXT` diagnostics. Every field is a place an animation arrives incompletely while still playing: channels skipped for an out-of-scene target, channels skipped for an undrivable path, tracks baked onto a union of disagreeing key times, adjacent input samples sharing a time, and animations that resolved to nothing at all. Resampling is counted as "more keys than the longest single source channel", which is the computable form of "the channels disagreed". Two silences were closed on the way: the skinned path gathered warnings and dropped them on the floor without logging any of them, and a channel targeting a non-joint node was skipped there with no count. A control fixture asserts every counter reads zero on a clean file, so a report that was never populated cannot pass the assertions that expect a zero. |
| GLTF-316 | Full animation L4 regression | ✅ | GLTF-302 | **Accept:** all `anim-*` fixtures green on both loaders. **Ten of them**, not the twelve this row guessed at when it was written; the count is what the surface actually needs rather than a number to pad to, and each one discriminates something no other does: a rigid node channel (`anim-rigid-node`), a non-zero first key (`anim-nonzero-start`), disjoint T/S key times (`anim-translation-scale`), STEP's half-open interval boundary (`anim-step`), CUBICSPLINE's tangent triplets evaluated at an interior time (`anim-cubicspline`), two clips one of them unnamed (`anim-two-clips`), a repeated input time (`anim-repeated-time`), a parent and child animated together (`anim-parent-child`), an undrivable `weights` path (`anim-weights-path`), and an out-of-scene target (`anim-out-of-scene-target`). Swept three ways: names and durations against each manifest, bone indices against the imported graph (with keys asserted strictly ascending and index 0 — the synthetic root, which no glTF channel can legitimately name — refused), and the offline/runtime agreement of `GLTF-314`. The sweep found two defects outside the animation layer that no existing fixture could reach: `Matrix::Decompose` losing every axis-aligned rotation to a negative-zero sign test, and the L6 normal-matrix oracle transposing its world one time too many — both invisible while every corpus world 3×3 was diagonal. |

---

### Phase 15 — Cameras, lights, scenes · owner: `GLTF-TRANSFORM` / `GLTF-EXTENSION`
*Entry: Phase 14. Exit: imported cameras and lights are available and correct, or explicitly absent.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-317 | **Import glTF cameras** | ✔ | GLTF-025, GLTF-114 | `cgltf_camera` has **0 occurrences**. **Accept:** camera nodes reach the model. **Landed:** `ExtractCamerasEXT` walks the **scene graph**, not `data->cameras` — a camera only counts if a node in the default scene instances it, and one camera instanced by several nodes is several placements, both of which walking the camera array would get wrong. Carried to a new CNAEXT `Model::CamerasEXT` holding `ModelCameraEXT`. **A property, not `Tag`** — the third time that collision has come up: `Tag` holds one object and `SkinningData` and `ModelAnimationsEXT` already contend for it, so a skinned model with cameras would have had to choose. Projection and placement stay **apart**, as §3.10 keeps them, because an application animating the camera node must recompute the view without touching the projection. |
| GLTF-318 | Perspective projection mapping | ✔ | GLTF-317 | `CreatePerspectiveFieldOfView(yfov, aspectRatio ?: viewportAspect, znear, zfar)`. **Accept:** `camera-perspective` matrix matches the manifest to 1e-6. **Landed**, matching to 1e-6 against a matrix the fixture computes from the specification's own formula rather than from CNA. An absent `aspectRatio` means "the viewport's" (§3.10.3), which the importer cannot know: it uses 1 and carries `yfov`/`znear` recoverably so an application with a real viewport can rebuild the projection without re-reading the file. |
| GLTF-319 | Infinite perspective (`zfar` absent) | ✔ | GLTF-318 | XNA has no such overload. **Accept:** a CNAEXT builder; `camera-perspective-infinite` matches. **Landed** as `CreateInfinitePerspectiveFieldOfViewEXT`, built from the finite matrix's **limits** as `zfar → ∞` (`M33 → −1`, `M43 → −znear`) rather than from a large finite `zfar` — which is not equivalent, because a large `zfar` perturbs *every* depth value, not only the ones near the horizon, turning an exact depth comparison into an approximate one. It throws `std::invalid_argument` on the same preconditions as `Matrix`'s finite overload and with the same exception type, since a caller switching between the two must not have to catch two different things. The two fixtures share every parameter but `zfar`, so a test isolates exactly the terms that may differ: `M11`/`M22` must be identical and the two `z` terms must not be. |
| GLTF-320 | Orthographic projection mapping | ✔ | GLTF-317 | `CreateOrthographic(2*xmag, 2*ymag, znear, zfar)`. **Accept:** `camera-orthographic` matches. **Landed.** `xmag`/`ymag` are **half** extents, so the volume is twice each — the classic silent factor of two, which gets its own test rather than only being implied by the matrix comparison. The fixture is deliberately non-square (3 × 1.5) so a swapped axis is caught too. |
| GLTF-321 | Camera view matrix from the node transform | ✔ | GLTF-317 | `view = inverse(worldTransform(cameraNode))`; the camera looks down its own −Z. **Accept:** manifest match. **Landed.** A glTF camera looks down its own −Z with +Y up, which is **XNA's own convention**, so no basis change is applied — and the manifest states that rule explicitly, so introducing one later fails a test rather than looking subtly wrong on screen. The node's world transform is carried on `ModelCameraEXT` and the view is its inverse, kept as a derivation rather than a stored second matrix that could drift from it. |
| GLTF-322 | `aspectRatio` absent ⇒ use the viewport | ✅ | GLTF-318 | **Accept:** documented and tested. **The assumption is now recorded, which is the whole task.** An absent `aspectRatio` means "the viewport's" (§3.10.3), which an importer has no viewport to satisfy, so 1 is assumed — and `ModelCameraEXT::HasAuthoredAspectRatio` says so. Without it a consumer cannot tell an author who framed a square shot from one who deliberately left the framing to the runtime, and would either stretch the first or letterbox the second; both read as a projection bug rather than as a missing field. `FieldOfView`/`NearPlaneDistance`/`FarPlaneDistance` are carried alongside so acting on the flag is possible at all: rebuilding at the real aspect otherwise means inverting a matrix to recover values the file stated outright, which is not even possible for the infinite variant without first knowing it *is* the infinite variant. `camera-perspective-no-aspect` differs from `camera-perspective` in exactly that one field, and a rebuild-from-the-carried-parameters test asserts the parameters and the matrix beside them describe the same camera. |
| GLTF-323 | The viewer keeps its own camera as default | ✅ | GLTF-317 | Otherwise a broken imported camera is indistinguishable from broken geometry. **Accept:** imported cameras are an explicit alternative view only. **Closed in viewer commit `28fa045`.** Loading `camera-perspective` with no camera option keeps the bounds-framed orbit view even though the model exposes `MainCam`; imported cameras are reachable only through the explicit direct-load `--camera <name|#global-index>` option. Name and `#0` captures are byte-identical, while the default differs at 25 217 of 262 144 pixels, so neither branch aliases the other. Missing, ambiguous and out-of-range selectors fail by name; offline use fails before SDL initialisation because `.cnj` deliberately carries no camera. The selected view uses the camera node's live `Model::Bones` transform every frame (so rigid animation moves it), inverts that world matrix for View, uses the authored projection, and rebuilds only a source-omitted perspective aspect ratio from the actual viewport. |
| GLTF-324 | Camera carrier on the model | ✅ | GLTF-025 | CNAEXT `GltfCameraEXT` list in owned resources. **Accept:** reviewed and tested. **Reviewed in `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.11**, moved there out of §2.3's deferral, which `GLTF-317` had overtaken — the entry now records the shape the gate approved rather than leaving it unspecified because it had been deferred. Landed as `std::vector<ModelCameraEXT>` on `Model`: one entry per camera-bearing **node** rather than per `cameras[]` entry (a camera only exists in the render if a node places it, and one camera may be placed several times), `Projection` built at import so no application reimplements the infinite-far-plane case, `WorldTransform` documented as an import-time snapshot because a camera node can be animated, and both of the importer's assumptions — infinite far plane, assumed aspect ratio — carried as flags rather than folded silently into the matrix. Additive and CNAEXT: no XNA 4.0 type changes, and a `Model` from any other source has an empty list, which is also what a glTF file with no camera produces so "no camera" is never confused with "a default camera". |
| GLTF-325 | `KHR_lights_punctual` current behaviour locked | ✔ | GLTF-101 | ≤3 lights, directional-only, point/spot aimed at the origin, `color*intensity` clamped to `[0,1]`. Two tests already exist. **Accept:** locked as the documented approximation. **Locked with `GLTF-328`,** including the approximation's own *rule*: a point light becomes a directional one aimed from its world position at the scene origin. An approximation with an unstated rule fails as a light pointing somewhere arbitrary while still lighting the scene. |
| GLTF-326 | Report dropped and approximated lights | ✔ | GLTF-035 | Extras beyond 3 are silently dropped. **Accept:** report entries. **Landed as `LightReportEXT`, covering four losses rather than the one the row names.** Beyond the >3 drop: point lights approximated as directional, spot lights approximated the same way *and* losing their cone, and — the one nobody had written down — `color * intensity` clamped to `[0,1]`. glTF intensity is **photometric and unbounded** (lux for a directional light, candela for the others) while `DiffuseColor` is a colour, so an authored 683 imports as plain white. Point and spot are counted apart because the approximation is materially worse for one than the other. The one behavioural change: the extraction loop no longer *stops* at three, because it has to keep walking to count what it drops — “3 of 6 imported” is actionable and “3 imported” is not. A control test asserts a file already inside XNA's lighting model reports nothing at all, so the diagnostic cannot degrade into noise every import prints. |
| GLTF-327 | Light `range` and cone angles | ✅ | GLTF-325 | Ignored. **Accept:** documented as GLTF ROBUST. **Ignored, and now counted where they are lost** (`LightReportEXT::ignoredRangeCount`/`ignoredConeAngleCount`), reported apart from the kind approximation because the two are different losses: that one is about what a light **is**, this one about how far it **reaches**. A directional light has no falloff and no cone, so a lamp the author scoped to one room lights the whole scene — and the error grows with distance from the light, which is to say it is smallest exactly where an author placing it is looking. `lights-kinds-and-reach` carries one light of each kind with two ranges and one cone, so the counts cannot all be wired to the same source, and its directional light is the control that proves the counters increment on an authored bound rather than on light presence. |
| GLTF-328 | Light node transform | ✔ | GLTF-325 | `cgltf_node_transform_world` is already used correctly here. **Accept:** locked. **Locked with the light under a rotated parent**, because a light parented to the scene root — how one is usually authored — cannot tell the world transform from the local one. Here the parent's quarter turn maps the light's local −Z onto −Y, so reading the local transform gives a light pointing forward where it must point straight down. |
| GLTF-329 | Light ordering determinism | ✅ | GLTF-325 | Node-array order restricted to the default scene. **Accept:** deterministic and documented. **Locked by `lights-over-budget`,** which declares five lights where XNA binds three and authors the extension's own `lights` array in **exactly reverse** of the node order. That separation is the point: the node array orders light *placements*, the extension array orders light *definitions*, and they are not the same thing — one definition may be instanced by several nodes, and a definition no node references is not in the scene at all. A reader using the wrong one keeps a visibly different three, which the fixture's per-light colours make immediate. Determinism is asserted by extracting twice from one parse rather than trusting a single run, since a reader walking an unordered container would very likely agree with itself once and differ on a rehash. |
| GLTF-330 | Intensity clamping documented | ✅ | GLTF-325 | glTF's photometric units have no XNA mapping. **Accept:** approximation table in `docs/`. **`docs/gltf-conventions.md` § "The approximation table"** — one row per property glTF carries and `DirectionalLight` does not, each naming what CNA does with it and why the loss matters. The intensity row says the thing the row title only implies: there is **no scale factor** that reconciles the two, because lux and candela are physical measurements and `DiffuseColor` is a shader input, and converting between them is an *exposure* decision belonging to a tone-mapping stage CNA does not have. So an ordinary authored value clamps — `683` is the lumens-per-watt constant, not an absurd number, and it imports as plain white. `lights-over-budget` authors exactly that, and the report's worst-pre-clamp channel is asserted because it is what tells an author how far out of gamut they were rather than merely that something was clipped. |
| GLTF-331 | Real point/spot lights | ✅ | GLTF-327 | No CNA stock effect supports them. **Accept:** scoped as GLTF ROBUST with a design sketch. **Scoped, not implemented**, with the four-step sketch in `docs/gltf-conventions.md` § "If real point and spot lights were wanted": a `PointLightEXT`/`SpotLightEXT` uniform block beside the three directional slots, the extension's own falloff and cone terms in the shader (transcription, not design — both are stated in its specification), an `EffectLightCollectionEXT`-style binding API because XNA's `IEffectLights` names exactly three directional lights and cannot express any of this, and every renderer's uniform layout updated in step. That last item is what makes it large and why it stays scoped: the light block is shared shader ABI, not a per-renderer detail. The sketch closes with the thing that must **not** happen meanwhile — an importer quietly picking a good-enough directional substitute without saying so, which is exactly what `GLTF-326`/`GLTF-327` exist to prevent. |
| GLTF-332 | Scene fixtures regression | ✅ | GLTF-148 | **Accept:** all `scene-*`/`camera-*`/`lights-*` fixtures green. **Eight of them** — one `scene-*`, five `camera-*` (`camera-perspective-no-aspect` is new for `GLTF-322`) and two `lights-*`, which did not exist at all before this row: every light rule was previously asserted only on scratch documents, which can vary one thing at a time but cannot pin *which* lights survive a budget or that the surviving order is one the file defines. `GltfLightBudget.EveryLightFixtureMatchesItsManifestOnEveryReportedLoss` sweeps all six `LightReportEXT` counters per fixture, so a change that fixed one loss while silently introducing another fails here rather than in whichever single test happened to cover the one that changed, and re-asserts XNA's hard ceiling of three plus normalised directions per fixture. Adding them required `KHR_lights_punctual` support in the fixture generator itself — a light attaches through a node's own `extensions` block rather than through a top-level property, and the extension is declared automatically by `add_light` so a fixture cannot forget to announce it. |

---

### Phase 16 — Extensions · owner: `GLTF-EXTENSION`
*Entry: **GLTF CORE 2.0 CORRECT**. Exit: every §19 row is implemented or explicitly and loudly rejected.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-333 | Enforce `extensionsRequired` (implementation) | ✔ | GLTF-023 | **Accept:** an unsupported required extension errors with its name. **Landed with `GLTF-023`:** both load paths refuse a file whose `extensionsRequired` names an extension `IsGltfExtensionSupportedEXT` does not implement, and the message names the extension. `gltf-required-extension-unsupported` is the witness. `GLTF-024`'s separate rule holds alongside it: an extension that is merely *used* is ignored with a warning, never an error — the severity difference is the point, and both are asserted by `GltfContainerValidation`. |
| GLTF-334 | Central extension-support registry | ✅ | GLTF-333 | One table mapping extension name → classification → handler. **Accept:** §19's table is generated from it, not maintained by hand. **`GltfExtensionRegistryEXT()`,** which `IsGltfExtensionSupportedEXT` is now a lookup into rather than a second hand-written list. The registry carries **two independent columns**, which is the design decision this row was really about: `support` (how completely CNA implements it) and `claimed` (whether a file listing it in `extensionsRequired` is accepted). They answer different questions and had never been separated — §19 called `KHR_texture_transform` PARTIAL while the gate claimed it, and nothing said why. `KHR_materials_transmission` is approximated and **not** claimed (a file requiring transmission wants refraction CNA cannot deliver) while `KHR_lights_punctual` is approximated and **is** claimed (refusing every lit file would be far worse than the approximation); collapsing the two columns would force one of those to be wrong. §19 is regenerated from the registry, and `Section19AgreesWithTheRegistryOnEveryRow` parses the plan and compares name, classification and owning task row by row, printing the corrected table on failure so fixing a drift is mechanical. |
| GLTF-335 | Extension conformance test template | ✅ | GLTF-334 | **Accept:** adding an extension requires a fixture and a classification. **Enforced in both directions rather than asked for.** An extension any corpus fixture declares must be in the registry — otherwise the fixture asserts the behaviour of an accident, silently ignored or loudly refused depending on a list its author never saw. And an extension CNA **claims** must have a corpus fixture declaring it, because a claim is a promise to accept files that cannot work without the extension and a promise nothing tests is not one. The rule found a real gap on its first run: `KHR_texture_transform` was claimed and heavily tested, but only ever on scratch documents, so the committed corpus had no witness. `tex-texture-transform` is that witness; its non-square scale, quarter turn and asymmetric offset ensure no alternative ordering agrees. After `GLTF-184`, authored UVs stay byte-exact at L5 and the float transform is locked where it belongs: exact affine rows at L6 and a discriminating sample at L7. |
| GLTF-336 | `KHR_texture_transform` completion | ✅ | GLTF-184 | **Complete: classification is `IMPLEMENTED_AND_TESTED`.** All five PBR maps retain independent offset/rotation/scale and `texCoord` override state through direct glTF and offline `.cnj`. Both PBR effects produce the same affine convention, every PBR renderer consumes it, the repository-wide renderer audit locks CPU upload and shader application, and `EasyGL_Gltf_TextureTransformPerMap` proves different base/normal transforms sharing one UV stream on OPENGLES2/3. The former named diagnostic and limitations row are retired. |
| GLTF-337 | `KHR_materials_unlit` | ✅ | GLTF-215 | Maps cleanly to `BasicEffect` with lighting off. **Accept:** fixture correct at L7. **Now proven through the real OPENGLES3 viewer as well as L3/L4/L6.** `baseColorFactor` becomes diffuse, the punctual/default rig is skipped, and parked light directions stay finite. The pinned Khronos comparison exposed the remaining renderer defect: EasyGL reused its lit stride-32 shader and still evaluated specular for `LightingEnabled=false`; an origin vertex made the unused eye vector `normalize(0)`, NaN multiplied by zero stayed NaN, and the whole triangle became black (reference RGB MAE 189.33). The shader now branches before all lighting/specular math, the viewer returns `(51,153,229,255)`, and the origin-vertex GPU regression is 5/5 green. `unlitEXT` remains distinct from merely non-PBR, and direct/offline loaders agree. One named limit remains: real XNA `SkinnedEffect` has no lighting-disable flag, so skinned unlit uses zero directional light plus white ambient and is classified with that explicit approximation. **Closing commit:** `a88b14220`. |
| GLTF-338 | `KHR_materials_unlit` + vertex colour / alpha | ✅ | GLTF-337 | **Accept:** tested. **`mat-unlit-vertex-color-alpha`** — the two things an unlit material is most often combined with, and the two most easily lost when "lighting off" is written as a special case rather than as a flag. `COLOR_0` multiplies the diffuse colour in the shader exactly as it does for a lit `BasicEffect`, so unlit needs no separate path at all — but an implementation that made unlit its own effect type would have to reimplement that, and would drop the alpha channel first. Alpha is the sharper half: the extension does **not** exempt a surface from `alphaMode`, an unlit material with a translucent base colour is the ordinary way to author a decal or a UI plane, and it is exactly where "unlit" is most likely to be read as "no material state at all". The fixture's final alpha is `baseColorFactor.a` **times** the vertex alpha, so a reader dropping either still produces a translucent surface — at the wrong translucency. It authors no `NORMAL` on purpose: §3.7.2.1 makes it optional, an unlit surface has no lighting term to use one for, and the stride-24 layout a coloured primitive lands on has no normal slot anyway, so authoring one would add an irrelevant loss to a fixture about exactly two things. |
| GLTF-339 | `KHR_materials_transmission` | ✔ | GLTF-230 | Ignored; causes the `ChronographWatch` opaque-glass defect. **Accept:** either a real transmission pass, or a **documented** alpha-blend approximation that is explicitly not physical — never silent. **Decided: the alpha-blend approximation.** A real pass samples the framebuffer behind the surface and blurs it by roughness, which needs a second pass and a scene-colour target no CNA stock effect has. So `alpha = 1 - transmissionFactor`, **multiplied into** the material's own alpha rather than replacing it (a surface that is both partly transparent and partly transmissive should end up more transparent than either alone), with `alphaMode` forced to `Blend`. The four ways it is not physical are named in the source, the header and every report: no refraction, no roughness blur, tinted glass **darkens** rather than tints what is behind it, and specular — which a transmissive surface keeps at full strength — fades out with the alpha. A transmission **texture** is flagged separately, because flattening a varying surface to one factor is materially worse than the uniform case. A factor of 0 leaves the material completely alone, so a neutrally-declared extension is not turned into a `BLEND` regression. And the extension is **still not claimed** in `IsGltfExtensionSupportedEXT`: an approximation is not an implementation, so a file that *requires* transmission is still refused by `GLTF-023` rather than loading with its glass drawn as tinted alpha. |
| GLTF-340 | Transmission render ordering | ✅ | GLTF-339 | **Accept:** the dial is visible through the glass in the retake. **Complete with a licence-clean generated analogue and an exact depth/blend counterexample.** `mat-material-variants` now makes its default opaque red PBR state the dial and its blue variant a real `KHR_materials_transmission=0.5` surface; the importer turns that authored variant into `AlphaModeEXT::Blend` with straight alpha 0.5 on direct and offline paths. Registered `EasyGL_Gltf_TransmissionOrdering` draws the same triangle twice at distinct depths. Glass-first writes the nearer depth, rejects the later dial and measures `(7,26,115)` over black; opaque-dial-first followed by application-selected `NonPremultiplied` glass measures `(83,38,127)`, where both source colours are present. The two orders pass on OPENGLES2 and OPENGLES3, so neither a no-depth test nor an opaque-glass false positive can satisfy the acceptance. This remains the explicitly non-physical `GLTF-339` approximation; the application owns transparent sorting and blend state exactly as `GLTF-230` decided. |
| GLTF-341 | `KHR_materials_variants` import | ✔ | GLTF-236 | 4 variants in `ChronographWatch`. **Accept:** variants imported; default mapping unchanged. **Landed on both loaders with a licence-clean synthetic witness.** `ExtractMaterialVariantsEXT` decodes each sparse primitive mapping through the same full `ExtractMesh` path as the core material, so an alternative may change effect class, vertex layout, maps, samplers and morph upload rather than only tinting an existing effect. The source-order root table is preserved and the freshly loaded model remains on the core/default material. `mat-material-variants` discriminates red default PBR (stride 48), blue PBR, green unlit `BasicEffect` (stride 32), and a third variant with no mapping; selecting that sparse case after green must restore red, which catches stale-state implementations. The fixture requires the extension, so the now-claimed registry entry is exercised through `extensionsRequired`; the old negative fixture moved to genuinely unsupported clearcoat. Direct STUB/HEADLESS tests and `.gltf` → `.cnj` → `Model` parity are green. |
| GLTF-342 | Variant selection API | ✔ | GLTF-341, GLTF-025 | **Accept:** CNAEXT selection; the viewer can switch. **`Model` now exposes the source-order name list, the active index, and an indexed CNAEXT setter; `-1` means the core/default mapping.** Index identity is deliberate: glTF names are presentation strings and need not be unique, whereas mapping identity is the root-array index. Invalid indices throw before changing state, sparse mappings reset only that primitive to its default, and `Model` copies share selection because their XNA mesh parts are already shared. Switching sets the complete captured part state and updates `ModelMesh::Effects` through the ordinary `setEffectProperty`, so draw-time World/View/Projection binding never sees stale alternative effects. `docs/gltf-api-change-review.md` §1.13 records the additive surface. The API is sufficient for the separate viewer to enumerate and switch variants without knowing importer internals. |
| GLTF-343 | `KHR_materials_ior` | ✅ | GLTF-235 | Affects `F0`. **Accept:** shader change + analytic check. **Complete across the importer, both PBR effects and all 15 PBR renderers.** `ior` survives cgltf → `MaterialOut` → direct/offline loaders → both clone-safe PBR effects → `GpuDrawParams::pbrDielectricF0/F90`. Every backend uploads those endpoints on its rigid and skinned PBR routes, mixes dielectric F0 with albedo by metallic, and evaluates Schlick with the transported F90. `EasyGL_Pbr_FresnelFactors` supplies the analytic reference: normal-incidence black/rough dielectric reduces to `F0/(4π)`, producing core `(11,11,11)` and authored IOR/specular `(2,9,43)` on both programs; the grazing pair holds F0 at `.04`, changes only F90 from 1 to .3, and measures `(33,33,33)` versus `(15,15,15)`. The same six-check shared test passes wherever the backend is runnable; native shader compilation/cross-builds cover platform-only paths. `EveryPbrShaderHonorsTransportedFresnelEndpoints` independently audits all 15 CPU upload and shader chains, including separately stored rigid/skinned programs. The registry now classifies and claims the extension as `IMPLEMENTED_AND_TESTED`. |
| GLTF-344 | `KHR_materials_specular` | ✅/⬜ | GLTF-343 | **Accept:** fixture correct. **Factor-only rendering remains complete across all 15 PBR renderers; texture transport is complete and EasyGL, OpenGL2, OpenGL4, DirectX9/11/12, Bgfx, Diligent, Magnum, SDL GPU and Vulkan now sample both texture inputs.** Both PBR effects carry CNAEXT factor/colour properties plus owned `SpecularMapEXT` and `SpecularColorMapEXT` inputs with independent UV selectors, affine transforms and colour-space state. Direct glTF and offline `.cnj` preserve both images, samplers and map metadata, material variants restore their sampler slots, and the draw contract retains the pre-clamp F0 needed because `specularColorTexture` multiplies before `min(...,1)`. The three completed GL bindings use white identity fallbacks at units 5/6, read strength from alpha and colour from sRGB-decoded RGB, apply independent transforms, and compute `min(unclampedF0 * colorSample, 1) * factor * strengthSample`; their shader and CPU binding chains are source-audited, and both desktop renderers compile natively. EasyGL additionally applies each map's UV selector and the official Khronos `SpecularTest.glb` compiles and renders through both direct and offline viewer paths on Xvfb, with only 7 edge pixels differing between those captures. The modern DirectX pair binds identity-white SRVs at t5/t6, samples alpha/RGB with independent transforms and colour decode, and uses the same seven-bit UV selector contract; D3D12 gained the previously missing stride-60/76 dual-UV route. All four rigid/skinned single/dual-UV HLSL variants pass D3DCompile, regenerated DXBC is checked in, both renderer libraries cross-compile with MinGW, and the focused source audit covers both CPU-to-shader chains. DirectX9 likewise binds white s5/s6 fallbacks and consumes both extension transforms/colour decode; its two native ps_3_0 variants compile to 5,588 bytes, disassemble to exact c24–c29/s5–s6 bindings and use 278 of 512 instruction slots, while the renderer cross-compiles and a checked-in generator makes the bytecode reproducible. Magnum uses white units 5/6, independent extension transforms, sampled render-target row-order flags and sRGB colour decode in its shared rigid/skinned GLSL; the renderer builds, the shader compiles on llvmpipe OpenGL 4.5 under Xvfb, and its existing six-check PBR plus six-check analytic Fresnel witnesses pass. SDL GPU binds the two white fallbacks plus independently imported sampler states at bindings 5/6, applies separate transforms and sRGB colour decode in the same rigid/skinned GLSL, and ships the regenerated 12,656-byte fragment SPIR-V. Its renderer builds and its rigid, skinned and seven-check Fresnel/factor pixel witnesses pass on a software GPU under Xvfb. Vulkan adds seven independent image/sampler pairs to both PBR descriptor layouts, a 124-float block with four extension transform rows and a seven-bit dual-UV selector, and regenerated rigid/skinned single/dual-UV SPIR-V. On lavapipe under Xvfb with Khronos validation enabled, both 8-check golden programs, the seven-check Fresnel program and the expanded 22-check texture program pass; the last one proves strength alpha and colour RGB at slots 5/6 for both effects. Diligent binds seven named dynamic resources with independently selected sampler slots, uploads a 76-float block containing pre-clamp F0, scalar strength, colour decode, four extension-transform rows and the seven-bit selector, and selects rigid/skinned stride-48/60/68/76 pipelines. Its shared HLSL passes 21/21 material-map, 22/22 texture-slot, 7/7 analytic Fresnel and 12/12 sRGB pixels through both Vulkan and OpenGL under Xvfb; Vulkan additionally runs with Khronos validation enabled. Bgfx binds identity-white stages 5/6, uploads independent extension transforms plus the seven-bit UV selector and uses rigid/skinned stride-48/60/68/76 layouts. All OpenGL, ESSL, SPIR-V and WGSL variants compile; its OpenGL llvmpipe path passes 21/21 material-map, 22/22 texture-slot, 7/7 analytic Fresnel, 12/12 sRGB and both 7/7 legacy PBR pixel suites under Xvfb. Remaining: carry the same two bindings through the other 4 PBR renderers, add the repository-wide texture audit and cross-renderer/reference image evidence, then claim the extension. Until then the registry stays `IMPLEMENTED_WITH_A_NAMED_LIMIT`, required use is refused, and optional use reports the exact partial boundary. |
| GLTF-345 | `KHR_materials_clearcoat` | ✔ | GLTF-343 | Second specular lobe; large. **Accept:** implemented or explicitly deferred with a report entry. **Deferred, and the deferral now has the report entry the acceptance requires** — which was the missing half: the registry already classified it `PARSED_BUT_IGNORED` with a reason, but nothing proved a file using it *said so*. `mat-unimplemented-extensions` declares clearcoat, sheen and volume at once with **non-default parameters** (a reader that "supported" them by parsing and applying nothing would be indistinguishable from one that ignored them if every value were the extension's own default), and `EveryDeferredMaterialExtensionIsReportedByNameRatherThanIgnoredSilently` asserts three things per extension: still classified `PARSED_BUT_IGNORED`, still **not claimed** (so a file *requiring* it is refused rather than loaded with the extension ignored), and a warning naming it. The file imports normally otherwise, which is the point of `extensionsUsed` being advisory. |
| GLTF-346 | `KHR_materials_sheen` | ✔ | GLTF-343 | **Accept:** as above. **Same fixture, same three assertions** — `sheenColorFactor` `[0.9,0.7,0.4]` and `sheenRoughnessFactor` 0.3 authored away from every default, classified `PARSED_BUT_IGNORED`, unclaimed, and named in a warning. |
| GLTF-347 | `KHR_materials_volume` | ✔ | GLTF-339 | Depends on transmission. **Accept:** as above. **Same fixture, same three assertions**, and the dependency is why the deferral is the *right* answer rather than a delay: volume is meaningless without a real transmission pass, and `GLTF-339` deliberately approximates transmission as alpha rather than implementing refraction. Implementing volume on top of an approximation would produce a number nobody could interpret. |
| GLTF-348 | Classify iridescence / anisotropy / dispersion as NOT_DESIRED | ✅ | GLTF-334 | **Accept:** recorded with rationale; required-use is rejected loudly. **All three in the registry with a per-extension reason**, which is the entire difference between a decision and an omission — a future reader finds why rather than spending a day implementing something the project does not want. Iridescence is a thin-film term whose shader cost falls on **every** PBR material to serve a rare one; anisotropy needs a tangent-aligned specular lobe and therefore a reliable tangent basis on every affected primitive, which `GLTF-086` shows CNA cannot carry at most strides; dispersion presupposes the refraction pass `GLTF-339` explicitly chose to approximate instead of implementing. None is claimed, so `GLTF-333` still refuses a file that *requires* one **by name** — asserted, because "not desired" is not "harmless to pretend". |
| GLTF-349 | `KHR_materials_pbrSpecularGlossiness` | ✅ | GLTF-334 | Archived by Khronos but present in older assets. **Accept:** converted to metallic-roughness, or rejected clearly. **Converted**, because rejecting would refuse a decade of assets that are otherwise perfectly importable. The standard mapping: `diffuseFactor` → base colour, `diffuseTexture` → the base-colour map (the only texture that survives, and reading only `pbrMetallicRoughness` would have left such an asset untextured on top of losing its tint), `metallic` → 0, `roughness` → `1 − glossiness`. `specularFactor` is **dropped**, and that is the whole approximation: specular-glossiness expresses a **coloured** specular reflection, which metallic-roughness can only approach by making the surface metal — which also tints the diffuse, giving a *different* material rather than a closer one. A dielectric converts almost exactly; a brass one goes grey. Both the fact and the magnitude are reported, at a severity that tracks the magnitude. Converting is **not** claiming: the extension stays unclaimed, so a file listing it in `extensionsRequired` is still refused, because it is asking for the one term the conversion discards. The change also moved such a material onto the PBR path — it was previously non-PBR, which silently dropped every factor it carried — and the L5 stride selector held a second, now-stale copy of that rule, caught by the byte comparison. `mat-specular-glossiness` authors glossiness 0.8 so the converted roughness is 0.2: neither the glTF default (1.0) nor the un-inverted 0.8, so "forgot to invert" and "forgot entirely" are both separable from correct. |
| GLTF-350 | `KHR_texture_basisu` / `EXT_texture_webp` | ✔ | GLTF-200 | Images silently vanish today. **Accept:** supported or explicitly rejected. **Decided: explicitly reported, and — where the file demands them — rejected.** Both extensions are specified so a texture MAY keep a plain PNG/JPEG `source` as a fallback for readers without the codec, so there are three outcomes and `GLTF-200` implements all three: the fallback is used when present and nothing is reported, the loss is named per map when it is not, and a file listing either in `extensionsRequired` is refused outright by `GLTF-023`. Neither is listed in `IsGltfExtensionSupportedEXT`, and a test guards that — claiming support would turn that refusal into a silent load with the textures missing, which is worse than today, not better. Adding a KTX2 or WebP decoder is a codec dependency, not a glTF conformance question. |
| GLTF-351 | `EXT_meshopt_compression` | ✅ | GLTF-334 | cgltf parses it but decoding needs a caller-supplied hook CNA does not provide ⇒ buffer data is absent. **Accept:** supported or explicitly rejected; **never** silently empty geometry. **Explicitly rejected, at validation.** The trap is that cgltf makes the extension look supported: it parses the compression block and validates its metadata thoroughly — stride, count, mode, filter, and that the compressed range fits its buffer — so both `cgltf_parse` and `cgltf_validate` succeed. It never decodes, and without a decoder `cgltf_buffer_view_data` falls through to `buffer->data + view->offset`, so every accessor over a compressed view reads whatever bytes happen to be there: not an error, not empty, undefined geometry that renders mangled or invisible with nothing to say why — precisely the outcome this row forbids. Refused even when the extension is only *used*, which is the one place `GLTF-024`'s severity rule does not apply: an ignorable extension is one whose absence leaves the file readable, and this one relocates the geometry. The control asserts the check does not mistake an ordinary bufferView for a compressed one, swept over the whole corpus rather than one document, because the check walks every bufferView in every file and a predicate reading the wrong field would refuse everything. |
| GLTF-352 | `EXT_mesh_gpu_instancing` | ✔ | GLTF-146 | CNA already has `DrawInstancedPrimitives`. **Accept:** design sketch; GLTF ROBUST. **Sketched in `docs/gltf-limitations.md` §4.1**, in four steps: the extraction emits one placement carrying N instance transforms (the report already counts such nodes, which is what makes today's loss visible), the transforms become a per-instance vertex buffer built **once at import** in the layout `DrawInstancedPrimitives` already streams, `ModelMeshPart` grows a CNAEXT instance-buffer property and `ModelMesh::Draw` branches on it — that being the API-change-review row and the real cost, a new public member plus a branch every XNA application shares — and the fallback for a renderer without instancing must draw N times or **refuse by name**, never draw once and look nearly right, which is exactly today's failure. **Why it stays unbuilt** is stated too, and it is not effort: every layer that could verify it is L7, because L3–L5 are identical whether the instance transforms arrive or not — they describe one mesh either way. Building an instancing path this environment cannot render is how `GLTF-157`'s lesson repeats. |

### Phase 17 — Draco and compression · owner: `GLTF-EXTENSION`
*Entry: Phase 16. Exit: Draco output is semantically identical to its uncompressed twin at L3.*

| ID | Title | St | Deps | Scope, evidence → acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GLTF-353 | **Draco parity fixture pair** | ✅ | GLTF-101 | The same mesh, uncompressed and Draco-compressed. **Accept:** POSITION, NORMAL, TANGENT, TEXCOORD, COLOR, JOINTS, WEIGHTS and the index list all match within a stated quantisation tolerance, **at L3**. **Complete with four generated assets and a stronger exact result.** `draco-vs-uncompressed-pair` contains PBR and vertex-colour compressed/ordinary pairs; `draco-skinned` adds JOINTS/WEIGHTS; `draco-morph` adds all three delta semantics; `draco-triangle` isolates decode/connectivity and participates in the Khronos L7 subset. Every named stream and face list is equal byte-for-byte because the pinned sequential encoder preserves these authored values. `GltfDracoEncoderPin` independently regenerates five compressed streams with vendored 1.5.7 and requires byte identity, so committed payload provenance is executable rather than a comment. **Closing commit:** `d4b0fe44d`. |
| GLTF-354 | Lock the shared-code-path property | ✅ | GLTF-353 | `unpackSemantic` makes every call site source-agnostic — Draco has **no** separate transform or material path. **Accept:** a test asserts the two paths produce identical `MeshOut` apart from quantisation. **Locked across every downstream shape:** rigid PBR, vertex-colour, skinning and morph pairs compare complete semantic dumps and final vertex/index bytes. The decoder supplies only per-semantic arrays plus face triples; layout selection, tangent generation, materials, skinning, morphing, transforms and packing stay in the ordinary path. |
| GLTF-355 | Draco point-count validation | ✅ | GLTF-353 | Already validated against the declared `POSITION.count`. **Accept:** locked. `DecodedPointCountMustMatchTheDeclaredPositionAccessor` parses a valid three-point stream, mutates only the accessor metadata to two, and requires the named mismatch error before any prefix can be packed into a plausible `MeshOut`. |
| GLTF-356 | Draco connectivity from the decoded face list | ✅ | GLTF-353 | Not from `prim.indices`, which has no backing data. **Accept:** locked. All four compressed fixtures have metadata-only index accessors, while their L3/L5 output connectivity exactly equals the ordinary twin. The earlier strip/list normalization control additionally prevents already-decoded face triples from being converted as a second strip. |
| GLTF-357 | Dequantisation semantics | ✅ | GLTF-353 | `SetSkipAttributeTransform` is never called, so values read back already dequantised — the same semantic level `cgltf_accessor_unpack_floats` gives. **Locked and documented against the pinned decoder:** `UnpackDracoAttribute` reads every mapped point through `ConvertValue<float>`, and the complete float/normalized-integer corpus streams compare with their ordinary decoded values before CNA packing. |
| GLTF-358 | `!CNA_DRACO_AVAILABLE` error path | ✔ | GLTF-353 | Already tested. **Accept:** locked. **Locked, and the assertion follows the build rather than skipping** — `#ifdef CNA_DRACO_AVAILABLE` picks the expectation, so the test is meaningful in both configurations. The point of the row is that **no Draco-specific error handling is needed**: the file is refused up front by the ordinary `extensionsRequired` gate, naming the extension, rather than failing later inside `ExtractMesh` with a message about an accessor that has no backing data. |
| GLTF-359 | Pin `FindDracoUniqueId`'s pointer arithmetic | ✔ | GLTF-353 | Correct but fragile against a cgltf upgrade. **Accept:** a direct unit test over a known attribute mapping. **Pinned independently of decoder availability.** `GltfImportCoreTest.DracoUniqueIdsFollowCgltfsFixedUpAttributePointers` sends a real JSON object through `cgltf_parse` with deliberately sparse, reordered `POSITION=7`, `NORMAL=2` and `TEXCOORD_1=5` Draco IDs, then requires exact semantic/set lookup plus `-1` for the absent `TEXCOORD_0`, colour and null-data controls. The adapter is now the named internal `FindDracoUniqueIdEXT`, compiled whether or not `libdraco` is present, rather than logic hidden behind `CNA_DRACO_AVAILABLE` and therefore untestable in the ordinary build. It validates that cgltf's fixed-up pointer lies on a complete element inside the current accessor array before recovering the index; a cgltf representation change therefore fails closed instead of invoking undefined pointer subtraction or selecting a plausible wrong attribute. The focused test and all 17 `GltfImportCoreTest` cases pass on the Draco-less SOFTWARE build. |
| GLTF-360 | Draco + skinning | ✅ | GLTF-271 | **Accept:** `draco-skinned` matches its twin. **Green at L3–L5:** exact base PBR streams, two-joint JOINTS/WEIGHTS, connectivity, bind-pose world geometry and stride-68 packed bytes agree with the ordinary primitive. **Closing commit:** `d4b0fe44d`. |
| GLTF-361 | Draco + morph | ✅ | GLTF-288 | **Accept:** `draco-morph` matches its twin. **Green at L3–L5:** exact base streams/connectivity and POSITION/NORMAL/TANGENT target deltas agree, followed by the same default morph result and packed buffers. **Closing commit:** `d4b0fe44d`. |

The permitted strip needs one extra guard: Draco exposes decoded connectivity as explicit faces, already a triangle list, so `DecodedDracoStripFacesAreNotConvertedAsASecondStrip` proves its two face triples survive unchanged while an ordinary four-index glTF strip still converts normally. This removes a latent double-conversion that would have invented four overlapping triangles from two decoded faces. The full **549-test** sanitizer selection is clean with the vendored decoder (**520 passed, 29 capability/opt-in skips, 105.46 s**), and its ten-rung CTest projection is 10/10 clean in 163.75 s. Leak detection alone cannot run under this environment's ptrace, so the functional LSan claim is left to the identical CI matrix where ptrace is absent. **Closing commit:** `d4b0fe44d`. |
